, and hence, does not satisfy the Osgood condition.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
, and
(B3)
hold. Then for any
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
, there exists a unique Carathéodory solution satisfying (
2
).

Proof.

As noted in the example of the rectified flow from standard uniform to itself, one cannot expect the velocity field
(t,z)\mapsto v(t,z)
to be jointly continuous on
[0,1]\times\Omega
. This prompts us to consider Carathéodory solutions to (
2
), i.e., we consider solutions satisfying

z(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v(s,z(s))ds,\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\in[0,1].

(42)

Because we have not defined
v(t,z)
for
z\in\partial\Omega
, this integral equation is not well-defined if
z(s)\in\partial\Omega
for any
s\in[0,1)
. We proceed with the following steps to prove the existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
42
). Because
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
,
\mbox{dist}(x,\,\partial\Omega)>0
.

1.

Lemma
8
proves that for any
t\in[0,1]
, the function
z\mapsto v(t,z)
is uniformly bounded on
\Omega
and uniformly continuous on every compact subset of
\Omega^{\circ}
. This verifies the assumptions for Theorem
3
(1), and implies the existence of
\bar{t}\in(0,1]
and a Carathéodory solution
z^{*}(\cdot)
of (
42
) on
[0,\bar{t})
that lies in
\Omega^{\circ}
.

2.

Note that every solution to (
42
) starting at
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
has to either stay in
\Omega^{\circ}
for
t\in[0,1)
or reach the boundary at some
\tau<1
and terminate (because we have not defined
z\mapsto v(t,z)
on
\partial\Omega
).
Lemma
9
proves that every solution
z(\cdot)
of (
42
) must satisfy
\mbox{dist}(z(t),\partial\Omega)\geq(1-t)\mbox{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, which implies that no solution of (
42
) starting in the interior can reach the boundary, except maybe at
t=1
.

3.

Using the modulus of continuity bound on the velocity field from Lemma
8
, Lemma
37
proves that for any
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
, there exists a unique solution
z^{*}(\cdot)
satisfying (
42
) and
z^{*}(t)\in\Omega^{\circ}
for all
t\in[0,1)
.

4.

Hence, for any
x\notin\partial\Omega
, there exists a unique solution. This implies that the rectified flow provides a valid transport map (Theorem
1
).

∎

The following lemma provides some basic boundedness and uniform continuity properties of the velocity field (
1
) under assumptions
(B1)
and
(B2)
.

Lemma 8
.

Under assumption
(B1)
,

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|v(t,z)\|\leq\mathrm{diam}(\Omega),\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\Omega^{\circ}.

(43)

Under assumptions
(B1)
and
(B2)
, for all
t\in[0,1],\varepsilon,\eta>0
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},\\
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,{\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|}~\leq~\mathfrak{L}_{1}\omega(\eta)+\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon)\eta,\end{split}

(44)

where

\begin{split}\mathfrak{L}_{1}&=9\mathrm{diam}(\Omega),\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{3\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\omega^{-1}(1)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}3d5^{d+1}\mathrm{diam}^{2}(\Omega).\end{split}

(45)

A proof of Lemma
8
can be found in Section
S.6.1
.
Note that if
\omega(\eta)=C\eta
for some constant
C
, then inequality (
44
) implies that
z\mapsto v(t,z)
is Lipschitz on
\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
for each
t\in[0,1]
.
Better bounds on the modulus of continuity of
z\mapsto v(t,z)
(depending on
t
) are available in the proof of Lemma
8
. In particular, from the proof of Lemma
8
, it follows that
\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon)
can be replaced with a time-dependent function

\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon;t)=\frac{3\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\omega^{-1}(1)}+\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\frac{3\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if }\min\{t,1-t\}\leq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega),\\
5^{d+1}/\min\{t,1-t\},&\mbox{if }\min\{t,1-t\}\geq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega).\end{cases}

(46)

See inequalities (
E.72
), (
E.73
), (
E.74
), and (
E.76
). It is easy to see that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon;t)=\mathfrak{L}_{2}(\varepsilon).

Remark

((Sub-)Optimality of Lemma
8
)
.

The modulus of continuity bound (
44
) is obtained as a result of

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq 3\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\left[3\omega(\eta)+\frac{\eta}{\omega^{-1}(1)}+\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mathrm{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\right],

where
\Delta
represents the symmetric difference of sets. This bound holds without any assumptions on
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
(i.e., we do not need
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
). In controlling the second term on the right hand side, the in-radius of
S_{t}(z)
is used, and this is obtained from
z\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}.
Lemma
8
controls this term when
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
for arbitrary convex sets with non-empty interior (in particular, with no assumption on the smoothness of the boundary). It is possible that for specific convex sets such as polygons and ellipsoids, better bounds could be obtained. We believe, however, that in the worst case Lemma
8
is sharp.

The following lemma, deriving bounds on distance to boundary, plays the most crucial role in the derivation of uniqueness and regularity properties of rectified flow.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
and
(B2)
hold. For
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
, let
y(\cdot)
be any function satisfying

y(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v(s,y(s))ds\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,T),

(47)

with
T=\sup\{t\in[0,1]:\,y(s)\in\Omega^{\circ}\mbox{ for all }s\in[0,t]\}
. Then
T=1
. (This implies that every solution exists on
[0,1]
.)
Furthermore, any solution
y:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
must satisfy

\mathrm{dist}(y(t),\,\partial\Omega)\geq(1-t)\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,1].

(48)

A proof can be found in Section
S.6.2
. Lemma
9
provides a lower bound on the distance to boundary of the path. Unfortunately, that quantitative bound does not imply that
z(1)\in\Omega^{\circ}
if
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
. The following result argues indirectly that
z(1)\in\Omega^{\circ}
for almost all
x
, which will be useful in establishing better rates of convergence for the rectified flow.

To state the result, we need some notation. For each
t\in[0,1]
and
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
, let
\mathfrak{R}(t,x)
be the unique solution to (
42
) in the sense that

\mathfrak{R}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))ds\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,1],\;x\in\Omega^{\circ}.

(49)

Note that Theorem
8
proves such a function is uniquely defined under assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
, and
(B3)
, which are also used in the following result. Also, define

\mathfrak{d}(x):=\inf_{t\in[0,1]}\,\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(t,x),\,\partial\Omega)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\Omega^{\circ}.

(50)

and for
t\in[0,1]
,

\begin{split}B(t)&:=\{z^{*}\in\Omega:\,z\mapsto v(t,z)\mbox{ is not differentiable at }z^{*}\}\\
&=\left\{z^{*}\in\Omega:\,\limsup_{h\to 0}\,\frac{v(t,z^{*}+h)-v(t,z^{*})}{h}\neq\liminf_{h\to 0}\,\frac{v(t,z^{*}+h)-v(t,z^{*})}{h}\right\}.\end{split}

(51)

Lemma 10
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
, and
(B3)
hold. Consider the events

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{1}&:=\{z\in\Omega:\,\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)>0\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{2}&:=\{z\in\Omega:\,\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,z),\,\partial\Omega)>0\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{3}&:=\{(t,z)\in[0,1]\times\Omega:\,\mathfrak{R}(t,z)\notin B(t)\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{4}&:=\{z\in\Omega:\,\mathrm{Leb}(\{t\in[0,1]:\,\mathfrak{R}(t,z)\in B(t)\})=0\}.\end{split}

(52)

Then
\mu_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})=1
. Furthermore, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathfrak{d}(x)~\geq~\frac{\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\,\partial\Omega)\varepsilon}{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)+\varepsilon},\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},

(53)

and for any
\gamma\in[0,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/2]
, setting

\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}:=\{x\in\Omega:\,\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma\},

(54)

we have

\mu_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma})\leq\gamma\frac{2^{d}d\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}{r_{\mathrm{in}}\underline{\mathfrak{p}}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\inf_{x\in\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{c}\cap\Omega^{-\varepsilon}}\,\mathfrak{d}(x)~\geq~\frac{\gamma\varepsilon}{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)+\varepsilon}.

(55)

Finally, if for some
C\geq 0
such that
\omega(\eta)\leq C\eta
for all
\eta>0
, then
(\mathrm{Leb}\times\mu_{0})(\mathcal{E}_{3})=1,
and
\mu_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{4})=1
.

A proof of Lemma
10
can be found in Section
S.6.3
. The first part of the result proves that
\mathfrak{d}(x)>0
for almost all
x\in\Omega
. Inequality (
53
) proves that if
\mathfrak{R}(0,x)
and
\mathfrak{R}(1,x)
are away from the boundary, then the entire path is away from the boundary. This follows from Lemma
9
and the boundedness of the velocity field. Inequality (
55
), on the other hand, proves that for almost all
x\in\Omega
,
\mathfrak{R}(1,x)
is away from the boundary. It is not obvious if one can obtain quantitative lower bounds on the distance of
\mathfrak{R}(1,x)
from the boundary for all
x\in\Omega
, without additional assumptions on
\Omega
. The last part of Lemma
10
proves that for almost all
(s,x)\in[0,1]\times\Omega
,
\mathfrak{R}(s,x)
is a differentiability point of
z\mapsto v(s,z)
.

Following Lemma
10
, we now present a result on the (smoothness) regularity of the rectified flow. From the stability/perturbation results of ODEs, uniform continuity of the rectified flow is not hard to derive. The modulus of continuity, however, depends very strongly on the distance to boundary of rectified flow path.

Theorem 9

(Uniform Continuity of Rectified Flow)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,
x\mapsto\mathfrak{R}(t,x)
is uniformly continuous on
\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
. Indeed, for all
\eta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},t\in[0,1],\\
\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|~

\displaystyle\leq~\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(\eta)/2+\mathfrak{C}_{1}+\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\max\left\{\frac{4\mathfrak{C}_{2}}{2\mathfrak{C}_{2}-\varepsilon\Psi(\eta)},\,\frac{\eta}{\Psi^{-1}(-2\mathfrak{C}_{2}/\varepsilon)}\right\},

for some constants
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}
depending only on
d,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega),
and
\omega^{-1}(1)
.

Moreover, for any
\gamma\in[0,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/2]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{c}\cap\Omega^{-\varepsilon},t\in[0,1],\\
\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|~

\displaystyle\leq~\Psi^{-1}\left(\Psi(\eta)+\mathfrak{C}_{1}+\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}{\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{3}}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right),

for some constants
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{3}
depending only on
d,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega),\omega^{-1}(1)
. (These constants may be different from the ones above.)

A proof of Theorem
9
can be found in Section
S.6.4
. To better understand the implications of Theorem
9
, consider the case where the densities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are Lipschitz continuous so that
\omega(\eta)\leq\eta
.
1
1

1

We assume the Lipschitz constant of 1, for simplicity.
In this case,
\Psi(u)=\ln(u)/2
which diverges to
-\infty
as
u\to 0
. In this case, the first part of Theorem
9
implies

\begin{split}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},t\in[0,1],\\
\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|~&\leq~\frac{8\mathfrak{C}_{2}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{4\mathfrak{C}_{2}+\varepsilon\ln(1/\eta)}+\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\exp\left(-\frac{4\mathfrak{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)\eta\\
&\quad+\exp\left(2\mathfrak{C}_{1}+\frac{2\mathfrak{C}_{2}\ln(\mbox{diam}(\Omega)/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\right)\eta^{1/2}.\end{split}

(56)

Although the right hand side converges to zero as
\eta\to 0
, it converges at an extremely slow logarithmic rate (from the first term on the right hand side). Additionally, the bound diverges exponentially fast with
\varepsilon\to 0
. It is unclear if this is bound on the modulus of continuity can be improved without further assumptions on
\Omega
. Any improvement in the Lipschitz constant (in Lemma
8
) of the velocity field yields an improvement of the modulus of continuity.

While the bound in general is pessimistic, the second part of Theorem
9
provides a better bound for almost all
x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
. Indeed, it yields

\begin{split}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{c}\cap\Omega^{-\varepsilon},t\in[0,1],\\
\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|~&\leq~\exp\left(2\mathfrak{C}_{1}+\frac{2\mathfrak{C}_{2}}{\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{2\mathfrak{C}_{3}}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right)\eta.\end{split}

(57)

In comparison with (
56
), inequality (
57
) shows that for almost all
x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
, the rectified flow map is Lipschitz continuous. But note that the Lipschitz constant is exponentially quickly growing with
1/\varepsilon
and
1/\gamma
.

6.2
Rates of convergence of estimates of the velocity and rectified flow

Because
v_{t}
is not smooth, we do not use
a regression estimator.
Instead we use a density-based estimator
based on the form given in
Lemma
1
.
The rate given in
Section
3

is not uniform in
t

so we will derive better rates in this section.
We define a new density estimator that accomodates
arbitrary boundaries.
To derive the rates of convergence of the rectified flow, we need perturbation bounds as in Theorem
5
. Unfortunately, our Lipschitz continuity bound in Lemma
8
is not strong enough to directly apply Theorem
5
. Hence, we follow the proof of Theorem
5
and derive the rates of convergence for the rectified flow. We define the following collection of velocity fields on
[0,1]\times\Omega
and prove a general equicontinuity property.

A velocity field
\nu:[0,1]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to belong to
\mathcal{V}
if there exists
\varpi(\cdot)
satisfying
(B3)
and a constants
\mathfrak{C}
such that:

(P1)

\|\nu(t,z)\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
for all
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

(P2)

For every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},\\
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\,\|\nu(t,z)-\nu(t,z^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq~\mathfrak{C}\varpi(\eta)+~\frac{\mathfrak{C}\eta}{\varepsilon}\times\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if }\min\{t,1-t\}\leq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega),\\
1/\min\{t,1-t\},&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(P3)

For every
t\in[0,1]
and
z\in\Omega
,

\nu(t,z)~\in~S_{t}(z)~=~\frac{z-\Omega}{t}\cap\frac{\Omega-z}{1-t}.

(The constant
\mathfrak{C}
and the function
\varpi(\cdot)
are the same for all functions in
\mathcal{V}
.) Note that if
\widetilde{\nu}:[0,1]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
(P2)
but not
(P1)
or
(P3)
, then one can consider

\nu(t,x):=\mbox{Proj}_{(\Omega-\Omega)\cap S_{t}(x)}(\widetilde{\nu}(t,x)),

which would satisfy all the assumptions. Here
\Omega-\Omega=\{z-z^{\prime}:\,z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega\}
. In other words, assumptions
(P1)
–
(P3)
are not restrictive.

From the proof of Theorem
8
, it follows that for every
\nu\in\mathcal{V}
, the integral equation (
42
) (with
\nu
replacing
v
) has a unique Carathéodory solution that lies entirely in
\Omega
. The following result proves an equicontinuity result for the solutions in terms of
\nu(\cdot,\cdot)
. For each
\nu\in\mathcal{V}
, let
\mathfrak{R}_{\nu}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the unique function satisfying

\mathfrak{R}_{\nu}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\nu(s,\mathfrak{R}_{\nu}(s,x))ds,\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\Omega^{\circ},\,t\in[0,1].

(58)

Define the supremum distance on
\mathcal{V}
as follows. For any two functions
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
, set

\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{\infty}~:=~\sup_{t\in[0,1],\,z\in\Omega^{\circ}}\,\|\nu_{1}(t,z)-\nu_{2}(t,z)\|.

Finally, set

\overline{\Psi}(u):=\int\frac{du}{\varpi(u)+u}du.

(This is an indefinite integral.)

Theorem 10
.

For every
x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
with
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\Delta
, we have

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\,\|\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{1}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{2}}(t,x)\|&\leq\overline{\Psi}^{-1}(\overline{\Psi}(\Delta)/2+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)\\
&\quad+\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\max\left\{\frac{4C}{2C-\varepsilon\overline{\Psi}(\Delta)},\,\frac{\Delta}{\overline{\Psi}^{-1}(-C/\varepsilon)}\right\},\end{split}

(59)

where
C
is a constant depending only on
\mathfrak{C}
(in
(P2)
). Moreover, if
x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
and there exists a
\gamma>0
such that

\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{2}}(1,x),\,\partial\Omega)\geq 2\gamma,

(60)

then, for all small enough
\Delta
(so that the right hand side of (
59
) is less than
\gamma
) and
\nu_{1}\in\mathcal{V}
satisfying
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\Delta
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{1}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{2}}(t,x)\|\leq\overline{\Psi}^{-1}\left(\overline{\Psi}(\Delta)+\frac{C}{\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right),

(61)

for a constant
C
depending only on
\mathfrak{C}
(in
(P2)
).

The proof of Theorem
10
is almost the same as that of Theorem
9
, and a condensed version of the proof can be found in Section
S.6.5
. The assumptions of Theorem
10
can be weakened slightly. It suffices to assume that both
\nu_{j},j=1,2
satisfy Properties
(P1)
and
(P3)
so as to ensure that
\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{j}}(t,x)\in\Omega^{-(1-t)\varepsilon},j=1,2
. Additionally, it suffices that one of
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
satisfy property
(P2)
for the validity of (
59
) and (
61
). Finally, under (
60
), we do not need
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\Delta
, we just need
\sup_{t\in[0,1],\,z\in\Omega^{-\kappa}}\|\nu_{1}(t,z)-\nu_{2}(t,z)\|\leq\Delta
for some
\kappa>0
depending on
\varepsilon,\gamma
. (This
\kappa
is precisely the right-hand side of (
53
).)

Theorem
10
proves that (uniform) closeness of velocity fields implies the closeness of the unique solutions. As remarked after Theorem
9
, the modulus of continuity with respect to the velocity field is not optimistic for arbitrary
x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
. When the distance of the solution to the boundary is bounded away from zero, then one obtains a better modulus of continuity. In fact, if
\varpi(\eta)=\eta
, then inequality (
61
) implies

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{1}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}_{\nu_{2}}(t,x)\|\leq\Delta\exp\left(\frac{C}{\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right).

A simple application of Theorem
10
is to understand the effect of discretization algorithms in estimating the solutions of (
42
). For example, Euler discretization corresponds to taking (for some
k\geq 1
)

\nu_{1}(t,x)=v(\lfloor kt\rfloor/k,\,x)\quad\mbox{for}\quad x\in\Omega,\,t\in[0,1].

One can apply Theorem
10
with
\nu_{2}\equiv v
and obtain an error bound in terms of
k
.
Note that, in this case,
\Delta=\sup_{x\in\Omega^{\circ}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|v(t,x)-v(\lfloor kt\rfloor/k,\,x)\|
, which can be controlled if
v(\cdot,\cdot)
is Lipschitz continuous in the first argument, uniformly in the second argument. The following result provides such Lipschitz continuity.

Proposition 4
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
hold. Also, suppose
\omega(\eta)\leq L\eta
for all
\eta>0
for some
L\geq 0
. Then for all
t\in[0,1]
and
h>0
such that
t+h\in[0,1]
, we have

\sup_{z\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}}\,\|v(t,z)-v(t+h,z)\|\leq 2\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\left(8L\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)+\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{4^{d+1}d\mathrm{diam}^{2}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon^{2}\bar{t}}\right).

A proof can be found in Section
S.6.8
.

Theorem
10
also allows us to consider rates of convergence of rectified flow estimators based on the rates of convergence of estimators of the velocity field. In particular, consider the density-based estimator of the velocity field (as discussed in Section
3.2.1
). Proposition
2
provides a simple result on the rate of convergence that is unfortunately inapplicable for Theorem
10
because the rate is not uniform in
t
. The following result provides a better rate of convergence using assumptions
(B1)
–
(B2)
.

Suppose
\widehat{p}_{0}
and
\widehat{p}_{1}
are the estimators of densities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
, respectively. (We do not require
\widehat{p}_{0}
and
\widehat{p}_{1}
to be densities or to be supported on
\Omega
. In case their support is not
\Omega
, we redefine
\widehat{p}_{j}(z)
as
\widehat{p}_{j}(z)\mathbf{1}\{z\in\Omega\}
.) Set

r_{n}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\,\max\left\{\left|\ln\frac{\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{p_{0}(x)}\right|,\,\left|\ln\frac{\widehat{p}_{1}(x)}{p_{1}(x)}\right|\right\}.

(62)

It is easy to see that, by definition,

e^{-r_{n}}p_{j}(x)\leq\widehat{p}_{j}(x)\leq e^{r_{n}}p_{j}(x)\quad\mbox{for}\quad j=1,2,\,x\in\Omega.

(63)

Additionally, if
r_{n}\to 0
, then (under assumption
(B2)
)

\max_{j=1,2}\,\|\widehat{p}_{j}-p_{j}\|_{\infty}~\asymp~r_{n}.

Define, as in (
15
),

\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z):=\frac{\int_{S_{t}(z)}\delta\widehat{p}_{0}(z-t\delta)\widehat{p}_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta}{\int_{S_{t}(z)}\widehat{p}_{0}(z-t\delta)\widehat{p}_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta}.

The domain of integration here is
S_{t}(z)
because
\widehat{p}_{0}
and
\widehat{p}_{1}
are assumed to be supported on
\Omega
(which is possible only if
\Omega
is known). Under this setting, the following result holds.

Theorem 11

(Rate of convergence of velocity field)
.

Suppose
\widehat{p}_{0}
and
\widehat{p}_{1}
are the estimators of densities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
, respectively. Then, for all
n\geq 1
,

\|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}-v\|_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in[0,1],\,z\in\Omega^{\circ}}\|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z)-v(t,z)\|~\leq~2\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)(e^{4r_{n}}-1).

If
r_{n}=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
, then

\sup_{t\in[0,1],\,z\in\Omega^{\circ}}\|\widehat{v}(t,z)-v(t,z)\|=O_{p}(r_{n}),\quad\mbox{as}\quad n\to\infty.

See Section
S.6.6
for a proof.

Theorem 12

(Rate of convergence of rectified flow)
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
11
. Suppose that

\widehat{\omega}(\eta):=\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega,\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq\eta}\,\max_{j=1,2}\left|\frac{\widehat{p}_{j}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{j}(x^{\prime})}-1\right|\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\eta>0,

with
\widehat{\omega}(\cdot)
satisfying assumption
(B3)
. Then
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfying

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(s,\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(s,x))ds,

(64)

is well-defined (i.e., uniquely) for all
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
. If, for some
\varepsilon,\gamma>0
,

x\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\,\partial\Omega)\geq 2\gamma,

(65)

then

\|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(1,x)-\mathfrak{R}(1,x)\|\leq{\Psi}^{-1}\left({\Psi}(2\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)(e^{4r_{n}}-1))+\frac{C}{\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right).

(Here
\Psi(u)=\int du/(\omega(u)+u)
from assumption
(B3)
.)

Proof of Theorem
12
.

From the setting of Theorem
11
and the assumption on
\widehat{\omega}(\cdot)
, we get that
\widehat{p}_{0},\widehat{p}_{1}
satisfy assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
, and
(W1)
, and hence, Theorem
8
implies the uniqueness of the solution to (
64
). The second part of the result follows from assumption
65
and Theorem
10
(in particular, (
61
)).
∎

Theorem
12
implies that the rectified flow shares the same rate of convergence as the velocity field if
\omega(u)\leq Lu
for all
u\geq 0
(i.e., the densities are Lipschitz continuous).

Corollary 2

(Rate of convergence of rectified flow)
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
11
. If
\omega(u)\leq Lu
for all
u\geq 0
, then

\|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(1,x)-\mathfrak{R}(1,x)\|\leq 2\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)(e^{4r_{n}}-1)\exp\left(\frac{C(L+1)}{\min\{\varepsilon,\,\gamma\}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{C(L+1)}{\varepsilon\gamma}\right).

6.3
Linearization and Asymptotic Normality

In this section, we prove an expansion for
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(1,x)-\mathfrak{R}(1,x)
in terms of
\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}-v
. The following lemma on the Lipschitz continuity of
\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}-v
is crucial for such an expansion.

Lemma 11
.

Suppose
\max\{\widehat{\omega}(\eta),\,\omega(\eta)\}\leq L\eta
for all
\eta>0
.
Define

s_{n}=\esssup_{x\in\Omega^{\circ}}\,\max\left\{\left|\frac{d\log\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{dx}-\frac{d\log p_{0}(x)}{dx}\right|,\,\left|\frac{d\log\widehat{p}_{1}(x)}{dx}-\frac{d\log p_{1}(x)}{dx}\right|\right\}.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
\eta\leq\min\{1/L,\,\varepsilon^{2}/\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},\\
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z)-v(t,z)-\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z^{\prime})+v(t,z^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\eta(e^{4r_{n}}-1)(\varepsilon^{-2}+\eta\varepsilon^{-4})+C^{\prime\prime}(1+\eta\varepsilon^{-2})^{2}\left[\exp(s_{n}\eta)-1+(e^{r_{n}}-1)L\eta\right],

for some constants
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
depending only on
d,L,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega),
and
(\overline{\mathfrak{p}},\,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})
. In particular, for
\eta
small enough and
n
large enough (so that
r_{n}
is small enough), we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon},\\
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z)-v(t,z)-\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(t,z^{\prime})+v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\eta(r_{n}+s_{n}),

for a constant
C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
depending on
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime},
and
\varepsilon
.

Theorem 13

(Linearization)
.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}
. (Recall the definitions from Lemma
10
.) Then
z\mapsto v(s,z)
is differentiable at
z=\mathfrak{R}(s,x)
for almost all
s\in[0,1]
. Let the derivative be denoted by
\partial_{z}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))
. Define
\widetilde{E}(t,x)
as the unique solution of the integral equation

\widetilde{E}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\{\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))-v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))\}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{z}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))\widetilde{E}(s,x)ds.

(66)

The existence of a unique solution to (
66
) follows from Proposition
7
. Indeed, this unique solution can be written as

\widetilde{E}(t,x)=\Phi(t)\int_{0}^{t}(\Phi(s))^{-1}\{\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))-v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))\}ds,

(67)

where
\Phi(\cdot)
is the unique invertible solution to the matrix differential equation

\Phi(t)=I+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{z}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))^{\top}\Phi(s)ds.

(68)

(Here
I
is the identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.)
Then, assuming
r_{n}+s_{n}=o_{p}(1)
, we get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\widetilde{E}(t,x)\|=o_{p}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widetilde{E}(t,x)\|\right).

(69)

A proof of Theorem
13
can be found in Section
S.6.9
. An important implication of Theorem
13
is that the rate of convergence and the limiting distribution of
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x)
matches that of
\widetilde{E}(t,x)
, if
\widetilde{E}(t,x)
and
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widetilde{E}(t,x)\|
share the same rate of convergence. Theorem
13
does not require any specific structure on the density estimators as long as
r_{n}+s_{n}=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

The condition of same rate of convergence can be verified by proving tightness of the process
t\mapsto a_{n}\widetilde{E}(t,x)
where
a_{n}
is such that
a_{n}\widetilde{E}(1,x)=O_{p}(1)
. Note that
\widetilde{E}(0,x)=0
and hence, verifying certain moment condition on the increment
a_{n}(\widetilde{E}(t,x)-\widetilde{E}(s,x))
would imply
a_{n}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widetilde{E}(t,x)\|=O_{p}(1)
; see, for example, Example 2.2.7 of
97
or Section 1.3 of
91
. However,
\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(s,z)-v(s,z)
is a ratio of random quantities which makes it difficult to directly apply this technique. This can be resolved by a further linearlization of
\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{den}}(s,z)-v(s,z)
as shown in the proof of Theorem
14
.

The Density Estimator.

The limiting distribution of properly standardized
\widetilde{E}(t,x)
depends heavily on the density estimators. We provide the following general limiting distribution result for a specific higher-order kernel-based density estimator.

We describe our estimator of densities
p_{0},p_{1}
. (Although we expect this estimator to be known, we could not find an explicit reference.) For any
z\in\Omega
and bandwidth
h>0
, set
V_{z,h}=(\Omega-z)\cap\mathcal{B}(0,h)
. Note that if
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
and
h
is small enough, then
V_{z,h}=\mathcal{B}(0,h)
. Our assumption
(B1)
on
\Omega
implies that
V_{z,h}
is a convex set with a non-empty interior for any
z\in\Omega
and
h>0
. Let
K_{z,h}:V_{z,h}\to\mathbb{R}
be an order
m\geq 1
kernel function satisfying

\int_{V_{z,h}}K_{z,h}(u)du=\mbox{Vol}(V_{z,h}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\{0,1,\ldots\}^{d},\\
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}\leq m\end{subarray}}\,\left|\int_{V_{z,h}}K_{z,h}(u)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{d}u_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}\right)du\right|=0.

(70)

The existence of such a function follows from defining an
L_{2}
space on
V_{z,h}
and applying Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization. In fact, set
\widetilde{K}(u)=\mathbf{1}\{u\in V_{z,h}\}
. For any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots\}^{d}
with
\|\alpha\|_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}\leq m
, define
\varphi_{\alpha}(u)=\prod_{j=1}^{d}u_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}.

Set
\varphi_{0}(u)=1
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
u\in V_{z,h}
, define

\Upsilon_{m}(u)~=~(\varphi_{\alpha}(u))_{1\leq\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq m}
,
where the elements are organized with respect to the total ordering on
\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots\}^{d}
defined for
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
by
\alpha\prec\alpha^{\prime}
if and only if
\|\alpha\|_{1}<\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{1}
, or
\|\alpha\|_{1}=\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{1}
and
\alpha_{i^{*}}<\alpha^{\prime}_{i^{*}}
where
i^{*}=\min\{1\leq i\leq d:\,\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\}
.
Define

\mathcal{B}_{m,h}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Upsilon_{m}(u)\Upsilon_{m}^{\top}(u)\widetilde{K}(u)du.

Lemma 6.1 of
12
implies that
\mathcal{B}_{m,h}(z)
is a positive definite matrix.
Set

\widetilde{K}_{z,h}(u)=\widetilde{K}(u)\left(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{K}(r)\Upsilon_{m}^{\top}(r)\mathcal{B}_{m,h}(z)^{-1}\Upsilon_{m}(u)dr\right).

This is the residual obtained from regressing
\widetilde{K}(u)
on
\Upsilon_{m}(u)
in
L_{2}(V_{z,h})
and hence, satisfies the second condition of (
70
). To satisfy the first condition of (
70
), it suffices to normalize
\widetilde{K}_{z,h}(u)
. Note that the boundedness of
V_{z,h}
implies that
K_{z,h}(\cdot)
, defined this way, is also bounded.

Define the kernel density estimator

\widehat{p}_{j}(z)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K_{z,h}(X_{ji}-z)}{\mbox{Vol}(V_{z,h})}.

(71)

Recall that
X_{j1},\ldots,X_{jn}
represents an IID sample from
\mu_{j},j=0,1
. For faster rates for the density estimator, we need to strengthen the smoothness assumption on the densities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
. Following
12
, we consider the following assumption:

(B4)

The densities
p_{j},j=0,1
are
\beta
-smooth, i.e., for all
z\in\Omega
, there exists a polynomial
q_{z,j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of degree
\llfloor\beta\rrfloor
2
2

2

\llfloor\beta\rrfloor
is the greatest integer smaller than
\beta
. In particular,
\llfloor\beta\rrfloor=\beta-1
if
\beta
is an integer.
such that

|p_{j}(z+u)-q_{z,j}(u)|\leq L\|u\|^{\beta},\quad\mbox{for all }u\in V_{z,h}.

It is interesting to note that
2
is not a traditional smoothness assumption in the sense that
2
does not require any strong notion of differentiability at the boundary of
\Omega
. (See Remark 2 of
12
for more details.)

Proposition 5
.

Suppose assumption
2
holds and that
K_{z,h}:V_{z,h}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
m
-th order kernel for some
m\geq\llfloor\beta\rrfloor
. Additionally, assume that
|K_{z,h}(u)|\leq\mathfrak{K}
for all
u\in V_{z,h},z\in\Omega
. Then the density estimator (
71
) satisfies

\max_{j=0,1}\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left|\mathbb{E}[\widehat{p}_{j}(z)]-p_{j}(z)\right|\leq L\mathfrak{K}h^{\beta},

and

\mathrm{Var}(\widehat{p}_{j}(z))~\leq~\frac{\mathfrak{K}^{2}\|p_{j}\|_{\infty}}{n\mathrm{Vol}(V_{z,h})},\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\Omega,\;j=0,1.

Additionally, under assumption
(B1)
,
\mathrm{Vol}(V_{z,h})/\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{B}(0,h))
stays bounded away from zero for all
z\in\Omega
, and hence,
\mathrm{Vol}(V_{z,h})\geq Ch^{d}
for all
z\in\Omega
(for small enough
h
).

A proof of Proposition
5
can be found in Section
S.6.10
. The final part of Proposition
5
appeared as an assumption in several works; see, for example, Assumption X of
39
, Definition 2 of
26
, and Definition 2.2 of
21
. Proposition
5
implies that the variance converges to zero at the rate of
1/(nh^{d})
. Additionally, assuming
\mathcal{K}=\{u\mapsto K_{z,h}(u):\,h\geq 0,\,z\in\Omega\}
satisfies
N(\varepsilon,\mathcal{K})\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
for some
C>0
and
\alpha>0
1
1

1

See
38
for details on the covering number.
, the proof of Theorem 1 (and corollary 1)
of
38
implies that with probability 1,

\sup_{h\in[a_{n},b_{n}]}\sup_{z\in\Omega}|\widehat{p}_{j}(z)-p_{j}(z)|=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/a_{n})+\log\log n}{na_{n}^{d}}}+b_{n}^{\beta}\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad n\to\infty,

(72)

whenever
b_{n}\to 0,
and
na_{n}^{d}/\log n\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. (This is an almost sure convergence statement.)

Theorem 14

(Asymptotic Normality)
.

Fix any
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
such that
\mathfrak{R}(s,x)\in\Omega^{\circ}
for all
s\in[0,1]
. Consider the density estimators
\widehat{p}_{j}(\cdot)
as in (
71
). Then for any
h\to 0
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x)\|=O_{p}\left(h^{\beta}+\frac{(\log(1/h))^{\mathbf{1}\{d=1\}/2}}{\sqrt{nh^{d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1)}}}\right).

(73)

Take any
h=o(n^{-1/(2\beta+d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1))})
as
n\to\infty
.
Further suppose

\|\widehat{E}(1,x)-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{E}(1,x)]\|~\gg~\frac{(\log(1/h))^{\mathbf{1}\{d=1\}/2}}{\sqrt{nh^{d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1)}}},

(74)

then

\frac{(\log(1/h))^{\mathbf{1}\{d=1\}/2}}{\sqrt{nh^{d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1)}}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(1,x)-\mathfrak{R}(1,x))~\overset{d}{\to}~N(0,\Sigma(x)),

for some covariance matrix
\Sigma(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

A proof of Theorem
14
can be found in Section
S.6.11
. The first part (
73
) proves that for
h\asymp n^{-1/(2\beta+d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1))}
, we get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x)\|=O_{p}\left(n^{-\beta/(2\beta+d(d-1)_{+}/(d+1))}(\log n)^{\mathbf{1}\{d=1\}/2}\right).

This is better than the usual non-parametric rate of convergence of
n^{-\beta/(2\beta+d)}
but not as good as the rate of convergence for the optimal transport map. It is not readily obvious if this is the correct rate for rectified flow.
For asymptotic normality, we use a smaller bandwidth for undersmoothing.
The (cumbersome) assumption (
74
) is made to ensure that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widetilde{E}(t,x)\|=O_{p}(\|\widetilde{E}(1,x)\|)
. The proof of Theorem
14
proves that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\widetilde{E}(t,x)-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{E}(t,x)]\|
is at most the right hand side of (
74
).

6.4
Definition of Velocity field (Strictly Convex Domain)

A set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be strictly convex at a boundary point
a\in\partial A
if for all
b\in A
(
b\neq a
),
(a+b)/2\in A^{\circ}
.
A quantitative measure of strict convexity at a point
a\in A
can be defined as

\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon;A):=\inf\left\{\mbox{dist}\left(\frac{a+b}{2},\,\partial A\right):\,b\in A,\,\|a-b\|\geq\varepsilon\right\},\quad\mbox{for}\quad\varepsilon\in[0,\mbox{diam}(A)].

(75)

This is closely related to the modulus of uniform convexity of a set
(
31
;
8
;
32
)
. However, unlike the existing definitions, we define the modulus of uniform convexity at a point. (Definition 3.1 of
31
can be recovered by taking the infimum over all
a\in A
.) We make this distinction to allow for sets
A
that are strictly convex at some points but not at others (cf. Observation 3.3(d) of
31
). For any set
A
, define

\mathrm{SC}(A):=\left\{a\in\partial A:\,\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon;A)>0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\varepsilon\in(0,\mbox{diam}(A)]\right\}.

It is easy to see that if
a\in\mathrm{SC}(A)
, then
A
is strictly convex at
a
. One can also prove the converse for compact sets:
A
is strictly convex at
a
implies
a\in\mathrm{SC}(A)
, if
A
is compact. A proof can be obtained by contradiction.
3
3

3

Suppose, if possible, for some
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon;A)=0
. This implies that for any
k\geq 1
, there exists
b_{k}\in A
such that
\|a-b_{k}\|\geq\varepsilon
and
\mbox{dist}((a+b_{k})/2,\,\partial A)\leq 1/k
.
The compactness of
A
implies that every sequence has a subsequence that converges. Let
b^{*}
be one such limit. Then the above conditions imply that
\mbox{dist}((a+b^{*})/2,\partial\Omega)=0
and
\|a-b^{*}\|\geq\varepsilon
, which is a contradiction to the hypothesis that
a
is a point of strict convexity.

The set
\mathrm{SC}(A)
is allowed to be empty; in fact, for polyhedral sets
A
,
\mathrm{SC}(A)=\emptyset

(
31
, Observation 3.3(d))
.
A set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be strictly convex if it is strictly convex at all its boundary points (or equivalently,
\mathrm{SC}(A)=\partial A
).

Proposition 6

(Well-defined Velocity)
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
and
(B2)
hold. Then the velocity field
v(\cdot,\cdot)
defined as

v(t,z)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]-z,&\mbox{if }t=0,z\in\Omega,\\
\frac{\int_{S_{t}(z)}\delta p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta}{\int_{S_{t}(z)}p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta},&\mbox{if }t\in(0,1),z\in\Omega^{\circ},\\
z-\mathbb{E}[X_{0}],&\mbox{if }t=1,z\in\Omega,\end{cases}

is continuous in
z
for each
t\in[0,1]
on its domain. Additionally,
v(t,z)=0
for
t\in(0,1)
and
z\in\mathrm{SC}(\Omega)
is the unique continuous extension of
z\mapsto v(t,z)
to
\mathrm{SC}(\Omega)
.

See Section
S.6.12
for a proof. Additionally, from the proof, it follows that for any
z\in\mathrm{SC}(\Omega)
and
z^{\prime}\in\Omega
, we have

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq\frac{\|z^{\prime}-z\|+\mathfrak{m}_{z}^{-1}(\|z-z^{\prime}\|/2;\,\Omega)}{\min\{t,1-t\}}.

In particular, this implies that the velocity field is uniformly continuous in the second argument if
\Omega
is strictly convex. Unfortunately, this does not suffice to establish a unique solution for (
42
) when the starting point is at the boundary, because the Lipschitz constant is not necessarily integrable. Even in the univariate case with the domain of
[0,1]
, one can construct densities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
for which the Lipschitz constant at time
t
behaves like
C/t
for
t<1/2
and
C>1
. On the other hand, clearly,
z(t)=\mathfrak{R}(t,x)=x
for all
t\in[0,1]
is a solution to (
42
) when
x\in\mathrm{SC}(\Omega)
.

7
Numerical experiments

We supplement our theoretical results with simulations highlighting the main features of rectified transport estimators. In the first experiment, in Figure
4
, we show samples from the kernel-regression estimator of trajectories
z_{t}(x)
, and hence
R(x)=z_{1}(x)
, for different starting points
x
. In this case, by the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
1
, the ground truth trajectories are given by
z_{t}(x)=\sqrt{t^{2}+(1-t)^{2}}x
, and in particular,
R(x)=x
. As we decrease the bandwidth parameter, the bias decreases, but the variance increases. However, the variance remains bounded even for small values of
h
, consistent with our result that the variance is of constant order in the one-dimensional case, Corollary
1
. Interestingly, although for larger values of
h
there are significant values at intermediate values of
t
, this tends to be smaller for
R(x)=z_{1}(x)
.

Figure 4
:
Estimating trajectories
z_{t}(x)
and
R(x)=z_{1}(x)
in the one-dimensional case, estimating the velocity with kernel regression. Each plot shows results for a different choice of bandwidth parameter. We show true and estimated trajectories
z_{t}(x)=z(t,0,x)
as a function of different starting points
x
, where
X_{0},X_{1}
are independent standard Gaussians. Estimators are based on
n=200
samples. Ground true trajectories are shown in black, and colored solid lines show mean trajectories over 1000 experiment repetitions, for each starting point (different colors). Shades represent 95% empirical intervals from these repetitions. Additionally, dashed colored lines show one selected sample from the kernel regression-based estimator.

In the second experiment, in Figure
5
, we show different approaches for estimating the transport map between high-dimensional (
d=50
) standard Gaussians. In this case, the Rectified transport coincides with the optimal transport, the identity function. To visualize our estimates, we plot each coordinate estimated function as a function of a unique variable while making all the other zeros; i.e., in plot
i,j
we show the function
\widehat{R}_{i}(0,\ldots,x_{j},\ldots,0)
. The first is a plug-in estimator for
R(x)
in the Gaussian case, as described in (
11
), where we replace the true means and covariance with their sample versions (purple). In the second estimator, we estimate the velocity by regressing
X_{1}-X_{0}
on
X_{t}
using a linear model on all coordinates (orange). The third estimator (blue) is like the second, but we used a cross-validated Lasso instead of linear regression. Finally, we used kernel regression (green) with bandwidth
h=1.0
and a Gaussian kernel.

All estimators exhibit reasonable performance, although the linear estimator is typically the worst. Even if the Nadaraya-Watson estimator has some errors, this is reasonable as we did not attempt parameter tuning. The best results are attained for the plug-in and Lasso estimators, illustrating the important point that we should encode in the estimators whatever structure of the underlying function is available to us. For example, the Lasso regression expresses that each velocity component is a sparse function of its coordinates, and our approach explicitly allows us to encode this in the modeling of the velocity.

Figure 5
:
Performance of four rectified estimators in the Gaussian case. We drew
n=100
samples from
X_{1},X_{0}\sim N\left(0,I_{d}\right)
with
d=50
.
In plot
(i,j)
we show
\widehat{R}_{i}(0,\ldots,x_{j},\ldots,0)
as a function of
x_{j}\in[-3,3]
. We show only the first 36=6x6 described above. functions. We consider the plug-in estimator (purple), linear regression (orange), cross-validated Lasso (blue), and kernel regression (green). Dashed black lines represent truth
R_{i}(0,\ldots,x_{j},\ldots,0)=x_{j}\delta_{i=j}
. In all cases, we used a naive ODE discretization by dividing the
[0,1]
interval into
T=50
steps.

8
Conclusions

In this paper, we provide statistical theory and inference for rectified flow for bounded and unbounded random vectors. Although the current work focused on rectified flow that starts with a linear interpolation of random vectors, most of the techniques should carry over to general interpolations discussed in
2
.

Several topics deserve
further investigation.
For example: iterating rectified flow to
approximate optimal transport,
the use of regression and density estimation tools
for estimating rectified flow
and the properties of smoothed rectified flow.
We hope to report on these issues
in the future.

9
and the proof of Theorem
8
. AKK also thanks Tomasz Tkocz and Abhimanyu Choudhary for helpful discussions on convex analysis. GM thanks Tudor Manole for discussions on linearization analysis.
We thank Chandramauli Chakraborty who helped with an earlier version of this work. GM was supported by NSF-DMS 2412895.

Supplement to “Statistical Properties of Rectified Flow”

S.1
Auxiliary Results from Convex Analysis

Lemma 12
.

For a convex set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
a\mapsto\mbox{dist}(a,\,\partial A)
is a concave function.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that for any two points
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
, and
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

\mbox{dist}(a,\,\partial A)\geq\lambda\mbox{dist}(a_{1},\,\partial A)+(1-\lambda)\mbox{dist}(a_{2},\,\partial A)\quad\mbox{for}\quad a=\lambda a_{1}+(1-\lambda)a_{2}.

(E.1)

If
\varepsilon_{j}=\mbox{dist}(a_{j},\partial A)
, then convexity of
A
implies
\mathcal{B}(a_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\subseteq A
. Consider the set

\mathcal{C}=\lambda B(a_{1},\varepsilon_{1})+(1-\lambda)B(a_{2},\varepsilon_{2})=\left\{\lambda b_{1}+(1-\lambda)b_{2}:\,\|b_{j}-a_{j}\|\leq\varepsilon_{j}\mbox{ for }j=1,2\right\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{C}\subseteq A
by convexity. Additionally,
a\in\mathcal{C}
. To prove (
E.1
), it now suffices to show that

\mathcal{B}(a,\,\lambda\varepsilon_{1}+(1-\lambda)\varepsilon_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{C}.

Take any point
b\in\mathcal{B}(a,\,\lambda\varepsilon_{1}+(1-\lambda)\varepsilon_{2})
. Then there exists
u\in S^{d-1}
such that

b=a+(\lambda\varepsilon_{1}+(1-\lambda)\varepsilon_{2})u=\lambda(a_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}u)+(1-\lambda)(a_{2}+\varepsilon_{2}u).

By definition,
a_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}u\in\mathcal{B}(a_{j},\varepsilon_{j})
and hence, the point on the right belongs to
\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 13
.

For a convex set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
x\in A
, if
y\in\partial A
satisfies

\|x-y\|=\inf\{\|x-z\|:\,z\in\partial A\},

then

(x-y)^{\top}(z-y)\geq 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in A.

Proof.

Note for any point
z^{\prime}\notin A
, convexity of
A
implies the existence of
z\in\partial A
such that
z=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)z^{\prime}
. Therefore,

\|x-z^{\prime}\|=\|x-z\|+\|z-z^{\prime}\|\geq\|x-z\|\geq\|x-y\|.

This implies that

\|x-y\|=\inf\{\|x-z\|:\,z\in\mbox{closure}(A^{c})\}.

Proposition 2.2 of
16
implies the result.
∎

Lemma 14
.

Suppose
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a compact convex set such that
\mathcal{B}(0,r)\subseteq A
for some
r>0
. Then, for any
\gamma>0
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)}{\mathrm{Vol}(A)}\leq\frac{d\gamma}{r}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{r}\right)^{d-1}.

Proof.

Steiner formula (Eq. (4.1) of
88
) implies that

\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\binom{d}{m}W_{m}(A)\gamma^{m},

where
W_{m}(A)
represent the quermassintegrals of
A
. (Here
W_{0}(A)=\mbox{Vol}(A)
and
dW_{1}(A)=\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial A)
; see, for example, Eq. (7.7) of
88
.) This implies that

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)}{d\gamma W_{1}(A)}=\sum_{m=1}^{d}\frac{1}{d}\binom{d}{m}\frac{W_{m}(A)}{W_{1}(A)}\gamma^{m-1}.

From Aleksandrov-Fenchel inequality (Eq. (7.66)of
88
with
j=m,i=m-1,k=m+1
), we obtain

\frac{W_{m+1}(A)}{W_{m}(A)}\leq\frac{W_{m}(A)}{W_{m-1}(A)}\leq\cdots\leq\frac{W_{1}(A)}{W_{0}(A)},

which, in turn, implies

\frac{W_{m}(A)}{W_{1}(A)}\leq\left(\frac{W_{1}(A)}{W_{0}(A)}\right)^{m-1}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad m\geq 1.

Therefore, we conclude that

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)}{d\gamma W_{1}(A)}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}\binom{d-1}{m}\left(\frac{\gamma W_{1}(A)}{W_{0}(A)}\right)^{m-1}\leq\left(1+\frac{\gamma W_{1}(A)}{W_{0}(A)}\right)^{d-1}.

Equivalently,

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)}{\mbox{Vol}(A)}\leq\gamma\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial A)}{\mbox{Vol}(A)}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{d}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial A)}{\mbox{Vol}(A)}\right)^{d-1}.

Lemma 2.1 of
44
(or Remark 13 on page 392 of
88
) implies

\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial A)}{\mbox{Vol}(A)}\leq\frac{d}{r}.

Therefore,

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)}{\mbox{Vol}(A)}\leq\frac{d\gamma}{r}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{r}\right)^{d-1},

which proves the result.
∎

Lemma 15
.

For a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, suppose
a\in\mathrm{SC}(A)
and
\{a_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\partial A
is a sequence converging to
a
. Then
a_{k}\in\mathrm{SC}(A)
for large enough
k
.

Proof.

We have
\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon;A)>0
for any
\varepsilon>0
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. It suffices to show that
\mathfrak{m}_{a_{k}}(\varepsilon;A)>0
for large enough
k
.
Note that for any
k\geq 1
and
b\in A
,

\mbox{dist}\left(\frac{a_{k}+b}{2},\,\partial A\right)\geq\mbox{dist}\left(\frac{a+b}{2},\,\partial A\right)-(1/2)\|a_{k}-a\|.

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{a_{k}}(\varepsilon;A)

\displaystyle\geq\inf\left\{\mbox{dist}\left(\frac{a+b}{2},\,\partial A\right):\,b\in A,\,\|a_{k}-b\|\geq\varepsilon\right\}-(1/2)\|a_{k}-a\|,

\displaystyle\geq\inf\left\{\mbox{dist}\left(\frac{a+b}{2},\,\partial A\right):\,b\in A,\,\|a-b\|\geq\varepsilon-\|a_{k}-a\|\right\}-(1/2)\|a_{k}-a\|

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon-\|a_{k}-a\|;A)-(1/2)\|a_{k}-a\|.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
K\geq 1
such that for all
k\geq K
,
\|a_{k}-a\|\leq\min\{\varepsilon/2,\,\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon/2;A)\}
. This implies that for
k\geq K
,

\mathfrak{m}_{a_{k}}(\varepsilon;A)\geq\mathfrak{m}_{a}(\varepsilon/2)/2>0.

This proves the result.
∎

S.2
Additional Result on Ordinary Differential Equations

Proposition 7

(Solutions of Specific Volterra Equations of Second Kind)
.

Suppose
a:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an integrable function and
b:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a bounded (in operator norm) function, i.e.,
\|b(s)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq M<\infty
. Consider the integral equation

w(t)=\int_{0}^{t}a(s)ds+\int_{0}^{t}b(s)w(s)ds\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,1],\quad\mbox{with}\quad w(0)=0.

(E.2)

Then (
E.2
) has a unique solution and moreover,

w(t)=\Phi(t)\int_{0}^{t}(\Phi(s))^{-1}a(s)ds,

where
\Phi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the unique invertible solution to the

\Phi(t)=I+\int_{0}^{t}b(s)\Phi(s)ds,\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,1],\quad\mbox{with}\quad\Phi(0)=I\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

(E.3)

Proof.

The uniqueness result follows from Theorem 1.2.3 of
17
. (The assumption of continuous kernel (i.e.,
K\in C(D)
, in the notation of
17
is not needed when
\|b(s)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq M
uniformly over
s\in[0,1]
. In this case, we interpret the solution as the Carathéodory solution. Moreover, the proof extends to the vector-valued case.) Also, see Theorem 3.1 (Chapter 3) of
22
.

Note that the integral equation (
E.3
) is of the same type as (
E.2
) and hence from Theorem 1.2.3 of
17
, we get the existence and uniqueness of the solution for (
E.3
). Because
\Phi(\cdot)
solves (
E.3
), it is absolutely continuous and almost everywhere differentiable. This implies that for almost all
t\in[0,1]
,

\frac{d\mbox{det}(\Phi(t))}{dt}=(\mbox{tr}(b(t)))\mbox{det}(\Phi(t)).

(See, for example, Theorem 7.3 of
22
.) Hence,

\mbox{det}(\Phi(t))=\mbox{det}(\Phi(0))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mbox{tr}(b(s))ds\right)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mbox{tr}(b(s))ds\right),

which implies that
\Phi(t)
is invertible for all
t\in[0,1]
. (This equation for the determinant is called the Abel–Jacobi–Liouville identity.)
∎

S.3
Proofs of Results in Section
3

S.3.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Note that
t\mapsto X_{t}
is a differentiable map and hence, for any continuously differentiable function
\varphi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
(with bounded derivative), we have by the dominated convergence theorem that

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{t})]=\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi^{\prime}(X_{t})(X_{1}-X_{0})\right]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\prime}(X_{t})\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}|X_{t}]]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\prime}(X_{t})v(t,X_{t})].

Here, the second equality follows from the law of iterated expectations, and the third equality follows from the definition of the velocity field
v
.
Hence,
\mu_{t}
, the law of
X_{t}
, satisfies the continuity equation (Eq. (8.1.1) of
3
; see the equivalent formulation (8.1.4) there). Hence, by Theorem 8.2.1(ii) of
3
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{t})]=\int_{\Omega\times\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma_{x}(t))d\eta(x,\gamma_{x}),

where
\Gamma
is the collection of functions from
[0,1]
to
\Omega
, and
\eta(\cdot,\cdot)
is a probability measure concentrated on the set of pairs
(x,\gamma_{x})
such that
\gamma_{x}:[0,1]\to\Omega
is absolutely continuous and solves
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}(t)=v(t,\gamma_{x}(t))
for almost all
t\in(0,1)
with
\gamma_{x}(0)=x
. From the hypothesis of a unique solution to (
9
) for almost all
x
, we get that

\int_{\Omega\times\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma_{x}(t))d\eta(x,\gamma_{x})=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(\mathfrak{R}(t,x))d\mu_{0}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathfrak{R}(t,X_{0}))].

Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{t})]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathfrak{R}(t,X_{0}))]
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Hence,
\mathfrak{R}(t,X_{0})
has the same distribution as
X_{t}
for all
t\in[0,1].
This completes the proof.

S.3.2
Proof of Lemma
2

We have

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}-X_{0}\\
tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}&-I_{d}\\
tI_{d}&(1-t)I_{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{0}\end{pmatrix}

Here
I_{d}
is the identity matrix with
d\times d
order, and

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{0}\end{pmatrix}\sim N\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}\\
m_{0}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}\bigg)

Then,

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}-X_{0}\\
tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}\end{pmatrix}\sim N\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}-m_{0}\\
tm_{1}+(1-t)m_{0}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{0}&t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0}\\
t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0}&t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}\bigg)

and, therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(X_{1}-X_{0}\mid X_{t})

\displaystyle=m_{1}-m_{0}+(t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0})(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{-1}(X_{t}-m_{t}),

and hence

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+(t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0})(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{-1}(z-m_{t}).

(E.4)

S.3.3
Proof of Proposition
1

We first need a lemma.

Lemma 16
.

Let

X_{0}\sim N(m_{0},\Sigma_{0})
,

X_{1}\sim N(m_{1},\Sigma_{1})
,
and suppose that
X_{1}=AX_{0}+b
so that
A\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}=\Sigma_{1}
and
b=Am_{0}-m_{1}
. Also, suppose that
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{1}
are invertible and that
A
is positive semidefinite. Then,

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+(A-I_{d})(tA+(1-t)I_{d})^{-1}(z-m_{t}).

(E.5)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
2
. In this case, we have

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}-X_{0}\\
tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}&-I_{d}\\
tI_{d}&(1-t)I_{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{0}\end{pmatrix}

and

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{0}\end{pmatrix}\sim N\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}\\
m_{0}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1}&A\Sigma_{0}\\
\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}&\Sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}\bigg).

Then,

\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}-X_{0}\\
tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}\end{pmatrix}\sim N\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}-m_{0}\\
tm_{1}+(1-t)m_{0}\end{pmatrix},\widetilde{\Sigma}\bigg)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}+\Sigma_{0}&tA\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0}+(1-t)A\Sigma_{0}-t\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}\\
tA\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0}+(1-t)\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}-tA\Sigma_{0}&t^{2}A\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}+t(1-t)\left(A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}\right)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A\Sigma_{0}A^{\top}+\Sigma_{0}&(A-I_{d})\Sigma_{0}(tA^{\top}+(1-t)I_{d})\\
(tA+(1-t)I_{d})\Sigma_{0}(A^{\top}-I_{d})&(tA+(1-t)I_{d})\Sigma_{0}(tA^{\top}+(1-t)I_{d})\end{pmatrix}

Therefore,

\displaystyle v_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{1}-m_{0}+(A-I_{d})\Sigma_{0}(tA^{\top}+(1-t)I_{d})\left((tA+(1-t)I_{d})\Sigma_{0}(tA^{\top}+(1-t)I_{d})\right)^{-1}(z-m_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{1}-m_{0}+(A-I_{d})(tA+(1-t)I_{d})^{-1}(z-m_{t}).

In the last line, we have used the fact that
tA+(1-t)I_{d}
is an invertible matrix. In turn, this follows from the fact that
A
is invertible and hence positive definite. To see that
A
is invertible we argue by contradiction. If it was not, let
m<d
be the rank of
A
. Then,
d=rank(\Sigma_{1})=rank(A\Sigma_{0}A^{T})\leq\min\{d,m\}=m
which is impossible.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Let’s compute the map
R_{1}(x)
resulting from the first iteration. We have, by Lemma
2
that

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+(t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0})(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{-1}(z-m_{t}).

(E.6)

Denote
C\equiv\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}
. Then, we can write

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}(tC-(1-t)I_{d})(t^{2}C+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})^{-1}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(z-m_{t}).

(E.7)

Write the eigendecomposition of
C
as
C=P\Lambda P^{\top}
. Then,

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}P(t\Lambda-(1-t)I_{d})(t^{2}\Lambda+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})^{-1}P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(z-m_{t}).

(E.8)

Denote by
z_{t}
the solution to (
2
) with velocity given by (
E.8
) and initial condition
z_{0}=x
. Define the variable
y_{t}=P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}z_{t}
. This variable satisfies the ODE

\displaystyle dy_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}dz_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(m_{1}-m_{0})+(t\Lambda-(1-t)I_{d})(t^{2}\Lambda+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})^{-1}P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(z-m_{t})\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(m_{1}-m_{0})+(t\Lambda-(1-t)I_{d})(t^{2}\Lambda+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})^{-1}(y-P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}m_{t})\right)dt.

With
y_{0}=P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}z_{0}=P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}x.
This ODE is of the form
dy_{t}=(a_{t}+p_{t}y_{t})dt
where
a_{t}
is a vector and
p_{t}
is a diagonal matrix. We can solve this equation component-wise. Using, for each coordinate, the integrating factor
g_{t}=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}-D_{s}ds\right)
we have the relation

\frac{d}{dt}(y_{t}g_{t})=a_{t}g_{t}
. By integrating both sides between 0 and t we obtain

y_{t}=g_{t}^{-1}\left(y_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}a_{s}g_{s}ds\right).

In particular, we observe that each coordinate of
Y_{t}
is an affine function of the initial condition
Y_{0}
(and hence,
X
). Therefore, for some vector
b
,

y_{1}=b+g_{1}^{-1}y_{0},

where

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{1}(t\Lambda-(1-t)I_{d})(t^{2}\Lambda+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})^{-1}ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\log(t^{2}\Lambda+(1-t)^{2}I_{d})\Big\rvert_{0}^{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda\right)=\sqrt{\Lambda}^{-1}.

We have found that for some vector
b
,

y_{1}=\sqrt{\Lambda}y_{0}+b
. Expressing the above in terms of the original variables
z
,

P^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}z_{1}=\sqrt{\Lambda}P^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}z_{0}+b.

Equivalently, since
R_{1}(x)=z_{1}
,

\displaystyle R_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}P\sqrt{\Lambda}P^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}x+b^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}C^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}x+b^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}\left(\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}x+b^{\prime},

where
b^{\prime}
is another constant vector, whose value we can compute explicitly. Indeed, since
R_{1}(X_{0})\sim N\left(m_{1},\Sigma_{1}\right)
where
X_{0}\sim N\left(m_{0},\Sigma_{0}\right)
we can simply express

R_{1}(x)=m_{1}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}\left(\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}\left(x-m_{0}\right).

We have found the solution to the first iteration. It remains to show that the second iteration leaves this initial iteration unchanged. The key fact is that after one iteration the initial coupling between
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
is deterministic (instead of the independence coupling), so we can use Lemma
16
. We can use a similar argument to solve the resulting ODE with velocity field

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+(A-I_{d})(tA+(1-t)I_{d})^{-1}(z-m_{t}),

where
A=\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}C\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}
and
C=\left(\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}
is a symmetric matrix. Therefore,

v_{t}(z)=m_{1}-m_{0}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}(C-I_{d})(tC+(1-t)I_{d})^{-1}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(z-m_{t}).

We can use the same diagonalization argument as before to decouple the system and solve each coordinate separately. Expressing
C=P\Lambda P^{t}
, this time

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{1}(\Lambda-I_{d})(t\Lambda+(1-t)I_{d})^{-1}ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\log(t\Lambda+(1-t)I_{d})\Big\rvert_{0}^{1}\right)=\exp\left(-\Lambda\right)=\Lambda^{-1}.

Consequently, calling
R_{2}(X)
the second iteration of the rectified map

\displaystyle R_{2}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{1}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}P\Lambda P^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\left(X-m_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{1}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}C\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\left(X-m_{0}\right)=R_{1}(X).

∎

S.3.4
Proof of Lemma
3

When
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are mixtures of Gaussians, then we have,

\displaystyle v_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int y\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{0}}\pi_{0}^{i}p^{i}_{0}(z-ty)\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{I_{1}}\pi^{j}_{1}p_{1}^{j}(z+(1-t)y)\bigg)dy}{\int\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{0}}\pi^{i}_{0}p^{i}_{0}(z-ty)\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{I_{1}}\pi^{j}_{1}p_{1}^{j}(z+(1-t)y)\bigg)dy}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i,j}^{I_{0},I_{1}}\pi^{i}_{0}\pi_{1}^{j}\int yp^{i}_{0}(z-ty)p^{j}_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy}{\sum_{i,j}^{I_{0},I_{1}}\pi^{i}_{0}\pi^{j}_{1}\int p^{i}_{0}(z-ty)p^{j}_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i,j}^{I_{0},I_{1}}\pi^{i}_{0}\pi^{j}_{1}v^{i,j}_{t}(z)\tau^{i,j}_{t}(z)}{\sum_{i,j}^{I_{0},I_{1}}\pi_{0,i}\pi_{1,j}\tau^{i,j}_{t}(z)},

where

\displaystyle v^{i,j}_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int yp^{i}_{0}(z-ty)p^{j}_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy}{\int p^{i}_{0}(z-ty)p^{j}_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy}

\displaystyle\tau^{i,j}_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\int p^{i}_{0}(z-ty)p^{j}_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy

and

p^{i}_{0}\sim N(\mu^{i}_{0},\Sigma^{i}_{0}),\quad\quad p^{j}_{1}\sim N(\mu^{j}_{1},\Sigma^{j}_{1}).

Each of the
v^{i,j}_{t}(z)
is the velocity field between pairs of mixture components
X_{0}^{i}\sim N(\mu^{i}_{0},\Sigma^{i}_{0})
and
X_{1}^{j}\sim N(\mu^{j}_{1},\Sigma^{j}_{1})
so that by Equation (
10
)

v^{i,j}_{t}(z)=\mu^{j}_{1}-\mu^{i}_{0}+(t\Sigma^{j}_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma^{i}_{0})(t^{2}\Sigma^{j}_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{0})^{-1}(z-(t\mu_{1}^{j}-(1-t)\mu^{i}_{0}).

It remains to compute
\tau^{i,j}(z,t)
. By simplicity remove
i,j
and focus on the integral

\tau^{i,j}(z,t)=\int p_{0}(z-ty)p_{1}(z+(1-t)y)dy.

Consider the change of variables
x=z+(1-t)y
(the case
t=1
is trivial). Then, we write

\tau_{i,j}(z,t)=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{d}}\int p_{0}\left(\frac{1}{1-t}-\frac{t}{1-t}x\right)p_{1}(x)dx.

Define the quantities
a=\frac{z}{1-t}
and
b=\frac{t}{1-t}
so that

\tau^{i,j}_{t}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{d}}\int p_{0}(a-bx)p_{1}(x)dx.

We can interpret the above integral as the marginal likelihood of
a
in the model

X=bX_{1}+X_{0}
where
X_{0}\perp X_{1}
and
X_{0i}\sim N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. The random variable
X
then distributes

X\sim N\left(b\mu_{1}+\mu_{0},b^{2}\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{0}\right)=N\left(\frac{1}{1-t}\left(t\mu_{1}+(1-t)\mu_{0}\right),\frac{1}{(1-t)^{2}}\left(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)\right).

If
p_{X}
denotes the density of
X
and
\mu_{t}=t\mu_{0}+(1-t)\mu_{1},\Sigma_{t}=t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\tau^{i,j}_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{d}}p_{X}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{d}}p_{X}\left(\frac{z}{1-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{d}}\frac{1}{\det\left(2\pi\frac{\Sigma_{t}}{(1-t)^{2}}\right)^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{1-t}\left(z-\mu_{t}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{\Sigma_{t}}{(1-t)^{2}}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{1-t}\left(z-\mu_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{t}\right)^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(z-\mu_{t}\right)^{\top}\Sigma_{t}^{-1}\left(z-\mu_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=N\left(z;\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}\right).

S.3.5
Proof of Proposition
2

Observe that

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{t}(z)-f_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta\{\widehat{p}_{0}(z-t\delta)-p_{0}(z-t\delta)\}\widehat{p}_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta p_{0}(z-t\delta)\{\widehat{p}_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)-p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)\}d\delta

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}.

We start with
\mathbf{II}
. By Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{II}\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\delta\|^{2}p_{0}^{2}(z-t\delta)d\delta\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{p}_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)-p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)|^{2}d\delta\right)

\displaystyle=t^{-d/2}(1-t)^{-d/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|z-u\|^{2}p_{0}^{2}(u)du\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{p}_{1}(u)-p_{1}(u)|^{2}du\right)^{1/2}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{I}\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\delta\|^{2}\widehat{p}_{1}^{2}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{p}_{0}(z-t\delta)-p_{0}(z-t\delta)|^{2}d\delta\right)

\displaystyle=(1-t)^{-d/2}t^{-d/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|z-u\|^{2}\widehat{p}_{1}^{2}(u)du\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{p}_{0}(u)-p_{0}(u)|^{2}du\right)^{1/2}.

To bound the first integral, we note that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|z-u\|^{2}\widehat{p}_{1}^{2}(u)du\leq\|\widehat{p}_{1}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|z-u\|^{2}\widehat{p}_{1}(u)du\leq\|\widehat{p}_{1}\|_{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|z-u\|^{2}p_{1}(u)du+\bar{\zeta}_{2}(\widehat{p}_{1},p_{1})\right).

Hence,

\|\widehat{f}_{t}(z)-f_{t}(z)\|\leq\frac{\|\widehat{p}_{1}-p_{1}\|_{2}\|p_{0}\|_{\infty}(\mathbb{E}\|z-X_{0}\|^{2})^{1/2}+\|\widehat{p}_{0}-p_{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{p}_{1}\|_{\infty}(\mathbb{E}\|z-X_{1}\|^{2}+\bar{\zeta}_{2}(\widehat{p}_{1},p_{1}))^{1/2}}{t^{d/2}(1-t)^{d/2}}.

Following the same strategy for
\widehat{p}_{t}(\cdot)
yields

|\widehat{p}_{t}(z)-p_{t}(z)|\leq\frac{\|p_{0}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{p}_{1}-p_{1}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{p}_{1}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{p}_{0}-p_{0}\|_{2}}{t^{d/2}(1-t)^{d/2}}.

S.3.6
Proof of Theorem
2

By the chain rule,
the efficient influence function is

\varphi=\frac{\varphi_{N}}{p_{t}}-v_{t}(z)\frac{\varphi_{D}}{p_{t}}

where

\varphi_{N}
is the efficient influence function of the numerator and

\varphi_{D}
is the efficient influence function of the denominator.
There are two distributions so

\varphi=\frac{\varphi_{N0}+\varphi_{N1}}{p_{t}(z)}-v_{t}(z)\frac{\varphi_{D0}+\varphi_{D1}}{p_{t}(z)}=\frac{\varphi_{N0}}{p_{t}(z)}-v_{t}(z)\frac{\varphi_{D0}}{p_{t}(z)}+\frac{\varphi_{N1}}{p_{t}(z)}-v_{t}(z)\frac{\varphi_{D1}}{p_{t}(z)}\equiv\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{1}.

The equations for these functions
follows by computing the Gateaux derivative.
Now consider the limiting distribution.
First consider
\psi_{D}
.
The von Mises expansion is

\displaystyle\psi_{D}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\psi}_{pi}+\int\varphi_{0}(x,\widehat{p}_{1})d\mu_{0}+\int\varphi_{1}(x,\widehat{p}_{0})d\mu_{1}+R_{n}

where
R_{n}
is a second order remainder.
Ignoring
R
we have,
and writing
\psi_{D}
as
\psi
for simplicity,

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}-\psi

\displaystyle=\psi_{pi}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},\widehat{p}_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},\widehat{p}_{0})-\psi(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle=\psi_{pi}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},\widehat{p}_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},\widehat{p}_{0})-\psi_{pi}-\int\varphi_{0}(x,\widehat{p}_{1})d\mu_{0}-\int\varphi_{1}(x,\widehat{p}_{0})d\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},\widehat{p}_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},\widehat{p}_{0})-\int\varphi_{0}(x,\widehat{p}_{1})d\mu_{0}-\int\varphi_{1}(x,\widehat{p}_{0})d\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},p_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},p_{0})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}(\varphi(X_{i},\widehat{p}_{1})-\varphi(X_{i},p_{1}))+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}(\varphi(Y_{i},\widehat{p}_{0})-\varphi(Y_{i},p_{0}))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -\int(\varphi_{0}(x,\widehat{p}_{1})-\varphi_{0}(x,p_{1}))d\mu_{0}-\int(\varphi_{1}(x,\widehat{p}_{0})-\varphi_{1}(x,p_{0}))d\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},p_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},p_{0})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{G}_{n}(\varphi_{0}(X,\widehat{p}_{1})-\varphi_{0}(X,p_{1}))+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{G}_{n}(\varphi_{1}(Y,\widehat{p}_{0})-\varphi_{1}(Y,p_{0}))

where
\mathbb{G}_{n}(f(X_{i}))=(1/\sqrt{n})(n^{-1}\sum_{i}f(X_{i})-\int f(x)dP(x))

is the empirical process.
As the two empirical process terms are
o_{p}(1)
,

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\psi}-\psi)=\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(X_{i},p_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varphi(Y_{i},p_{0})\right)+o_{p}(1).

A similar argument applies to

\psi_{N}
and then
the limit of the ratio followed by the delta method.

S.3.7
Proof of Lemma
4

The asymptotic variance is

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{2}(X_{0})]+\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{1}^{2}(X_{1})]
.
Now

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{2}(X_{0})]

\displaystyle=\int\varphi_{0}^{2}(x,p_{1})d\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{t}^{2}(z)}\int\varphi_{N0}^{2}(x,p_{1})p_{0}(x)dx+\frac{v_{t}^{2}(z)}{p_{t}^{2}(z)}\int\varphi_{D0}^{2}(x,p_{1})p_{0}(x)dx-2\frac{v_{t}(z)}{p_{t}^{2}(z)}\int\varphi_{D0}(x,p_{1})\varphi_{N0}(x,p_{1})p_{0}(x)dx.

The first term is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{t}^{2}(z)}\int\frac{1}{t^{2d}}

\displaystyle\int\left(\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{2}p_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{z-x}{t}+x\right)p_{0}(x)dx+f_{t}^{2}(z)-\frac{2f_{t}(z)}{t^{d}}\int\left(\frac{z-x}{t}\right)p_{1}\left(\frac{z-x}{t}+x\right)p_{0}(x)dx

\displaystyle\equiv A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}.

Next,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\left(\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\left(\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{T}p_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{z-x}{t}+x\right)p_{0}(x)dx}{(\int p_{0}(z-tx)p_{1}(z+(1-t)x)dx)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t^{d}}\frac{\int xx^{T}p_{0}(z-tx)p_{1}^{2}(z+(1-t)x)dx}{(\int p_{0}(z-tx)p_{1}(z+(1-t)x)dx)^{2}}.

Hence

\lim_{t\to 0}t^{d}A_{1}=\frac{1}{p_{0}(z)}\int\delta^{2}p_{1}^{2}(z+x)dx

so that

A_{1}\asymp 1/t^{d}
.
Similar calculations apply to the other terms.

S.4
Proofs of Results in Section
4

S.4.1
Proof of Theorem
3

1.

The first part of the theorem follows from Theorem 1 of
41
; Take
d=\mbox{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
and
m(s)=B
for all
s\in[0,1]
. Although Theorem 1 of
41
is written for
d=1
, it still applies for
d>1
.

2.

The second part of the theorem follows from the first one using the fact that under
(C4)
, no solution of (
24
) can escape to
\infty
. To see this, note that for any solution
y(\cdot)
of (
24
) defined on
[0,T]
, we have

\|y(t)\|\leq\|x\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|F(s,y(s))\|ds\leq\|x\|+B\int_{0}^{t}(1+\|y(s)\|)ds.

Set
R(t)=\|x\|+B\int_{0}^{t}(1+\|y(s)\|)ds
, so that
\|y(t)\|\leq R(t)
. Observe that

R^{\prime}(t)=B(1+\|y(t)\|)\leq B(1+R(t))\quad\Rightarrow\quad\frac{R^{\prime}(t)}{1+R(t)}\leq B.

Integrating both sides implies that

\ln\left(\frac{1+R(t)}{1+R(0)}\right)\leq Bt\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|y(t)\|\leq R(t)\leq(1+\|x\|)e^{Bt}-1,\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Now applying part 1 of the theorem with
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the upper bound on
\|F(t,x)\|
as
(1+\|x\|)Be^{B}
, we obtain a solution defined on
[0,1]
.

3.

The proof of the third part follows the same logic as the first. However, let us first address a trivial case. If
\mbox{dist}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{S})\geq B
, then one can simply apply part 1 of the theorem to prove the existence of a solution on
[0,1]
that lies entirely in
\mathcal{S}
. Under assumption
(C3)
, any solution to (
24
) satisfies
\|y(t)-x\|\leq Bt
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Hence, in the definition of (
24
), one can without loss of generality define
F
on
[0,1]\times\mathcal{B}(x,B)\cap\mathcal{S}
. Additionally, for all
x\in\mathcal{B}(x,B)\cap\mathcal{S}
,
T_{\mathcal{S}}(x)=T_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{B}(x,B)}(x)
. Therefore, for the remaining part of the proof, we take
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{B}(x,B)
, which is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The proof proceeds as follows. We construct a uniformly equicontinuous sequence of functions
\{x_{m}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{S}\}_{m\geq 1}
, show that they approximately solve the ODE (
23
), and obtain a subsequential limit which solves the ODE. For any
x\in\mathcal{S}
, define the intermediate cone of
\mathcal{S}
at
x
as

\displaystyle T_{\mathcal{S}}^{\flat}(x)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\mbox{dist}(x+hv,\mathcal{S})}{h}=0\right\}.

See Definition 4.1.5 of
4
.
Proposition 4.2.1 of
4
implies that
T_{\mathcal{S}}(x)=T^{\flat}_{\mathcal{S}}(x)
under convexity of
\mathcal{S}
assumed in
(V1)
. From Lemma 3 of
92
, it follows that for
\Delta:\mathcal{S}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

\Delta(y,h):=\sup_{v\in T_{\mathcal{S}}(y)\cap\mathcal{B}_{0}}\,\frac{1}{h}\mbox{dist}(y+hv,\,\mathcal{S}),

satisfies
\lim_{h\to 0}\Delta(y,h)=0
for all
y\in\mathcal{S}
. (The convergence need not be uniform in
y
.) Hence, for each
y\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists
h_{y}\in(0,1/m)
(for any
m\geq 1
) such that

\Delta(y,h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{3m}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad y\in\mathcal{S}.

(E.9)

Define the open set

U(y)=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,\mbox{dist}(x+h_{y}F(t,y),\mathcal{S})<\mbox{dist}(y+h_{y}F(t,y))+\frac{h_{y}}{3m}\right\},\;y\in\mathcal{S}.

Clearly,
y\in U(y)
and hence, there exists a
\delta_{y}\in(0,1/m)
such that
\mathcal{B}(y,\delta_{y})\subseteq U(y)
. Moreover,

\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\{y\}\subseteq\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{B}(y,\delta_{y}).

Compactness of
\mathcal{S}
(from
(V1)
) implies that we can find a finite set
\mathcal{Y}
such that

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{B}(y,\delta_{y}).

(This is because every cover has a finite sub-cover for compact sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.) Set
h_{0}=\min\{h_{y}:\,y\in\mathcal{Y}\}\in(0,1/m)
. Note that the cardinality of
\mathcal{Y}
depends on
m
, in general.

Define the sequence of approximate solutions, recursively, as follows. Set
x_{m}(0)=x
for all
m\geq 1
. Since
x\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists
y_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
such that
x\in\mathcal{B}(y_{1},\delta_{y_{1}})
. Set

z_{1}(s):=\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(x+h_{y_{1}}F(s,y_{1}))\quad\mbox{for all}\quad s\in[0,1],

and define
t_{1}=h_{y_{1}}
,
v_{0}(s)=(z_{1}(s)-x)/h_{y}
,

x_{m}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v_{0}(s)ds\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\in[0,t_{1}].

By convexity of
\mathcal{S}
,

x_{m}(t)=x\left(1-\frac{t}{t_{1}}\right)+\frac{t}{t_{1}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}v_{0}(s)ds\in\mathcal{S}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,t_{1}].

Having defined
t_{0}=0,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
along with
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-1}
, we get
x_{m}(t_{k})\in\mathcal{S}
. Find
y_{k+1}\in\mathcal{Y}
such that
x_{m}(t_{k})\in\mathcal{B}(y_{k+1},\delta_{y_{k+1}})
. Set

z_{k+1}(s):=\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{m}(t_{k})+h_{y_{k+1}}F(s,y_{k+1}))\quad\mbox{for all}\quad s\in[0,1].

Define
t_{k+1}=t_{k}+h_{y_{k+1}}
,

v_{k}(s)=\frac{z_{k+1}(s)-x_{m}(t_{k})}{h_{y_{k+1}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad x_{m}(t)=x_{m}(t_{k})+\int_{t_{k}}^{t}v_{k}(s)ds\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}].

Continue this process until
t_{k}
reaches
1
. There are only finitely many steps (for a fixed
m\geq 1
) because
h_{0}>0
. Let
k_{m}\geq 1
be such that
t_{k_{m}}>1
. Then redefine
t_{k_{m}}=1
.

Setting

e_{k}(t)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if }t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}),\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

we observe

x_{m}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}e_{k}(s)v_{k}(s)ds.

(E.10)

Therefore,
x_{m}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{S}
is absolutely continuous. Moreover, for
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1})
,

\displaystyle\|x_{m}^{\prime}(t)-F(t,y_{k+1})\|

\displaystyle=\left\|v_{k}(t)-F(t,y_{k+1})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{z_{k+1}(t)-x_{m}(t_{k})}{h_{y_{k+1}}}-F(t,y_{k+1})\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h_{y_{k+1}}}\|z_{k+1}(t)-x_{m}(t_{k})-h_{y_{k+1}}F(t,y_{k+1})\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h_{y_{k+1}}}\mbox{dist}\left(x_{m}(t_{k})+h_{y_{k+1}}F(t,y_{k+1}),\mathcal{S}\right).

Recall now that
x_{m}(t_{k})\in\mathcal{B}(y_{k+1},\delta_{y_{k+1}})\subseteq U(y_{k+1})
, and therefore, from the definition of
U(y)
, we conclude

\mbox{dist}(x_{m}(t_{k})+h_{y_{k+1}}F(t,y_{k+1}),\,\mathcal{S})<\mbox{dist}(y_{k+1}+h_{y_{k+1}}F(t,y_{k+1}),\,\mathcal{S})+\frac{h_{y_{k+1}}}{3m}.

Hence, for
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1})
, we conclude

\|x_{m}^{\prime}(t)-F(t,y_{k+1})\|\leq\Delta(y_{k+1},h_{y_{k+1}})+\frac{1}{3m}.

Recall, from (
E.9
),
\Delta(y,h_{y})\leq 1/(3m)
for all
y
. Therefore,

\|x_{m}^{\prime}(t)-F(t,y_{k+1})\|=\|v_{k}(t)-F(t,y_{k+1})\|\leq\frac{2}{3m}\quad\mbox{ for all }t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}).

(E.11)

In particular,
\|x_{m}^{\prime}(t)\|\leq B+1
for
m\geq 1
, which implies uniform equicontinuity and also boundedness of the sequence of functions
\{x_{m}(\cdot)\}_{m\geq 1}
. Hence, by the Arzela-Ascoli theorem, we can choose a uniformly convergent subsequence (denoted again by
\{x_{m}(\cdot)\}_{m\geq 1}
) with its limit denoted by
x^{*}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{S}
. Now we need to show that
x^{*}
satisfies the ODE (
23
) almost everywhere
t\in[0,1]
. We will show that

\left\|x_{m}(t)-x-\int_{0}^{t}F(s,x_{m}(s))ds\right\|\to 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad m\to\infty.

(E.12)

From dominated convergence theorem and uniform convergence of
x_{m}
to
x^{*}
, this yields
\|x^{*}(t)-x-\int_{0}^{t}F(s,x^{*}(s))ds\|=0
.
For this, fix
t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
, and note that

\displaystyle\|x_{m}(t)-y_{k+1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{m}(t)-x_{m}(t_{k})\|+\|x_{m}(t_{k})-y_{k+1}\|

\displaystyle\leq h_{y_{k+1}}\sup_{s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}]}\|x_{m}^{\prime}(s)\|+\delta_{y_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\leq h_{y_{k+1}}\left(B+1\right)+\frac{1}{m}\leq\frac{B+2}{m}.

This can equivalently be written as

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left\|x_{m}(s)-\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}y_{k+1}e_{k}(s)\right\|\leq\frac{B+2}{m}.

(E.13)

This implies

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}e_{k}(s)v_{k}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}F(s,x_{m}(s))ds\right\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}e_{k}(s)F(s,y_{k+1})ds-\int_{0}^{t}F(s,x_{m}(s))ds\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}e_{k}(s)\{v_{k}(s)-y_{k+1}\}\right\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\{F\left(s,\sum_{k=0}^{k_{m}}e_{k}(s)y_{k+1}\right)-F(s,x_{m}(s))\}ds\right\|+\frac{2}{3m}

The last inequality follows from (
E.11
). Because of (
E.13
) and the fact that continuity of
F
on the compact set
\mathcal{S}
implies uniform continuity, we get that the left-hand side converges to zero. Therefore, (
E.12
) holds, which in turn implies
x^{*}
is a solution to the ODE and belongs to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.4.2
Proof of Theorem
4

Suppose
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are any two solutions to the ODE (
23
). This implies that

y_{j}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}F(s,y_{j}(s))ds.

From assumption
(C3)
, we get
\|y_{j}(t)-x\|\leq Bt
and hence
y_{j}(t)\in\mathcal{S}
for all
t\leq\mbox{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal{S})/B
. In other words, all the solutions to the ODE lie in
\mathcal{S}
up to time
T
.

To prove the uniqueness, observe that

y_{1}(t)-y_{2}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\{F(s,y_{1}(s))-F(s,y_{2}(s))\}ds.

From assumption
(C3)
, we have the simple upper bound

\|y_{1}(t)-y_{2}(t)\|\leq 2Bt\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,T].

Fix any
\gamma\in(0,t)
. From assumption
(W1)
, we get

\|y_{1}(t)-y_{2}(t)\|\leq\int_{0}^{\gamma}a(s)\kappa(2Bs)ds+\int_{\gamma}^{t}a(s)\kappa(\|y_{1}(s)-y_{2}(s)\|)ds.

Hence,
\Delta(s)=\|y_{1}(s)-y_{2}(s)\|
satisfies the inequality

\Delta(t)\leq\int_{0}^{\gamma}a(s)\kappa(2Bs)ds+\int_{\gamma}^{t}a(s)\kappa(\Delta(s))ds.

Applying Bihari’s generalization
(
36
, Theorem 4)
of Grönwall’s inequality
(
63
, Lemma 1.3.8)
, we conclude

\Delta(t)\leq\Psi^{-1}\left(\Psi\left(\int_{0}^{\gamma}a(s)\kappa(2Bs)\right)+\int_{\gamma}^{t}a(s)ds\right).

Because
\gamma>0
is arbitrary, from assumption
(W1)
, we get
\Delta(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
. The proof of part 2 under
(C4)
follows the same steps as in Theorem
3
.

To prove the uniqueness under
(V1)
, define
G:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

G(t,y)=G(t,\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y)).

Because
\mathcal{S}
is a compact convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the projection of
y
onto
\mathcal{S}
is uniquely defined and hence,
G(\cdot,\cdot)
is a well-defined function. It is also clear that
\|G(t,y)\|\leq B
from
(C3)
. Additionally, for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\|G(t,y)-G(t,y^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle=\|F(t,\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y))-F(t,\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y^{\prime}))\|
