\displaystyle\leq a(t)\kappa(\|\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y)-\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y^{\prime})\|)

\displaystyle\leq a(t)\kappa(\|y-y^{\prime}\|),

because projection is a contraction. Hence,
G(\cdot,\cdot)
verifies conditions
(C3)
and
(W1)
with the domain for the second argument being
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e, with
\mathcal{S}
in assumptions
(C3)
and
(W1)
being
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). Hence, applying the first part (of Theorem
4
), we find that there exists a unique solution to the ODE
y^{*}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

y^{*}(0)=x\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{dy^{*}(t)}{dt}=F(t,\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(y^{*}(t)))\mbox{ almost everywhere }t.

(E.14)

But Theorem
3
already proves the existence of a solution
\widetilde{y}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{S}
such that

\widetilde{y}(0)=x\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{d\widetilde{y}(t)}{dt}=F(t,\widetilde{y}(t))\mbox{ almost everywhere }t.

Because
\widetilde{y}(t)\in\mathcal{S}
and hence,
\widetilde{y}(t)=\mbox{Proj}_{\mathcal{S}}(\widetilde{y}(t))
. This implies that
\widetilde{y}(\cdot)
satisfies (
E.14
), which makes it the unique solution to (
23
).

S.4.3
Proof of Theorem
5

Theorem
3
implies the existence of
T\in(0,1]
such that
w:[0,T]\to\mathcal{S}
solves (
26
) for
t\in[0,T]
.
Because
y(\cdot)
and
w(\cdot)
are respectively solutions to (
24
) and (
26
), we get

\displaystyle y(t)-w(t)

\displaystyle=x-x^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}\{F(s,y(s))-G(s,w(s))\}ds

\displaystyle=x-x^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}\{F(s,y(s))-F(s,w(s))\}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\{F(s,w(s))-G(s,w(s))\}ds.

From assumption
(C3)
(assumed for both
F
and
G
), we conclude that

\|y(t)-w(t)\|\leq\|x-x^{\prime}\|+2Bt,\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[0,T].

This proves (
28
) for
t\in[0,\delta]
. For
t\in[\delta,T]
,

\displaystyle\|y(t)-w(t)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|x-x^{\prime}\|+\left\|\int_{0}^{\delta}\{F(s,y(s))-G(s,w(s))\}ds\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\int_{\delta}^{t}\{F(s,w(s))-G(s,w(s))\}ds\right\|+\left\|\int_{\delta}^{t}\{F(s,y(s))-F(s,w(s))\}ds\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t)+\int_{\delta}^{t}\|F(s,y(s))-F(s,w(s))\|ds

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t)+\int_{\delta}^{t}a(s)\kappa(\|y(s)-w(s)\|)ds.

This is a differential inequality, with a drift of
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t)
. To control
\Delta(s)=\|y(s)-w(s)\|
, define

V(t)=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t)+\int_{\delta}^{t}a(s)\kappa(\Delta(s))ds.

We have
\Delta(s)\leq V(s)
, which implies

\frac{d}{ds}V(s)=\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(s)+a(s)\kappa(\Delta(s))\leq\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(s)+a(s)\kappa(V(s)).

Dividing both sides by
\kappa(V(s))
, we get

\frac{V^{\prime}(s)}{\kappa(V(s))}\leq\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\delta}^{\prime}(s)}{\kappa(V(s))}+a(s).

Because
\kappa(\cdot)
is non-decreasing and by definition,
V(s)\geq\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(s)
, we get

\frac{V^{\prime}(s)}{\kappa(V(s))}\leq\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\delta}^{\prime}(s)}{\kappa(\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(s))}+a(s).

Integrating both sides over
s\in[\delta,t]
, we get

\Psi(V(t))-\Psi(V(\delta))\leq\Psi(\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t))-\Psi(\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(\delta))+\int_{\delta}^{t}a(s)ds.

Equivalently, (because
V(\delta)=\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(\delta)
), we obtain

V(t)\leq\Psi^{-1}\left(\Psi(\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(t))+\int_{\delta}^{t}a(s)ds\right).

Hence, the first part of the result follows. The second part follows from the same proof by noting that under
(V1)
any solution
w(\cdot)
of (
26
) can be extended on
[0,1]
to lie in
\mathcal{S}
.

S.5
Proofs of results in Section
5

S.5.1
Proof of Lemma
5

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Let’s first prove bounds on the derivatives of
p_{j}
under (
U2
).
A Taylor series expansion around
\widetilde{x}_{j}
, using that the second derivatives of
\phi
are bounded, yields that for some constant
\mathfrak{C}
,

|\phi_{j}(x)-\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x}_{j})|=|\phi_{j}(x)-\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x}_{j})-\nabla\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x}_{j})^{\top}(x-\widetilde{x}_{j})|\leq\mathfrak{C}\|x-\widetilde{x}_{j}\|^{2}.

This implies
|\phi_{j}(x)|\leq c_{0}+c_{2}\|x\|^{2}
for some constants
c_{l}<\infty
. Moreover, for some constant
\mathfrak{C}
,

\left|\frac{\partial\phi_{j}(x)}{\partial x_{k}}-\frac{\partial\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x}_{j})}{\partial x_{k}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{C}\|x-\widetilde{x}_{j}\|.

This implies
|\partial\phi_{j}(x)/\partial x_{k}|\leq c_{0}+c_{\beta}\|x\|
for some constants
c_{0},c_{1}<\infty
.
We conclude that
\phi_{j}(\cdot)
and its derivative grow like quadratic and linear functions, respectively. For higher-order derivatives, we can generalize the above bounds, and under (
U2
) we can state that for any
k=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
with
2\leq\|k\|_{1}\leq\lceil\beta\rceil-1
, there exist constants
c_{l,k}
for
0\leq l\leq\lceil\beta\rceil-\|k\|_{1}-1
such that

\left|\frac{\partial^{\|k\|_{1}}}{\partial x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{k_{d}}}\phi_{j}(x)\right|\leq\sum_{l=0}^{\lceil\beta\rceil-\|k\|_{1}-1}c_{l,k}\|x\|^{l}+c_{\beta,k}\|x\|^{\beta-\|k\|_{1}}.

This follows by a Taylor series expansion of
\partial^{\|k\|_{1}}\phi_{j}(x)/\partial x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{k_{d}}
around
x=0
. This inequality implies that any
l
-th order partial derivative is bounded by a polynomial of degree at most
\max\{2,\beta-l\}
for any
0\leq l\leq\lceil\beta\rceil-1
. By the multivariate Faá di Bruno’s formula, we get that any
l
-th order partial derivative of
p_{j}(x)
has the following form:

\partial_{l}p_{j}(x)=p_{j}(x)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\geq 0,\\
\sum_{u=1}^{l}uw_{u}=l\end{subarray}}c_{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{l}}\prod_{u=1}^{l}(\partial_{u}\phi_{j}(x))^{w_{l}},

(E.15)

where
c_{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{l}}
are some combinatorial constants and
\partial_{u}\phi_{j}(x)
is a short-hand notation for some
u
-th order derivative of
\phi_{j}(x)
;
(\partial_{u}\phi_{j}(x))^{w_{l}}
here can refer to product of
w_{l}
many potentially different
u
-th order partial derivatives of
\phi_{j}(x)
. Because of the polynomial bounds on the derivatives of
\phi_{j}(\cdot)
, we get (
29
). This also implies the boundedness of the derivatives of
p_{j}
up to the order of
\lceil\beta\rceil-1
. It then only remains to prove that for
2\leq l=\lceil\beta\rceil-1

\lvert\partial_{l}p_{j}(x)-\partial_{l}p_{j}(y)\rvert\leq\mathfrak{C}|x-y|^{\beta-l}.

To do so, we show that each of the terms in (
E.15
) satisfies this bound. Reducing, by simplicity, to the one-dimensional case, note each of these terms is of the form
p_{j}(x)\partial_{u}\phi(x)^{\omega}_{l}
where
1\leq u\leq l
and
w_{l}
is an integer with
w_{l}=1
if
u=l=\lceil\beta\rceil-1
. We first analyze this case

\displaystyle\lvert p_{j}(x)\partial_{l}\phi(x)-p_{j}(y)\partial_{l}\phi(y)\rvert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lvert p_{j}(x)\left(\partial_{l}\phi(x)-\partial_{l}\phi(y)\right)+\partial_{l}\phi(y)\left(p_{j}(x)-p_{j}(y)\right)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta-l}+\lvert\partial_{l}\phi(y)\rvert\lvert p_{j}(x)-p_{j}(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}\left(\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta-l}+\lvert x-y\rvert\right).

In the second line, we used the Hölder condition for
\phi
, and in the third line, we used that
\partial_{l}\phi
is bounded since
l\geq 2
and that
p_{j}(x)
is satisfies a Lipschitz condition since its gradient is bounded, as a consequence of (
29
) and log-concavity. To conclude, we divide the analysis into
\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1
and
\lvert x-y\rvert\geq 1
. If
\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1
then
\lvert x-y\rvert\leq\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta-l}
so

\lvert p_{j}(x)\partial_{l}\phi(x)-p_{j}(y)\partial_{l}\phi(y)\rvert\leq\mathfrak{C}\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta-l}
, by the above bounds. If
\lvert x-y\rvert\geq 1
we use the fact that
x\to p_{j}(x)\partial_{l}\phi(x)
is a bounded function (by some constant
\mathfrak{C^{\prime}}
), again, as a consequence of polynomial and log-concavity bounds. This implies that

\lvert p_{j}(x)\partial_{l}\phi(x)-p_{j}(y)\partial_{l}\phi(y)\rvert\leq 2\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\leq 2\mathfrak{C}|x-y|^{\beta-l}
.

The lower-order derivatives are easier to analyze. Indeed, the derivatives of
p_{j}(x)\partial_{u}\phi(x)^{\omega}
are bounded since they are bounded by products of polynomials and
p_{j}(x)
, by the same arguments. Therefore, the functions are Lipschitz, and since the functions themselves are also bounded, this implies the final bound by the same reasoning as before.
∎

S.5.2
Proof of Lemma
6

Proof of Lemma
6
.

For simplicity, we consider the
d=1
case, but the argument carries over to several dimensions. Note first that we can write
f_{k}(t,z)
as

\displaystyle f_{k}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{(z-x)^{k}}{t^{k+d}}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}\kappa_{i}z^{i}\int x^{k-i}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\kappa_{i}z^{i}g_{k-i}(z,t).

The constants
\kappa_{i}
depend on
t
, but we can assume w.l.g. that
t>0.5
so that they all remain bounded; otherwise, we can write the above as an integral with respect to
p_{1}(x)
. Above, we have defined
g_{k}(z,t):=\int x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)dx
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that each of the
z_{i}g_{k-i}(z,t)
are
\beta
-Hölder continuous. Indeed, let’s first bound their derivatives of order
l\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
. We have

\lvert g_{k}^{(l)}(t,z)\rvert=\Big\lvert\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\right)^{l}\int x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}^{(l)}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx\Big\lvert\lesssim\int x^{k}P\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx.

where we have used that, by Lemma
5
,
p_{1}^{(l)}
is bounded by a polynomial of degree bounded by
l+1
(denoted by
P
) times
p_{1}
. If we are able to show that the right-hand side above is finite, this would imply both the validity of differentiation under the integral sign (by dominated convergence) and a bound to the derivative. By Lemma
19
we have that for
m>0
,

\int x^{m}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{0}||z||^{2}}\left(1+\lVert z\rVert^{m}\right).

(E.16)

and since
t>0.5
, we can bound, for some constants
\widetilde{c}_{l}
,

g_{k}^{(l)}(t,z)\leq\exp\left(-c_{1}\lVert z\rVert^{2}\right)\sum_{l=0}^{l+k+1}\widetilde{c}_{l}\lVert z\rVert^{l},

(E.17)

so that
g_{k}^{(l)}(t,z)
is bounded over
z
and
t
. Now, note that derivatives of
f_{k}(t,z)
are sums of derivatives of products of the monomials
z^{i}
and
g_{k}(t,z)
. Each derivative of
z^{i}
will at most increase the degree of the polynomial above, but this will remain bounded because of the exponential term. Therefore, derivatives of
f_{k}(t,z)
up to order
l
are bounded.

We now need to bound the differences
\lVert f^{(l)}_{k}(t,z_{1})-f^{(l)}_{k}(t,z_{2})\rVert
where
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
. Again, by the above argument, this derivative expresses as a product of derivatives of monomials of
z^{i}
and of
g_{k-i}(z,t)
. In turn, by the product rule, these derivatives will be sums of monomials of lower degree and of
g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z,t)
, for
l^{\prime}\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
. In particular, note that if
l^{\prime}<\lfloor\beta\rfloor
then
l^{\prime}+1\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
so that by (
E.17
)

(z^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z,t))^{\prime}\leq\exp(-c_{1}\lVert z\rVert^{2})Q(z),

for some polynomial
Q
of bounded degree, so the right-hand side is uniformly bounded in
t
and
z
. Therefore, since the derivative of
z^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z,t)
is bounded we get the Lipschitz bound

\lVert z_{1}^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z_{1},t)-z_{2}^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z_{2},t)\rVert\lesssim\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert.

Also, since
z^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k-i}(z,t)
is bounded, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5
(dividing in the cases
\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert<1
and
\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert>1
) we conclude that the right-hand side term in the bound above can be replaced by
\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}
.
Then, it remains to analyze the case
l^{\prime}=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
. In this case, if we are able to show that

\lVert z_{1}^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k}(t,z_{1})-z_{2}^{i}g^{(l^{\prime})}_{k}(t,z_{2})\rVert\lesssim\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor},

(E.18)

then, by the above argument, collecting terms, we will deduce that

\lVert f^{(l)}_{k}(t,z_{1})-f^{(l)}_{k}(t,z_{2})\rVert\lesssim\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}+\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert\lesssim\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor},

and the proof would be concluded. To show (
E.18
), we note first that by the Faá di Bruno’s formula (as in the proof of Lemma
5
)

\displaystyle g_{k}^{(l^{\prime})}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\right)^{l}\int x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}^{(l^{\prime})}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\right)^{l^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1},\ldots,w_{l^{\prime}}\geq 0,\\
\sum_{u=1}^{l^{\prime}}uw_{u}=l^{\prime}\end{subarray}}c_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l^{\prime}}}\int x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\prod_{u=1}^{l^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{u}\phi_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\right)^{w_{l^{\prime}}}dx.

Again, we will produce bounds based on the study of each terms. As in the proof of Lemma
5
, we argue first that for
u<l^{\prime}=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
, all the terms containing
\partial_{u}\phi_{j}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{w_{l^{\prime}}}
remain bounded.
Indeed, the function
z\rightarrow z^{i}\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)\partial_{u}\phi_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)^{w_{l^{\prime}}}dx
has bounded derivative since, by the product rule, expresses as an integral of terms that can be uniformly bounded, by our previous arguments. Therefore, again, it suffices to analyze the term
u=l^{\prime}=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
. To analyze this case, consider the function

m(z,t):=\int z^{i}x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\phi_{1}^{(l^{\prime})}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx.

We can bound

\displaystyle\lVert m(z_{1},t)-m(z_{2},t)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\lVert\int z_{1}^{i}x^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{1}-x}{t}\right)\left(\phi_{1}^{(l^{\prime})}\left(x+\frac{z_{1}-x}{t}\right)-\phi^{(l^{\prime})}_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{2}-x}{t}\right)\right)dx\Big\rVert

(E.19)

\displaystyle+\Big\lVert\int x^{k}p_{0}(x)\phi_{1}^{(l^{\prime})}\left(x+\frac{z_{2}-x}{t}\right)\left(z_{1}^{i}p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{1}-x}{t}\right)-z_{2}^{i}p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{2}-x}{t}\right)\right)dx\Big\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{i}\int\lVert x\rVert^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{1}-x}{t}\right)\frac{1}{t}\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}dx

\displaystyle+\int\lVert x\rVert^{k}p_{0}(x)H(x)\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert dx.

In the second inequality, we used the Hölder bound on
\phi_{1}
and the fact that
\phi_{1}^{(l^{\prime})}
is bounded. Also, we have defined
H(x):=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\partial_{z}G(x,z)\rVert
where
G(x,z)=z^{i}p_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)
. We can bound
H(x)
as follows. If
i\geq 1
(otherwise the analysis is simpler), using that
\phi_{1}(x)
is minimized at
x_{1}
we have

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\partial_{z}G(x,z)\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert iz^{i-1}\rVert p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)+\frac{\lVert z^{i}\rVert}{t}p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\Big\lVert\phi_{1}^{(1)}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\Big\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert z\rVert^{i-1}\left(\lVert z\rVert+1\right)\exp(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\lVert x+\frac{z-x}{t}-x_{1}\Big\rVert^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{\lVert z\rVert\leq 1}\lVert z\rVert^{i-1}\left(\lVert z\rVert+1\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha}{4t^{2}}\Big\lVert z-tx_{1}\Big\rVert^{2}\right)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(t-1)^{2}}{2t^{2}}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(t-1)^{2}}{2t^{2}}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\right).

Above, we used that
\phi^{(1)}
is bounded, that
\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}\geq\lVert x\rVert^{2}/2-\lVert y\rVert^{2}
, and the fact that the products of terms containing
z
is bounded.
Also, since

p_{0}(x)H(x)\lesssim\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\lVert x\rVert^{2}}{2}\left(1-\frac{t^{2}}{(1-t)^{2}}\right)\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\frac{2t-1}{(1-t)^{2}}\right),

we can continue bounding the differences as

\displaystyle\lVert m(z_{1},t)-m(z_{2},t)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{i}\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\int\lVert x\rVert^{k}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z_{1}-x}{t}\right)dx

\displaystyle+\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert\int\lVert x\rVert^{k}\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\frac{2t-1}{(1-t)^{2}}\right)dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{i}Q(z_{1})\exp\left(-c\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{2}\right)\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}+\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}.

In the second-to-last inequality, we used the polynomial bound described earlier and the fact that
t>0.5
. The last inequality follows by dividing into the cases
\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert\leq 1
and
\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}\rVert\geq 1
(to apply this argument, we need to ensure that
m(z_{1},t)
is bounded, which is also true by the same rationale as before).

∎

S.5.3
Proof of Proposition
3

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Without loss of generality,
t>0.5
. Otherwise, can switch the roles of
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
. Let’s call
\gamma>0
any upper bound on the Hessians of
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
.
We have that

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}v_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{d}}\nabla_{z}\frac{\int(z-x)p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t^{d}}\right)dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{d}}-\frac{1}{t^{d}}\nabla_{z}\frac{\int xp_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}.

The first term
1/t
is uniformly bounded in
z
. Define the family of measures

p_{z}(x)=\frac{p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}

Note that

\displaystyle t\nabla_{z}\frac{\int xp_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t\nabla_{z}\int xp_{z}(x)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int xp_{0}(x)\nabla p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}dx}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}-\int xp_{z}(x)dx\frac{\int p_{0}(x)\nabla p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}dx}{\int p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int xp_{z}(x)\frac{\nabla p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}}{p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)}dx-\int xp_{z}(x)dx\int\frac{\nabla p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}}{p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)}p_{z}(x)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int xp_{z}(x)\left(\frac{\nabla p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}}{p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)}-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\frac{\nabla p_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)^{\top}}{p_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)}\right)\right)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int xp_{z}(x)\left(\nabla\log\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)^{\top}-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)^{\top}\right)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X\left(\nabla\log\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)^{\top}-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Cov}_{p_{z}}\left(X,\nabla\log\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right).

Therefore, it suffices to bound each of the terms in the above covariance matrix uniformly over
z
and
t\geq 0.5
. Call
C_{i,j}
each entry, then by Cauchy-Schwarz

\lvert C_{i,j}\rvert\leq\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\log p_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}.

Note that assumption (
U3
) implies that
p_{z}
is strongly log-concave, uniformly on
z
and
t
. Indeed, if we write

p_{z}(x)\propto\exp\left(-\phi_{z}(x)\right),\quad\phi_{z}(x)=\phi_{0}(x)+\phi_{1}\left(\frac{z-(1-t)x}{t}\right),

the function
\phi_{z}(\cdot)
satisfies

\alpha\left(1+\frac{(1-t)^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)I_{d}\preceq\nabla^{2}\phi_{z}(x)=\nabla^{2}\phi_{0}(x)+\frac{(1-t)^{2}}{t^{2}}\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\preceq\gamma\left(1+\frac{(1-t)^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)I_{d}.

(E.20)

Additionally, since
p_{1}
is strongly log-concave,

\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{z}(x)\right)^{-1}\preceq\frac{t^{2}}{(1-t)^{2}}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)\right)^{-1}.

(E.21)

We will use the above bounds later on when we
invoke the Brascamp-Lieb inequality
(
14
, Theorem 4.2)
, that for a (strictly) log-concave measure
p(x)\propto\exp\left(-\phi(x)\right)
and a differentiable function
h
with finite
\mathrm{Var}_{p}(h(X))
we have

\mathrm{Var}_{p}\left(h(X)\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{p}\left(\nabla^{\top}h(X)\left(\nabla^{2}\phi(X)\right)^{-1}\nabla h(X)\right).

(E.22)

In particular, if
p
is strongly log-concave, so that
I_{d}\preceq\sigma^{2}\nabla^{2}\phi(x)
for some
\sigma^{2}>0
then
it satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant
\sigma^{2}
;

\mathrm{Var}_{p}\left(h(X)\right)\leq\sigma^{2}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left(\lVert\nabla h(X)\rVert^{2}\right).

(E.23)

To bound the first variance term, we consider the function
h_{i}(x)=X_{0i}
with gradient
\nabla h(x)=e_{i}
(the
i
-th canonical vector). By (
E.20
) and (
E.22
).

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(e_{i}^{\top}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{z}(X)\right)^{-1}e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(e_{i}^{\top}e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{dt^{2}}{\alpha(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})}.

To bound the other variance term, consider the function

h_{j}(x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\log p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\phi_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right).

By (
E.21
) and (
E.22
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\log p_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla h_{j}(X)^{\top}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{z}(X)\right)^{-1}\nabla h_{j}(X)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}}{(1-t)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla h_{j}(X)^{\top}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)^{-1}\nabla h_{j}(X)\right).

Now, note that the vector
\nabla h_{j}(x)
is (up to the term
t/(1-t)
) the
j
-th row of the matrix
\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)
. Then, the term inside the expectation is of the form
v_{j}V^{-1}v_{j}
where
V
is a positive definite matrix, and
v_{j}
is an arbitrary row of
v_{j}
. Since

v_{j}V^{-1}v_{j}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}V^{-1}v_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}(V),

and using the uniform upper bound on
\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}
we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\log p_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}}{(1-t)^{2}}\frac{(1-t)^{2}}{t^{2}}\ \mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d\gamma.

Let’s now turn to the second derivatives. We fix one coordinate
v_{i}(t,z)
and will bound each coordinate of the Hessian
\nabla^{2}v_{i}(t,z)
. We will use the identity

(f/g)^{\prime\prime}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{g}-\frac{f}{g}\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{g}-2\frac{g^{\prime}}{g}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}}{g}-\frac{f}{g}\frac{g^{\prime}}{g}\right).

Taking
g=p_{t}(z)
,
f=\int X_{0i}p_{0}(x)p_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)dx
, and reasoning as with the first derivative we obtain

\displaystyle t^{d+2}\nabla^{2}_{z}v_{i}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\frac{\nabla^{2}p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)}{p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\frac{\nabla^{2}p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)}{p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)}\right)

\displaystyle-2\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log\left(w(X,z\right)\right)\right)^{\top}\right),

where we have defined
w(x,z):=x+(z-x)/t
.
We will further rearrange the above expressions. Since

\left(\log f(x)\right)^{\prime\prime}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f(x)}-\frac{f^{\prime}(x)^{2}}{f(x)^{2}}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f(x)}-(\log f(x))^{\prime 2},

We can express the above as

\displaystyle t^{3}\nabla^{2}_{z}v_{i}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle-2\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)\right)^{\top}\right).

We can identify the first line above as
\text{Cov}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i},\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}(w(X,z)\right)
. Likewise, since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left(X-\mathbb{E}(X)\right)(Y-\mathbb{E}(Y))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(XY^{2})-\mathbb{E}(X)\mathbb{E}(Y^{2})-2\mathbb{E}(Y)\mathbb{E}\left(X(Y-\mathbb{E}(Y))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Cov}(X,Y^{2})-2\mathbb{E}(Y)\text{Cov}(X,Y).

Then, if we call
\widetilde{w}(x,z)=\nabla\log p_{1}(w(x,z))

\displaystyle t^{2+d}\nabla^{2}_{z}v_{i}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Cov}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i},\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}(w(X,z))\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\left(X_{0i}-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}(X_{0i})\right)\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)\right)\right)\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)\right)\right)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}(X_{0i})^{1/2}\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}\left(w(X,z)\right)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Var}_{p_{z}}\left(X_{0i}\right)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)\right)\right)^{2}\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\widetilde{w}(X,z)\right)^{\top}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

In the above displays, inequalities are interpreted component-wise. It remains to bound each of the variance and expectation terms. The variance of
X_{0i}
was already bounded. Also, an immediate bound for the variance of the Hessian term follows from the fact that
\nabla^{2}\log p_{1}(w(X,z))=-\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}(w(X,z))
has bounded eigenvalues, by (
E.20
). In turn, this entails entry-wise bounds for the matrix, thanks to elementary properties of matrix norms
(
47
)
.

It only remains to bound the quadratic gradient term above. We use an established moment bound for measures satisfying a Poincaré condition (see
48
and Lemma
28
). By applying (
E.56
) to the
j
-th coordinate function of the centered version of
\widetilde{w}(x,z)
,

h_{j}(x)=\widetilde{w}_{j}(x,z)-\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}(\widetilde{w}_{j}(x,z))
, and using that
p_{z}(\cdot)
satisfies (
E.23
) with parameter
\sigma^{2}=t^{2}/\alpha((t^{2}+(1-t)^{2}))
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\left(\widetilde{w}_{j}(x,z)-\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{w}_{j}(x,z)\right)^{4}\right)\leq 4\frac{t^{4}}{\alpha^{2}(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{z}}\left(\Big\lVert\nabla\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\phi_{1}\left(X+\frac{z-X}{t}\right)\Big\rVert^{4}\right).

The term inside the expectation is bounded uniformly over
z
since it is the fourth power of the norm of a vector made up from entries of the matrix
\nabla^{2}\phi_{1}(x+(z-x)/t)
. Therefore, the fourth centered moment above is bounded for each index
j
. We achieve the final conclusion by applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to the matrix of cross moments in the above displays.
∎

S.5.4
Additional results for Section
5.1

Lemma 17
.

Suppose that
p_{0},p_{1}
are log-concave. Then,

and for each
\alpha>0
:

\sup_{s\in[0,1],z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}\left(\lVert\Delta\rVert^{\alpha}|X_{s}=z\right)p_{s}(z)<\infty.

Proof.

By Lemma 1 in
27
there are constants
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

p_{1}(x)\leq e^{-a\|x\|+b}
. Also, without loss of generality,
t>0.5
. From this,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\lVert\Delta\rVert^{\alpha}|X_{s}=z\right)p_{s}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\delta\rVert^{\alpha}p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert z-x\rVert^{\alpha}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z\rVert^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert x\rVert^{\alpha}p_{0}(x)dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z\rVert^{\alpha}p_{s}(z)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert x\rVert^{\alpha}e^{-a\lVert x\rVert+b}dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\lVert z\rVert^{\alpha}p_{s}(z)+\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\alpha+d-1}e^{-r}dr.

In the last line, we used polar coordinates. We can identify the last integral with a moment of the exponential distribution, which is finite if
\alpha+d-1>-1
. The first term above is finite if
\alpha>0
, by Lemma
19
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Suppose that
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
satisfy (
U1
). Then, for any bounded set
B
, we have that

\inf_{z\in B,t\in[0,1]}p_{t}(z)>0.

Proof of Lemma
18
.

Let
B_{1}
be the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
centered at the origin. Recall that

p_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\geq\int_{B_{1}}p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta.

Hence, for
z\in B
,
p_{t}(z)
is bounded below because
\inf_{\delta\in B_{1}}p_{0}(z-t\delta)>0
and
\inf_{\delta\in B_{1}}p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)>0
. (Recall that assumption (
U1
) implies that
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are bounded away from zero on all bounded subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, logconcave densities are continuous in the interior of any compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.)
∎

A first observation, showed as a separate Lemma, is that the above assumptions imply that
p_{0},p_{1}
also have
\beta
-Hölder smoothness, so our results can be put in the perspective of the classical Kernel-based estimators
(
95
)
.

Lemma 19
.

Suppose that
X_{0}\sim\mu_{0}
and
X_{1}\sim\mu_{1}
are independent, and that assumption (
U2
)
holds. Set
m_{j}=\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]
and
\Sigma_{j}=\mathrm{Var}(X_{j})
(
j=0,1
). If
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}
are positive definite with eigenvalues bounded away from
0
and
\infty
, and if
p_{0},p_{1}
are log-concave, then there exist constants
c_{0}=c_{0}(\{m_{j}\},\{\Sigma_{j}\}),c_{1}=c_{1}(\{m_{j}\},\{\Sigma_{j}\})>0
depending only on
m_{0},m_{1},\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}
such that

p_{t}(z)\leq c_{0}\exp(-c_{1}\|z\|)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\in[0,1].

(E.24)

Additionally, if assumption (
U3
) holds (i.e.
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
are strongly-log concave), for following functions
h_{m}(z,t)
defined for
m>0

h_{m}(z,t):=\frac{1}{t^{d+m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x^{m}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right),

we have that for some
c_{0},c_{1}>0

h_{m}(z,t)\leq c_{0}(1+\lVert z\rVert^{m})\exp(-c_{1}\|z\|^{2})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\in[0,1].

(E.25)

In particular,

p_{t}(z)\leq c_{0}\exp(-c_{1}\|z\|^{2})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\in[0,1].

(E.26)

Proof of Lemma
19
.

Let’s first show the statement under log-concavity. As
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are log-concave and independent, this implies that
X_{t}=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}
is also log-concave. Moreover,
\mbox{Var}(X_{t})=(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}+t^{2}\Sigma_{1}
, whose minimum eigenvalues are lower bounded by those of
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}
and the maximum eigenvalues are upper bounded by those of
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}
.
Thus, inequality (
E.24
) follows from Assumption (
U3
) and Corollary 6(a) of
64
and implies the boundedness of the densities. We note that while Corollary 6(a) of
64
is proved with an assumption that the largest eigenvalue of the covariance matrix is bounded by
1+\eta
for some
\eta\in(0,1)
, the proof can be extended to the case of an arbitrarily finite largest eigenvalue.

Now, under (
U3
) call
x_{i}
the unique minimizer of
\phi_{i}
, and assume w.l.g that
t>0.5
; otherwise we can exchange the roles of
p_{0},p_{1}
. By strong log-concavity we have

\displaystyle h_{m}(z,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{d+m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x^{m}p_{0}(x)p_{1}\left(x+\frac{z-x}{t}\right)dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle x^{m}\exp\left(-\left(\phi_{0}(x_{0})+\phi_{1}(x_{1})\right)\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\lVert x-x_{0}\rVert^{2}+\lVert x+\frac{z-x}{t}-x_{1}\rVert^{2}\right)\right)dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x+x_{0})^{m}\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\lVert x+x_{0}+\frac{z-x-x_{0}}{t}-x_{1}\rVert^{2}\right)\right)dx.

In the last line, we used the change of variables
y=x-x_{0}
.
We can identify the above sum of squares as
\|x\|^{2}+||Ax+\widetilde{x}||^{2}
where
A=(t-1)/t
and
\widetilde{x}=(z+(t-1)x_{0}-tx_{1})/t
. Further, since

\|x\|^{2}+||Ax+\widetilde{x}||^{2}=(1+A^{2})\left(\lVert x+\frac{A}{1+A^{2}}\widetilde{x}\rVert^{2}\right)+\frac{||\widetilde{x}||^{2}}{1+A^{2}},

we conclude that up to the
||\widetilde{x}^{2}||/(1+A^{2})
term and the normalizing constant, the above exponential identifies with a Gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}_{t}
and mean
\mu_{t}
given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=I_{d}\dfrac{1}{\alpha(1+A^{2})}=

\displaystyle I_{d}\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{A}{1+A^{2}}\widetilde{x}=

\displaystyle(1-t)\frac{z-(1-t)x_{0}-tx_{1}}{t^{2}+(1-t)^{2}}

By replacing, and using that
\sigma_{t}
is bounded we get

\displaystyle h_{m}(z,t)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(2\pi\sigma_{t}^{2})^{d/2}\exp\left(-\frac{\lVert\widetilde{X}\rVert^{2}}{2\alpha(1+A^{2})}\right)\mathbb{E}_{X\sim N\left(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t}\right)}(X+x_{0})^{m}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\alpha\frac{\lVert z+(t-1)x_{0}-tx_{1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})}\right)\mathbb{E}_{X\sim N\left(0,I_{d}\right)}\left(\sigma_{t}X+\mu_{t}+x_{0}\right)^{m}.

Let’s first show that the first term is bounded (up to constants independent on
t
) by
\exp(-c||z||^{2})
. Indeed, the denominator in the exponential is bounded below and the numerator writes as
\alpha\lVert z-x_{t}\rVert^{2}
, where
x_{t}=tx_{1}+(1-t)x_{0}
. Then,
\lVert z-x_{t}\rVert^{2}\geq 0.5\lVert z\rVert^{2}-\lVert x_{t}\rVert^{2}\geq 0.5\lVert z\rVert^{2}-\max_{t\in[0,1]}\lVert x_{t}\rVert^{2},
and we conclude.
Then, it only remains to bound each of the expectation terms. We claim that each of these is bounded by a constant times
z^{k}
. Indeed, since
\mu_{t}+x_{0}=a_{t}z+b_{t}
for some
a_{t},b_{t}
of bounded norm (since
t>0.5
), and since the
(\sigma_{t}X+\mu_{t}+x_{0})^{m}
is a polynomial expression in the above bounded quantities of degree
m
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim N\left(0,I_{d}\right)}\left(\sigma_{t}X\mu_{t}+x_{0}\right)^{m}\lesssim\lVert z\rVert^{m}+1
.
∎

S.5.5
Proof of Theorem
7
and related results

Here we prove Theorem
7
and other intermediate results regarding the linearization and their bounds (Proposition
8
, Theorem
15
, Lemma
20
, Lemma
21
,Proposition
9
)

Proof.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

Denote
\widehat{z}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
and
z(t,t_{0},z_{0})
the solutions to an ODE with
t\geq t_{0}
, initial condition
\widehat{z}(t_{0})=z_{0},z(t_{0})=z_{0}
and right hand sides
\widehat{v}(s,z)
and
v(s,z)
and respectively. We use Lemma
7
to compare the population rectified transport
R(x)=\mathfrak{R}(1,x)=z(1)=z(1,0,x)
to its empirical version;
\widehat{R}(x)=\widehat{z}(1)=\widehat{z}(1,0,x)
(same initial condition but different right-hand side). By
(
35
),

\displaystyle\widehat{R}(x)-R(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\left(\widehat{v}(s,z(s))-v(s,z(s))\right)ds+

(E.27)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left(\widehat{\Phi}\left(1,s,z(s)\right)-\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\right)\left(\widehat{v}(s,z(s))-v(s,z(s))\right)ds,

where
\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z)=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{z}(t,s,z)
. We will use repeatedly the fact that for any arbitrary
\widehat{f},f,\widehat{p}>0,p>0
we have

\frac{\widehat{f}}{\widehat{p}}-\frac{f}{p}=\frac{1}{p}\left(\widehat{f}-\frac{f}{p}\widehat{p}\right)\left(1-\frac{\widehat{p}-p}{\widehat{p}}\right).

(E.28)

Define the quantities

\widehat{L}(s,z):=\Phi\left(1,s,z\right)\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z),\quad\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z):=\frac{\widehat{f}(s,z)-v(s,z)\widehat{p}(s,z)}{p_{s}(z)},\quad\widehat{r}(s,z):=\widehat{p}(s,z)-p(s,z).

(E.29)

from (
E.28
) it follows that

\widehat{v}(s,z(s))-v(s,z(s))=\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))-\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))\frac{\widehat{r}(s,z(s))}{\widehat{p}(s,z(s))},

and therefore, we can re-state (
S.5.6
) as

\displaystyle\widehat{R}(x)-R(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{L}(x)-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{L}(s,z(s))\frac{\widehat{r}(s,z(s))}{\widehat{p}(s,z(s))}ds}_{\widetilde{S}_{1}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\left(\widehat{\Phi}\left(1,s,z(s)\right)-\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\right)\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))}_{\widetilde{S}_{2}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\left(\widehat{\Phi}\left(1,s,z(s)\right)-\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\right)\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))\frac{\widehat{r}(s,z(s))}{\widehat{p}(s,z(s))}ds}_{\widetilde{S}_{3}},

By Theorem
15
, for a CLT to hold, it suffices to show that for
i=1,2,3
,

\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\widetilde{S}_{i}=o_{p}(1)
.
We will analyze each term separately. First, note that in either case, as a consequence of uniform consistency of the kernel density estimator, and using that this consistency is also uniform in
s
, (see the proof of Proposition
8
), and since
\inf_{s\in[0,1]}p_{s}(z_{s})>0
(Lemma
18
), we have

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\frac{1}{\widehat{p}(s,z_{s})}=O_{p}(1).

The above implies that the
\widehat{p}
terms in the denominators can be ignored in subsequent estimates.
Let’s bound
\widetilde{S}_{1}
. By a similar argument as in Lemmas
32
and
33
:

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right),

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{L}(s,z(s))\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\rVert\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widetilde{\widehat{L}}(s,z(s))\rVert=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\widehat{r}(s,z(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right).

Therefore,

|\widetilde{S}_{1}|\lesssim\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{L}(s,z(s))\rVert\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\widehat{r}(s,z(s))|=O_{p}\left(\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right)^{2}\right)=o_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh^{d-1}_{n}}}\right).

To analyze
\widetilde{S}_{2},\widetilde{S}_{3}
we use the bounds on the difference
\widehat{\Phi}-\Phi
shown in Lemma
20
. By a similar argument, we conclude that

\widetilde{S}_{2}=O_{p}\left(\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2}}}+h^{\beta-1}\right]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h^{\beta}\right]\right)

and

\widetilde{S}_{3}=O_{p}\left(\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2}}}+h^{\beta-1}\right]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h^{\beta}\right]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right]\right).

Both of these terms are
O_{p}\left((nh_{n}^{d-1})^{-1/2}\right)
whenever
h_{n}\gg n^{-\frac{1}{d+2+\varepsilon}}
, for any
\varepsilon>0.

∎

S.5.6
Proposition
8

Proposition 8
.

Consider the linearized quantity

\widehat{L}(x):=\int_{0}^{1}\Phi\left(1,s,z(s)\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{f}(s,z(s))-v(s,z(s))\widehat{p}(s,z(s))}{p_{s}(z(s))}\right)ds.

(E.30)

Suppose that assumptions (
K1
), (
U1
), (
U2
), (
U3
),and (
U4
) hold. Then, for any convex and compact set
B

(a)

Uniform consistency: almost surely,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in B}||\widehat{L}(x)||=0.

(b)

Bias:

\sup_{x\in B}||\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))||\leq Ch^{\beta},

(E.31)

(c)

Variance:

\sup_{x\in B}\lVert nh^{d-1}\text{Cov}\left(\widehat{L}(x)\right)-\Sigma_{h}(x)\rVert=o(1),

(E.32)

for some matrix
\Sigma_{h}
detailed in the proof.
If (
U5
) also holds, we have that
\Sigma_{h}(x)\to\Sigma(x)
where
\Sigma(x)
is defined in (
32
), and

\lVert\text{Cov}\left(\widehat{L}(x)\right)\rVert\lesssim\frac{1}{nh^{d-1}}.

(E.33)

We defer the more involved proof of Proposition
8
to the next Section
S.5.7
. There, we also include additional lemmas that this proof relies on. With all of the above, we can state a CLT for
\widehat{L}(x)
.

Theorem 15
.

Suppose that assumptions Suppose that assumptions (
K1
), (
U1
), (
U2
), (
U3
),and (
U4
) hold. In the setup of Theorem
7
then, for
\Sigma_{h}
defined in (
E.39
) we have

\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\Sigma_{h}^{-1/2}(x)\widehat{L}(x)~\overset{d}{\to}~N\left(0,I_{d}\right).

If (
U5
) also holds, we have that

\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\widehat{L}(x)~\overset{d}{\to}~N\left(0,\Sigma(x)\right),

Proof of Theorem
15
.

\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\widehat{L}(x)=\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\left(\widehat{L}(x)-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))\right)+\sqrt{nh_{n}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x)).

By the bias property in Proposition
8
b, (
E.31
), and the undersmoothing condition
h_{n}\ll n^{-1/(d-1+2\beta)}
, the second term in the right-hand side above is
o(1)
. We will now apply the Lyapunov CLT (
29
) to the triangular array

U_{n,i}:=L_{n,i}(x)-\mathbb{E}(L_{n,i}(x)),\quad L_{n,i}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}(\Delta_{i}-v(s,z_{s}))K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z(s))ds.

We can then express

\displaystyle U_{n,i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U^{1}_{n,i}+U^{2}_{n,i},

\displaystyle U^{1}_{n,i}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\left(\Delta_{i}K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})-\mathbb{E}\left(\Delta_{i}K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})\right)\right)ds,

\displaystyle U^{2}_{n,i}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}v(s,z_{s})\left(K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})-\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})\right)\right)ds.

We will bound
\mathbb{E}\left(||U_{n,i}||^{2+\kappa}\right)
for any
\kappa>0
. To do this, first recall that
||x+y||^{2+\kappa}\lesssim\|x\|^{2+\kappa}+||y||^{2+\kappa}
, so we can analyze
U_{n,i}^{1}
and
U_{n,i}^{2}
separately. Also, recall that for a vector-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
on a probability space with measure
\mu
we have

\displaystyle\Big\lVert\int f(x)d\mu(x)\Big\rVert^{2+\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int\lVert f(x)\rVert d\mu(x)\right)^{2+\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\lVert f(x)\rVert^{2+\kappa}d\mu(x).

The second inequality follows from Hölder’s inequality. Note also that for any vector
x

\Big\lVert\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}x\Big\rVert\leq\Big\lVert\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\Big\rVert\lVert x\rVert\lesssim\|x\|,

where the last inequality follows from uniform upper and lower bounds on
\Phi(1,s,z_{s})
and
p_{s}(z_{s})
over
s\in[0,1]
, respectively. Also, since
v(s,z_{s})
is also upper bounded on this interval, we conclude that it suffices to bound uniformly in
s
the quantities

U_{n,i}^{1}(s):=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})-\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})\right)\right)^{2+\kappa}\right]

and

U_{n,i}^{2}(s):=\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\Delta_{i}K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})-\mathbb{E}\left(\Delta_{i}K_{h_{n}}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})\right)\rVert^{2+\kappa}\right]).

For an arbitrary
a>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})^{a}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{-ad}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K^{a}\left(\frac{z-z_{s}}{h}\right)p_{s}(z)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{-ad+d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)^{a}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{-ad+d}p_{s}(z_{s})\int K(u)^{a}du+h^{-ad+d+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)^{a}u\cdot\nabla p_{s}(z_{s}+\tau hu)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{-ad+d}),

since
p_{s}(z)
and the gradient of
p_{s}(z)
are uniformly bounded in
s
and
z
(Lemma
6
). Then, for an arbitrary
a>0
,
Taking
a=1
, we obtain that
\mathbb{E}(K_{h}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s}))\lesssim 1
. Taking
a=\kappa+2
, using that
K
has bounded support we obtain
\mathbb{E}(K_{h}(X_{i}(s)-z_{s})^{2+\kappa})\lesssim h^{-d(1+\kappa)}
Then, since
\mathbb{E}|X+Y|^{p}\lesssim\mathbb{E}|X|^{p}+\mathbb{E}|Y|^{p}
we conclude that
U_{n,i}(s)\lesssim h^{-d(1+\kappa)}
, uniformly in
s
.
To bound
U_{n,i}^{2}(s)
we follow a conditioning argument. Call
f(z)=\mathbb{E}\left(\lVert\Delta_{i}\rVert^{2+\kappa}|X_{i}(s)=z\right)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\Delta_{i}K_{h}\left(X_{i}(s)-z_{s}\right)\rVert^{2+\kappa}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\lVert\Delta_{i}K_{h}\left(X_{i}(s)-z_{s}\right)\rVert^{2+\kappa}|X_{i}(s)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\lVert\Delta_{i}\rVert^{2+\kappa}|X_{i}(s)\right)|K_{h}\left(X_{i}(s)-z_{s}\right)|^{2+\kappa}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)K^{2+\kappa}_{h}(z-z_{s})p_{s}(z)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{-d(1+\kappa)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z_{s}+hu)p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)K^{2+\kappa}(u)du

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{-d(1+\kappa)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K^{2+\kappa}(u)du

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{-d(1+\kappa)}.

In the second-to-last line, we used the moment bound in Lemma
17
;
f(x)p_{s}(x)
is bounded uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s\in[0,1]
. In the last line, we used that
K
is bounded. By a similar argument as before, we conclude that
U^{2}_{n,i}(s)\lesssim h^{-d(1+\kappa)}
and so
U_{n,i}(s)
is also bounded by
h^{-d(1+\kappa)}
.

We now turn to lower-bound the smallest eigenvalue of
U_{n,i}
. Using that (Proposition
8
c)

\lVert nh^{d-1}\text{Cov}(\widehat{L}(x))-\Sigma_{h}(x)\rVert_{\infty}=o(1),

we can deduce that
\lambda_{min}(\text{Cov}(\widehat{L}(x)))\gtrsim n^{-1}h^{1-d}\left(1+o(1)\right)
: indeed, suppose first that
\Sigma_{h}(x)
is the identity matrix. In that case, since
||A-B||\lesssim||A-B||_{\infty}
, it suffices to show that if
\lVert A-I_{d}\lVert_{\infty}<a
then
\lambda_{min}(A)>1-a
, which easily follows from Gershgorin circle theorem. In the general case, since
\Sigma(x)
is positive definite, we can pre-multiply to obtain a lower bound on the eigenvalues of
\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\widehat{L}(x)\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}
. In turn, since
\Sigma(x)
is positive definite, this leads to a bound on the eigenvalues of
\widehat{L}(x)=\Sigma(x)^{1/2}\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\widehat{L}(x)\Sigma(x)^{-1/2}\Sigma(x)^{1/2}
. Then, using that

v_{n}^{2}=n^{2}\lambda_{min}\left(\text{Cov}(\widehat{L}(x))\right)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2+\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert U_{n,i}\rVert^{2+\kappa}\lesssim\frac{nh^{-d(1+\kappa)}}{n^{1+\kappa/2}h^{(1-d)(1+\kappa/2)}}\lesssim\left(nh^{d+1+2/\kappa}\right)^{-\kappa/2}.

Therefore, for any
\varepsilon>0
we can take
\kappa=2/(1+\varepsilon)\leq 2
such that
\left(nh^{d+1+2/\kappa}\right)^{-\kappa/2}=\left(nh^{d+2+\varepsilon}\right)^{-\kappa/2}=o(1)
under the condition
h=h_{n}\gg n^{-\frac{1}{d+2+\varepsilon}}

From this, we conclude that

\text{Cov}\left(\widehat{L}(x)\right)^{-1/2}\left(\widehat{L}(x)-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))\right)~\overset{d}{\to}~N\left(0,I_{d}\right),

and since
nh^{d-1}\text{Cov}\left(\widehat{L}(x)\right)~\overset{d}{\to}~\Sigma(x)
, by Slutsky’s theorem, that

\sqrt{nh^{d-1}}\left(\widehat{L}(x)-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))\right)~\overset{d}{\to}~N\left(0,\Sigma(x)^{1/2}\Sigma(x)^{1/2}\right).

∎

Lemma 20
.

We have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{\Phi}(1,s,z(s))-\Phi(1,s,z(s))\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2}}}+h_{n}^{\beta-1}\right)

Proof.

We will use Alekseev’s formula (Lemma
7
) to bound the difference
\widehat{\Phi}(1,s,z(s))-\Phi(1,s,z(s))
, this time using the ODEs that defines
\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s))
and
\Phi(t,s,z(s))
for
t\geq s
.
By Lemma
34
, they solve, respectively, the linear ODE’s

\displaystyle x_{1}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,\widehat{z}(t,s,z(s)))x_{1}(t),

\displaystyle x_{2}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,z(s)))x_{2}(t),

with initial condition
x_{1}(s)=x_{2}(s)=I_{d}
. Then, by (
35
),

\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s))-\Phi(t,s,z(s))=\int_{s}^{t}\Theta(t,u,\widehat{\Phi}(u,s,z(s)))g(u,\widehat{\Phi}(u,s,z(s)))du,

(E.34)

where

\Theta(t,s,z_{0})=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\Phi(t,s,z_{0}),

(E.35)

and

g(u,w)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(u,\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s))-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(u,z(u,s,z(s))\right)w.

Let’s now bound the difference above. Note that, by uniqueness of solutions to the ODE, we have
u\geq s
,
z(u,s,z(s))=z(u)
, and
\widehat{z}(u,s,\widehat{z}(s))=\widehat{z}(u)
. Then, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(u,\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s))-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(u,z(u,s,z(s)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(u,\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s)))-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(u,\widehat{z}(u))}_{B_{1}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(u,\widehat{z}(u))-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(u,\widehat{z}(u))}_{B_{2}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(u,\widehat{z}(u))-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(u,z(u))}_{B_{3}}.

We start by bounding
B_{1}
. By the mean value theorem, and using that by assumption (
U4
) and continuity of
z(s)
,
\sup_{s,\lambda\in[0,1]}||\lambda z(s)+(1-\lambda)\widehat{z}(s)||\leq R
for
R=O_{p}(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq u\leq 1}\Big\lVert\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s))-\widehat{z}(u,s,\widehat{z}(s))\Big\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\widehat{z}(u,s,\lambda z(s)+(1-\lambda)\widehat{z}(s))\Big\rVert\left\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\sup_{||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\widehat{\Phi}(u,s,z)\Big\rVert\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\right\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}(1)\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\rVert.

In the last line, we used Lemma
21
(c).
Therefore, by a similar argument, and using Lemma
21
(b) we get the bound

\displaystyle\lVert B_{1}\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq u\leq 1}\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\widehat{v}(u,\lambda\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s))+(1-\lambda)\widehat{z}(s))\Big\rVert\sup_{0\leq s\leq u\leq 1}\Big\lVert\widehat{z}(u,s,z(s))-\widehat{z}(u,s,\widehat{z}(s))\Big\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(1\right)\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\rVert.

Similarly, by Proposition
3
,

\displaystyle\lVert B_{3}\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1],z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}v(t,z)\Big\rVert\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\rVert\lesssim\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{z}(s)-z(s)\rVert.

Finally, by assumption (
U4
), for
R=O_{p}(1)
,

\displaystyle\lVert B_{2}\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\rVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z)\Big\rVert.

From all of the above, in addition to Lemma
21
(d), we deduce that

\displaystyle\lVert B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3}\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(1\right)\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z)\Big\rVert+\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big\lVert z(t)-\widehat{z}(t)\Big\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(1\right)\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z)\Big\rVert+\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\widehat{v}(t,z)-v(t,z)\Big\rVert\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}(1)O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2}}}+h_{n}^{\beta-1}+\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d}}}+h_{n}^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2}}}+h_{n}^{\beta-1}\right)

With all of this, we can finally bound the difference (
E.34
)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s))-\Phi(t,s,z(s))\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\int_{s}^{t}\lVert\Theta(t,u,\widehat{\Phi}(u,s,z(s)))\rVert\lVert g(u,\widehat{\Phi}(u,s,z(s)))\rVert du

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{t,s\in[0,1],z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\Theta(t,s,z)\rVert\sup_{t,s\in[0,1]}\lVert g(t,\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s)))\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{t,s\in[0,1]}\lVert g(t,\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s)))\rVert.

In the last inequality, we used that
\Theta
is bounded. This follows from the fact that
\Theta
is the derivative of
\Phi
with respect to its initial value (
E.35
), and that by Proposition
34
, this quantity satisfies the ODE

\frac{d}{dt}\Theta(t,s,z)=\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial z^{2}}(t,z(t,s,z))\Phi(t,s,z)+\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z(t,s,z))\Theta(t,s,z).

Since the first and second derivatives of
v
are uniformly bounded, and since
\Phi(t,s,z)
is bounded as well, we deduce that
||\Theta^{\prime}(t,s,z)||^{2}\leq A+B||\Theta(t,s,z)||^{2}
, and we conclude by Gronwall lemma as we have done before. We obtain the final conclusion by noting that
g(t,\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s)))=(B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3})\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z(s))
and using Lemma
21
(c) once more.∎

Lemma 21
.

Suppose that
p_{0},p_{1}
satisfy (
U1
),(
U2
) (
U3
), and(
U4
). Then,
for any radius
R=O_{p}(1)

(a)

\sup_{s\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)=O_{p}(1).

(b)

\sup_{s\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\widehat{v}(t,z)=O_{p}(1).

(c)

\sup_{s,t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,z)=O_{p}(1).

(d)

If
B=\sup_{s,\in[0,1]}||z(s)||
:

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq 1}\lVert\widehat{z}(t,s,x)-z(t,s,x)\rVert=O_{p}(1)\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq B}\lVert\widehat{v}(t,z)-v(t,z)\rVert.

Proof.

To show (a) and (b), we use that for
l=1,2
and any radius
R>0

\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}v(t,z)\Big\rVert\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}v(t,z)\Big\rVert+\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}v(t,z)\Big\rVert.

By standard results in density estimation
53
;
37
, the difference term above is bounded in probability, provided that the radius is bounded in probability. To establish (c) we use Proposition
9
: by (
U4
)

\sup_{t,s,\|x\|\leq R}||\widehat{\Phi}(t,s,x)||\lesssim\exp\left(2d\sup_{t\in[0,1]||z||\leq\widetilde{R}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)\right),

where
\widetilde{R}=O_{p}(1)
is the radius of a ball such that
\sup_{t,s,\|x\|\leq R}||\widehat{z}(t,s,x)||\leq\widetilde{R}
with arbitrarily large probability. In turn, by (a), the right-hand side is
O_{p}(1)
.
It only remains to show (d). We have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\in[0,1]}\lVert\widehat{z}(t,s,x)-z(t,s,x)\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big\lVert\int_{s}^{t}\widehat{\Phi}\left(t,u,z(u)\right)\left(\widehat{v}(u,z(u))-v(u,z(u))\right)du\Big\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{p}(1)\sup_{t\in[0,1],||z||\leq B}\Big\lVert\widehat{v}(t,z)-v(t,z)\Big\rVert,

where we used (c) and the fact that the population trajectory
z(t)
lives on a compact. Again, the existence of this set is guaranteed by Gronwall’s inequality, which gives an explicit control of the magnitude of such trajectories in terms of the (also bounded) derivatives of the population velocity.∎

Proposition 9
.

Let
\Phi(t,s,x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}z(t,s,x)
where
z
solves (
2
) (for a generic sufficiently smooth velocity). Suppose that
z(t,s,x)\in\widetilde{B}
for each
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
and
x\in B
. Then, we have the following bound on
\Phi(t,s,x_{0})

\sup_{t,s\in[0,1],x\in B}\big\lVert\Phi(t,s,x)\big\rVert^{2}\leq d\exp\left(2d\sup_{t\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z)\Big\rVert_{2}\right).

Proof.

Define
\lambda(t)=\big\lVert\Phi(t,s,x)\big\rVert^{2}
. Then, by Lemma
34
,

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Phi(t,s,x)^{\top}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))^{\top}\right)\Phi(t,s,x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))^{\top}\right)\Phi(t,s,x)\Phi(t,s,x)^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))^{\top}\right]\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Phi(t,s,x)^{\top}\Phi(t,s,x)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))^{\top}\right]\lambda(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))^{\top}\Big\rVert_{2}\lambda(t)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sup_{t\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z(t,s,x))\Big\rVert_{2}\lambda(t).

Above, we used the fact that the largest eigenvalue is bounded by the spectral norm. The conclusion follows from Gronwall’s inequality (Lemma
30
), since
\lambda(0)=d
.
∎

S.5.7
Proof of Proposition
8
and additional lemmas

Proof of Proposition
8
.

Note that
\widehat{L}(x)
is a vector. We will bound each coordinate but will avoid indexing to simplify notation.
While (a) is deduced from (b) and (c) we will show first (a) because it better introduces the proof technique.
The naive approach to show (a) would be to invoke classical results on nonparametric regression and density estimation
(
51
)

to conclude that for each starting point
x
and
s
,
\widehat{f}(s,z(s))\rightarrow f(s,z(s))
and

\widehat{p}(s,z(s))\rightarrow p_{s}(z(s))
, a.s.,
from which it follows that the limiting integrand is zero.
However, we need to ensure that this a.s. convergence is uniform in
s
and
starting point
x
, and that we can exchange the limit and integration over
s
.
To do so, we will rely on classical uniform almost sure convergence rate results
for kernel-type estimators
(
37
)
.
We first note that by Lemma 2 in
15
and
since the spatial derivatives of the velocity are bounded
uniformly over
s
(Proposition
3
), there exists a compact
\widetilde{B}
such that uniformly over
initial conditions
z(0)=x
the trajectories
z(s)\in\widetilde{B},\forall s\in[0,1]
.
Therefore, we can focus on establishing uniformity over that compact.
Suppose that we are able to show that for some diverging positive sequence
a_{n}

there are uniformly bounded constants
C_{s}<C<\infty
such that for any
s\in[0,1]
almost surely we have

a_{n}\sup_{z\in\widetilde{B}}|\widehat{L}(s,z(s))-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(s,z(s))|\leq C_{s}<C,

(E.36)

with
\widehat{L}(s,z)
as in (
E.29
)
If (
E.36
) were to hold, since all the variables
X_{s}=sX_{1}+(1-s)X_{0}
are functions of the same pair
(X_{0},X_{1})
as we vary
s\in[0,1]
, the a.s. statement applies uniformly in
s
and we would conclude that a.s.

a_{n}\sup_{z\in\widetilde{B}}|\widehat{L}(s,z)-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(s,z))|<C\quad\forall s\in[0,1].

Since
a_{n}
diverges, this would imply that a.s.,

\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\sup_{z\in\widetilde{B}}|\widehat{L}(s,z)-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(s,z))|=0.

If, in addition, we can show that

\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\sup_{s\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}|\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{L}(s,z)\right)|=0,

(E.37)

then, we would deduce that a.s.

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\sup_{x\in B}|\widehat{L}(x)|\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\sup_{x\in B}\int_{0}^{1}|\widehat{L}(s,z(s))|ds\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\sup_{s\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}|\widehat{L}(s,z)|=0,

and the proof would be concluded. Let’s now show (
E.36
); we defer (
E.37
) to the proof of (b). To establish (
E.36
) we rely on Lemma
32
: it suffices to show that

\sup_{s\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}\mathbb{E}\left(\Big\lVert\Phi(1,s,z)\frac{\Delta-v(s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}\Big\rVert^{2}\Big\lvert X_{s}=z\right)p_{s}(z)<\infty,

a claim that follows since
\inf_{s\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}p_{s}(z)>0
(Lemma
18
),
\sup_{s\in[0,1],z\in\widetilde{B}}\lVert\Phi(1,s,z)\rVert<\infty
(Proposition
9
), and Lemma
17
.

For the bias analysis (b), note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(s,z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Delta_{i}-v(s,z)\right)K_{h}\left(X_{i}(s)-z\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}\int\int\left(\delta-v(s,z)\right)K_{h}\left(u-z\right)p_{0}(u-s\delta)p_{1}(u+(1-s)\delta)d\delta du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}\int\int\left(\delta-v(s,z)\right)K\left(u\right)p_{0}(z+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}\int K\left(u\right)\left(v(s,z+hu)-v(s,z)\right)p_{s}(z+hu)du.

The proof follows the classical analysis of the bias of kernel estimators
(
95
, Chapter 1)
of prescribed order. We perform a Taylor expansion of order
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
for the function

g_{s,z}(w):=\left(v(s,z+w)-v(s,z)\right)p_{s}(z+w)=f(s,z+w)-v(s,z)p_{s}(z+w),

(E.38)

around
w=0
, to control the difference
g_{s,z}(hu)-g_{s,z}(0)=g_{s,z}(hu)
. To avoid unnecessary notation, and since the argument extends easily, we will proceed as if the derivatives were unidimensional. We have that

g_{s,z}(hu)=\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}\frac{(uh)^{j}}{j!}g_{s,z}^{(j)}(0)+(uh)^{l}g_{s,z}^{(l)}(\tau uh),

where
\tau\in[0,1]
depends on
z,s,u,h
. Since the first
l-1
terms in the sum depend on
u
only through the term
(uh)^{j}
, and since the kernel is of order
l
, these terms vanish after integration with respect to
u
. While the last term does not vanish, we can still bound
g_{s,z}^{(l)}(\tau uh)
uniformly over
z\in B
and
s\in[0,1]
: indeed, by Lemma
6
and (
E.38
),
g_{s,z}(\cdot)
is the difference of two
\beta
-Hölder functions,
f(s,z+\cdot)
and
v(s,z)p_{s}(z+\cdot)
. Although the factor
v(s,z)
may be unbounded over
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it remains bounded under the constraint
z\in B
. Therefore

M:=\sup_{z\in B,s\in[0,1]}||g_{s,z}||_{\beta}<\infty.

Then, as
\int K(u)u^{l}du=0
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B}|\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B,s\in[0,1]}\int_{0}^{1}|\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(s,z(s))|ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Big\lvert\frac{\Phi(1,s,z(s))}{p_{s}(z(s))}\int K\left(u\right)g_{s,z(s)}(hu)duds\Big\rvert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Big\lvert\frac{\Phi(1,s,z(s))}{p_{s}(z(s))}\int K\left(u\right)\frac{(uh)^{l}}{l!}\left(g^{(l)}_{s,z(s)}(\tau hu)-g^{(l)}_{s,z(s)}(0)\right)duds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Big\lvert\frac{\Phi(1,s,z(s))}{p_{s}(z(s))}\int\lvert K\left(u\right)\rvert\frac{(uh)^{l}}{l!}M\lvert(\tau uh)^{\beta-l}\rvert duds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{\beta},

so we have established (
E.31
). In the last line, we used that
p_{s}(z_{s})
is bounded below (Lemma
18
) and that
\Phi(1,s,z(s))
is bounded. Note that we can apply the same argument to establish (
E.37
) (we only avoid integration over
s
).

It remains to show (c). Recall that
\widehat{f}(s,z)
and
\widehat{p}(s,z)
are sample averages of independent copies of the variables
\Delta_{i}K_{h}(I_{i}(s)-z)
and
K_{h}(I_{i}(s)-z)
, respectively. Therefore, using that
\text{Cov}(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=\text{Cov}(X,Y)/n
and upon calling

\gamma(t):=\frac{\Phi(1,t,z_{t})}{p_{t}(z_{t})},

we have

\displaystyle\text{Cov}\left(\widehat{L}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{Cov}\left(\gamma(s)\left(\widehat{f}(s,z_{s})-v(s,z_{s})\widehat{p}(s,z_{s})\right),\gamma(t)\left(\widehat{f}(t,z_{t})-v(t,z_{t})\widehat{p}(t,z_{t})\right)\right)dsdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{Cov}\left(\gamma(s)\left(\Delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)K_{h}\left(X_{s}-z_{s}\right),\gamma(t)\left(\Delta-v(t,z_{t})\right)K_{h}\left(X_{t}-z_{t}\right)\right)dsdt

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}\left(X_{s}-z_{s}\right)K_{h}\left(X_{t}-z_{t}\right)\left(\Delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)\left(\Delta-v(t,z_{t})\right)^{\top}\right)\gamma(t)^{\top}dsdt}_{\Sigma^{0}_{h}(x)}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left(\gamma(s)K_{h}\left(X_{s}-z_{s}\right)\left(\Delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\gamma(t)K_{h}\left(X_{t}-z_{t}\right)\left(\Delta-v(t,z_{t})\right)\right)^{\top}dsdt}_{\Omega_{h}(x)}.

Let’s analyze
\Sigma^{0}_{h}(x),\Omega_{2}(x)
separately. Regarding
\Omega_{h}(x)
, since
\lVert uv^{t}\rVert\leq\lVert u\rVert\lVert v\rVert
:

\displaystyle n\lVert\Omega_{h}(x)\rVert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}\left(X(s)-z_{s}\right)\gamma(s)\left(\Delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)\right)\rVert\lVert\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}\left(X_{t}-z_{t}\right)\gamma(t)\left(\Delta-v(t,z_{t})\right)\right)\rVert dsdt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h^{2}.

In the last line, we used the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}\left(X_{s}-z_{s}\right)\lVert\Delta-v(s,z_{s})\rVert\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lVert\delta-v(s,z_{s})\rVert p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)dud\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lVert\delta-v(s,z_{s})\rVert p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)dud\delta

\displaystyle+h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lVert\delta-v(s,z_{s}))\rVert\nabla p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)d\delta du

\displaystyle+h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lVert\delta-v(s,z_{s}))\rVert p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)\nabla p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)d\delta du

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle h.

In the last line we used that the first term in the sum of the second to last line is zero by the definition of
v(s,z_{s})
, and that the sum of the integrals is bounded (uniformly over
s
) by Lemma
6
.
Then, if we define

\Sigma_{h}(x):=nh^{d-1}\Sigma^{0}_{h}(x)

(E.39)

we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert nh^{d-1}\text{Cov}(\widehat{L}(x))-\Sigma_{h}(x)\lVert=\sup_{x\in B}h^{d-1}\lVert\Omega_{h}(x)\rVert=O(h^{d+1})=o(1).

The convergence analysis of
\Sigma_{h}(x)
is more delicate, and its bulk is contained in the separate Lemma
23
that we only invoke here. By the identity
X_{s}=X_{t}+(s-t)\Delta
, we can express the integral in
\Sigma^{0}_{h}(x)
in terms of
X_{t}
and
\Delta
only. Specifically,

\displaystyle n\Sigma^{0}_{h}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\mathbb{E}\left(K_{h}\left(X_{t}+(s-t)\Delta-z_{s}\right)K_{h}\left(X_{t}-z_{t}\right)\left(\Delta-v(s,z_{s}\right)\left(\Delta-v(t,z_{t}\right)^{\top}\right)\gamma(t)^{\top}dsdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\gamma(s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{h}\left(hu+(s-t)\delta+z_{t}-z_{s}\right)K\left(u\right)\left(\delta-v_{s}(z_{s})\right)\left(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)^{\top}\gamma(t)^{\top}

\displaystyle\times p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)dud\delta dsdt.

The above expression follows from i) the fact that the joint density of
\Delta,X_{t}
at
(\delta,z)
is
p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)
and ii) the change of variables
u=(X_{t}-z_{t})/h
. Considering the additional change of variable
\omega=(s-t)/h
, we obtain

\displaystyle n\Sigma_{h}^{0}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}\gamma(\omega h+t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{h}\left(hu+h\omega\delta+z_{t}-z_{\omega h+t}\right)K\left(u\right)\left(\delta-v_{\omega h+t}(z_{\omega h+t})\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)^{\top}\gamma(t)^{\top}p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)dud\delta d\omega dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{1-d}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)g(t,u,\delta,h)\left(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle\times p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)\gamma(t)^{\top}dud\delta dt,

where

\displaystyle g(t,u,\delta,h)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}\gamma(wh+t)K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{z_{\omega h+t}-z_{t}}{h}\right)\left(\delta-v_{\omega h+t}(z_{\omega h+t})\right)d\omega.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}\gamma(t)K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{z_{\omega h+t}-z_{t}}{h}\right)\left(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)d\omega

\displaystyle+\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}whK\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{z_{\omega h+t}-z_{t}}{h}\right)\frac{d}{dy}\left[\gamma(y)\left(\delta-v_{y}(z_{y})\right)\right]\Big\lvert_{y\in[t,t+\omega h]}d\omega.

Using the fact that the derivatives of
\gamma(y)
and
v_{y}(z_{y})
are bounded in the compact
[0,1]
(they are continuous), and that
|\omega h|\leq 1
, we derive the following component-wise bound for the entries of
g(t,u,\delta,h)
, that holds whenever the denominator is well-defined:

\displaystyle\lvert g_{i}(t,u,\delta,h)\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+\lVert\delta\rVert)\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{z_{\omega h+t}-z_{t}}{h}\right)d\omega

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lVert\delta\rVert)(1+\lVert u\rVert)}{\lvert\delta_{j}-v_{j}(t,z_{t})\rvert}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{|\delta_{j}-F^{j}_{t}(x_{j}(t,m))|^{1/2}},

where, in the last line, we used Lemma
23
, and the functions
F^{j}_{t}
and the points
x_{j}(t,m)
are the ones defined in this Lemma. Note that the above bound is valid for each
1\leq i,j\leq d
. We have found a bound on
g(t,u,\delta,h)
that is independent of
h
. We will use it to invoke dominated convergence on each coordinate of the matrix
\Sigma_{h}^{i,j}(x)
of
\Sigma_{h}(x)
. Indeed, we have (since
\Sigma^{i,j}_{h}(x):=\Sigma_{h}^{0,i,j}(x)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i,j}_{h}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lvert g_{i}(t,u,\delta,h)\rvert\lvert\delta_{j}-v_{j}(t,z_{t})\rvert p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)dud\delta dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)(1+\lVert\delta\rVert)(1+\lVert u\rVert)p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)dud\delta dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{(1+\lVert u\rVert)(1+\lVert\delta\rVert)}{|\delta_{j}-F^{j}_{t}(x_{j}(t,m))|^{1/2}}p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)dud\delta dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)(1+\lVert u\rVert)dt

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\infty

The second-to-last step above is not fully obvious, and we show it as a separate Lemma
22
. To invoke this Lemma, we used that
z_{t},u,h,F_{t}^{j}(x_{j}(t,m))
are uniformly bounded over
t
.

As a conclusion of the above discussion,
\Sigma^{i,j}_{h}(x)
converges to the integral of the integrand with respect to
t,\delta,u,\omega
when
h\rightarrow 0
. It only remains to evaluate this limit. We start with
g(t,u,\delta,h)
. Note that the integrals with respect to
\omega
become integrals in
[-\infty,\infty]
, except from the measure-zero set
\{0,1\}
. Also, by (
2
), the definition of derivative and using the continuity of
K
we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}K\left(u+\omega-\frac{z_{\omega h+t}-z_{t}}{h}\right)=K\left(u+\omega\left(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)\right).

The rest of the expressions containing
h
are all of the form
\omega h+t
, which converge pointwise to
t
. Therefore,
\Sigma(x):=\lim_{h\to 0}\Sigma_{h}(x)
satisfies

\displaystyle\Sigma(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}g(t,u,\delta,h)(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t}))^{\top}p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)\gamma(t)^{\top}dud\delta dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(u)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(t)K(u+\omega(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t})))(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t}))(\delta-v_{t}(z_{t}))^{\top}

\displaystyle\times p_{0}(z_{t}-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+(1-t)\delta)\gamma(t)^{\top}dud\delta dt

This last expression coincides with (
32
).
∎

Lemma 22
.

In the setup of Proposition
8
, if
u,h,z_{t},F_{t}(x_{j}(t,m))
are bounded uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1+\lVert\delta\rVert}{|\delta_{j}-F^{j}_{t}(x_{j}(t,m))|^{1/2}}p_{0}(z_{t}+hu-t\delta)p_{1}(z_{t}+hu+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\lesssim 1,

(E.40)

Proof.

Using that both
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
are
\alpha
-strongly log-concave, by completing squares we deduce that for each
z=z_{t}+hu

\displaystyle p_{0}(z-\delta t)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)\lesssim p_{0}(x_{0})p_{1}(x_{1})\exp\left(-a\lVert\delta-\delta_{0}\rVert^{2}+K\right),

where
a=\alpha(t^{2}+(1-t)^{2})/2
,
x_{0},x_{1}
are the minimizers of
p_{0},p_{1}
, and

\displaystyle\delta_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t)x_{1}-tx_{0}-(1-2t)z}{t^{2}+(1-t)^{2}}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\lVert z-x_{0}\lVert^{2}+\lVert z-z_{1}\rVert^{2}-\frac{\lVert(1-2t)z+tx_{0}-(1-t)x_{1}\rVert^{2}}{t^{2}+(1-t)^{2}}\right).

Note that as
z
is bounded, then both
\delta_{0}
and
K
are bounded uniformly in
t
. Likewise,
a
is bounded above and below by a constant greater than zero. This implies that in the bounds below, we can treat them as constants.
Likewise,
\delta_{0}
also depends on these quantities, but it is free from
\delta
. The above indicates that as long as we can produce the following bound

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\lVert\delta\rVert}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}\exp\left(-a\lVert\delta-\delta_{0}\rVert^{2}\right)d\delta<L_{y,\delta_{0}},

for
L_{y,\delta_{0}}
that is bounded if
y
and
\delta_{0}
are bounded, we can conclude (
E.40
). We finally show that this is the case. We will rely on the two following bounds: first, that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\exp\left(-a\lVert x-\mu\rVert^{2}\right)}{|x-x_{0}|^{1/2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|x-x_{0}|\leq 1}\frac{\exp\left(-a\lVert x-\mu\rVert^{2}\right)}{|x-x_{0}|^{1/2}}dx+\int_{|x-x_{0}|\geq 1}\frac{\exp\left(-a\lVert x-\mu\rVert^{2}\right)}{|x-x_{0}|^{1/2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|x-x_{0}|\leq 1}\frac{1}{|x-x_{0}|^{1/2}}dx+\int_{|x-x_{0}|\geq 1}\frac{\exp\left(-a\lVert x-\mu\rVert^{2}\right)}{|x-x_{0}|^{1/2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}},

which is a constant independent of
\mu,x_{0}
. Second, we use that

\int|x|\exp(-a||x-\mu||^{2})dx\lesssim\mu+1.

To use the above bounds, note first that
\lVert\delta\rVert\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{d}|\delta_{i}|
. Therefore, we can reduce the analysis to examining each integral with a
|\delta_{j}|
term in the numerator. For
j\neq i
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\delta_{j}|\exp\left(-a\lVert\delta-\delta_{0}\rVert^{2}\right)}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}}^{d-2}\int|\delta_{j}|\exp\left(-a\lvert\delta_{j}-\delta^{j}_{0}\rvert^{2}\right)d\delta_{j}\int\frac{\exp\left(-a\lvert\delta_{i}-\delta^{i}_{0}\rvert^{2}\right)}{\lvert\delta_{i}-y_{i}\rvert^{1/2}}d\delta_{i}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|\delta^{j}_{0}|+1,

The case of
j=i
is similar, we express

\frac{|\delta_{i}|}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|}\leq\frac{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|+|y_{i}|}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|}=1+\frac{|y_{i}|}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|},

implying that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\delta_{i}|\exp\left(-a\lVert\delta-\delta_{0}\rVert^{2}\right)}{|\delta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}d\delta\lesssim|y_{i}|+1,

∎

Lemma 23
.

Suppose that
K
is a kernel with bounded support, and let
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a twice continuously differentiable function with coordinates
f_{i}
. For each
1\leq i\leq d
define the functions

F^{i}_{t}(x):=\frac{f_{i}(t+x)-f_{i}(t)}{x},\quad x\in[-t,1-t].

Note that these are all uniformly bounded over
t,x
since
f_{i}^{\prime}
is bounded. Suppose that they satisfy the following conditions

•

Are twice differentiable, and their second derivatives are equicontinuous in
t
.

•

For each
t
,
F^{i}_{t}
has at most
M
critical points, where
M
is uniform in
d
and
t\in[0,1]
.

•

The critical points are uniformly non-denegerate, i.e. if

C(t,i)=\Big\{x\in[-t,1-t],(F^{i})^{\prime}_{t}(x)=0\Big\}.

is the set of such critical points, then, there is
L>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\bigcup_{t\in[0,1],1\leq i\leq d}C(t,i)}\Big\lvert(F_{t}^{i})^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x)\Big\rvert\geq L.

The above is equivalent to

\sup_{x\in\bigcup_{t\in[0,1],1\leq i\leq d}C(t,i)}\Big\lvert\frac{1}{x}f_{i}^{\prime\prime}(t+x)\Big\rvert>L.

If we denote by
x_{i}(t,m)
any enumeration of the critical points of
F^{i}_{t}
, for each
1\leq i\leq d,\delta,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the right-hand side below is well-defined, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\right)d\omega\lesssim\frac{1+||u||}{|\delta_{i}-f_{i}^{\prime}(t))|}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert\delta_{i}-F^{i}_{t}(x_{i}(t,m))\rvert}},

(E.41)

In the above bound, there are only hidden dependencies the support of the kernel and bounds on the first and second derivatives of
F_{i}(x)
.

Proof.

Since
K
is a bounded kernel, it suffices to bound the Lebesgue measure
\Lambda(S)
of the set
S\subseteq[-t/h,(1-t)/h]
where the evaluation of the kernel may be positive, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{-t/h}^{(1-t)/h}K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\right)d\omega\lesssim\lambda(S).

Specifically, let’s
R
be the size of a ball containing the support of the kernel. The condition
\omega\in S
implies, in addition to
-t\leq h\omega\leq 1-t
, that

K\left(u+\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\right)>0,

(E.42)

which in turn implies that

\Big\lVert\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\Big\rVert\leq\Big\lVert u+\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\Big\rVert+\lVert u\rVert\leq R+\lVert u\rVert.

Then, for each
i
,
S
is contained in the set

S_{i}=\Big\{\omega\in[-t,1-t],\Big\lvert\omega\delta_{i}-\frac{f_{i}(t+h\omega)-f_{i}(t)}{h}\Big\rvert\leq R+\lVert u\rVert\Big\},

(E.43)

and so
\lambda(S)\leq\lambda(S_{i})
.
The conclusion follows from bounding the Lebesgue measure of each of these sets, which we address in the following lemma

∎

Lemma 24
.

Under the setup of
23
. We have that

\lambda(S_{i})\lesssim\frac{1+||u||}{|\delta_{i}-f_{i}^{\prime}(t,z_{t})|}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert\delta_{i}-F^{i}_{t}(x_{i}(t,j))\rvert}}

Proof.

In this proof we drop the
i
indexing and denote
\delta,f,f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},F_{t}
any of the
\delta_{i},f^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime\prime}_{i},F^{i}_{t}
. Likewise, we denote by
S
any of the
S_{i}
in (
E.43
). We define the more generic sets, for
-t\leq a\leq b\leq 1-t
and
\widetilde{R}>0
,

S(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\Big\{\omega\in\left[\frac{a}{h},\frac{b}{h}\right]:\Big\lvert\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\Big\rvert\leq\widetilde{R}\Big\}.

By the definition of
F_{t}(x)
the condition
\omega\in S(\widetilde{R},a,b)
implies

\Big\lvert\omega\left(\delta-F_{t}(\omega\delta)\right)\Big\rvert=\Big\lvert\omega\delta-\frac{f(t+h\omega)-f(t)}{h}\Big\rvert\leq\widetilde{R}.

We want to control
