S=S(R+\lVert u\rVert,-t,1-t)
. This is not obvious since the above inequality is nonlinear in
\omega
. We will partition the interval of
[-t,1-t]
in a way so that on each of these sub-intervals the above measure is well controlled. Specifically, if
a_{0}=-t,\ldots a_{m},\ldots,a_{\widetilde{M}}=1-t
is a partition of the interval
[-t,1-t]
, then

S(R+\lVert u\rVert,t,1-t)=\bigcup_{m=0}^{\widetilde{M}-1}S(R+\lVert u\rVert,a_{m},a_{m+1}),

(E.44)

so that as long as the number of pieces is finite, we can bound
S
if we have individual bounds on the subintervals. We choose the partition consisting on the division of the
[-t,1-t]
interval induced by the critical points of
F_{t}
. This partition contains at most
M+1
subintervals. We bound the measure of each of these subintervals using Lemma
25
, from which we easily conclude.

∎

Lemma 25
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
23
, consider the restriction of
f(x)
and
F_{t}(x)=(f(x+t)-f(t))/x
to an interval
[a,b]\subseteq[-t,1-t]
such that
F_{t}(x)
is strictly monotonic on this interval, and that if there is a critical point of
F_{t}(x)
it must be either
a
or
b
, and such that if
a
and
b
are both critical points then
F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(a)F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(b)<0
.

Then, whenever
\delta\neq f^{\prime}(t),\delta\neq F_{t}(a),\delta\neq F_{t}(b)
,

\lambda\left(S(\widetilde{R},a,b)\right)\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\delta-F_{t}(a)|}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\delta-F_{t}(b)|}}\right).

Proof.

Let us define one additional function

G_{t}(x)=\frac{F_{t}(x)-f^{\prime}(t)}{x}.

Note that
G_{t}(x)
is uniformly bounded in
t
and
x\in[-t,1-t]
since
f^{\prime\prime}(t)
is bounded in
[0,1]
and

|G_{t}(x)|=\Big\lvert\frac{f(t+x)-f(t)-xf^{\prime}(t)}{x}\Big\rvert=|f^{\prime\prime}(\upsilon)|.

for some
\upsilon\in[t,t+x]\subseteq[0,1]
.
Since

G_{t}(x)=xF_{t}(x)+f^{\prime}(t)
, the condition
\omega\in S(\widetilde{R},a,b)
implies

\Big\lvert\omega^{2}hG_{t}(h\omega)-\omega\left(\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\right)\Big\rvert=\Big\lvert\omega\left(\delta-F_{t}(\omega\delta)\right)\Big\rvert\leq\widetilde{R}.

The above displays imply that for each index (w.l.g.,
\omega\neq 0
)

\Big\lvert\omega^{2}hG_{t}(\omega h)-\omega\left(\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\right)\Big\rvert\leq\widetilde{R}.

Write
A=A(\omega,\delta,t,h):=hG_{t}(\omega h)/(\delta-f^{\prime}(t))
. Note that the case
A=0
directly implies that
\lvert\omega\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|^{-1}
. So, assuming that
A\neq 0
, we can express the above as

\Big\lvert\left(\omega-\frac{1}{2A}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\Big\rvert\leq\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}.

(E.45)

We will carefully study this region. Consider first the set

D(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\{\omega\in S(\widetilde{R},a,b):\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert<4\lvert A\rvert\widetilde{R}\}.

In this set, the condition (
E.45
)
is equivalently stated as

\frac{1}{2A}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}\leq\omega\leq\frac{1}{2A}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}.

(E.46)

Suppose first that
A>0
. We will use repeatedly the fact that for any
x>0
and
y
such that
x+y\geq 0
,

\lvert\sqrt{x+y}-\sqrt{x}\rvert\leq\frac{\lvert y\rvert}{\sqrt{x}}.

(E.47)

By (
E.47
), the leftmost inequality in (
E.46
) implies that

-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f(t)\rvert}\lesssim\omega.

On the other hand, by (
E.47
) and owing to the fact that
\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert<4\lvert A\rvert\widetilde{R}
, the rightmost inequality in (
E.46
) implies

\omega\leq\frac{1}{A}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}-\frac{1}{2A}\lesssim\frac{1}{A}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f(t)\rvert}\lesssim\frac{4\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f(t)\rvert}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f(t)\rvert}\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f(t)\rvert}.

The case
A<0
is completely analogous. Therefore,

\Lambda(D(\widetilde{R},a,b))\lesssim\Lambda\left(|\omega|\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\right)\lesssim\widetilde{R}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|},

(E.48)

which is independent of
h
.
The above implies that we can now focus only on the complement
D^{c}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
of this set. In this complement, the set of
\omega
satisfying (
E.45
) is the set belonging to the union of the two sets defined below

S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\Bigg\{\omega\in D^{c}(\widetilde{R},a,b):\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}+\frac{1}{2A}\leq\omega\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}+\frac{1}{2A}\Bigg\},

and

S_{2}(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\Bigg\{\omega\in D^{c}(\widetilde{R},a,b):\frac{1}{2A}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}\leq\omega\leq\frac{1}{2A}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}\Bigg\},

Suppose
A>0
so
A=|A|
. In this case, the analysis of
S_{2}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
is simpler.
From (
E.47
) we deduce that if
\omega\in S_{2}
then

\lvert\omega\rvert\leq 2\lvert A\rvert\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}=2\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}.

Therefore, in this set
\omega
has an amplitude at most proportional to
\widetilde{R}\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert^{-1}
and so

\Lambda(S_{2}(\widetilde{R},a,b))\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}.

(E.49)

The analysis of
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
is much more delicate. We will show that
\omega
must be contained on an interval of small length, although the center can be large. By subtracting
1/A=(\delta-f^{\prime}(t))/hG_{t}(\omega h)
to the inequalities defining
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
and using (
E.47
) we have

\frac{-\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{4|A|^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}-\frac{1}{2|A|}\leq\omega-\frac{\delta-f^{\prime}(t)}{hG_{t}(\omega h)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4A^{2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert A\rvert\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}}-\frac{1}{2|A|}\leq\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}.

The above implies that

\frac{-\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\lesssim\frac{h\omega G_{t}(\omega h)-(\delta-f^{\prime}(t))}{hG_{t}(\omega h)}\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|},

Since
xG(x)+f^{\prime}(t)=F_{t}(x),
and since
G_{t}(x)
is bounded, the above, in turn, implies that whenever
\omega\in S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
then

\lvert F_{t}(\omega h)-\delta\rvert\lesssim h\frac{|G_{t}(\omega h)|\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\lesssim\frac{h\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}:=\widetilde{R}_{h}.

(E.50)

In what follows, we will show that the above inequality implies that
\omega
belongs to a set whose Lebesgue measure by a quantity independent on
h
(but depending on
\delta,\widetilde{R},a,b,f
and
t
). We will analyze several scenarios, depending on whether
\delta
is in the range of
F_{t}
, whether
F_{t}
is increasing or decreasing, and whether
a
or
b
are critical points.
We will first assume that
F_{t}
is increasing, that
b
is a critical point with
F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(b)<0
, and that
a
is not a critical point. Other scenarios will reduce to this one.

We will analyze different ranges for
\delta
. On each of them, will also separate
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
into two sub-intervals,
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)\cap\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
with

\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\{\omega\in S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b);a\leq h\omega\leq h\omega_{\kappa}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)=\{\omega\in S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b);h\omega_{\kappa}<h\omega\leq b\},

where
\kappa
is the length of the interval anticipated in Lemma
27
and
\omega_{\kappa}:=(b-\kappa)/h
, so that
\lvert h\omega-b\rvert>\kappa
on
\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
and
\lvert h\omega-b\rvert<\kappa
on
\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
. By Lemma
27
, on
\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
,
h\omega
is far from the critical point
b
and so
F^{\prime}_{t}(h\omega)>L^{\prime}>0
for some constant
L^{\prime}>0
that depends only on the family
F_{t}
.
Therefore, if
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, we have that whenever
\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(h\omega_{2})\rvert\lesssim Q

\lvert h\omega_{1}-h\omega_{2}\lvert\leq\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(h\omega_{2})\rvert\lesssim Q.

(E.51)

Contrarily, on
\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
,
h\omega
is near the critical point
b
and so by Lemma
27

F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(h\omega)<-L/2
, where
L
is the uniform lower bound on the absolute second derivatives at critical points. Then, if
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, by Lemma
26
we have the relations

\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{L}{2}}\lvert h\omega_{1}-h\omega_{2}\rvert\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}\leq\frac{L}{2}|h\omega_{1}-b\rvert\lvert h\omega_{1}-h\omega_{2}\rvert\leq\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(h\omega_{2})\rvert,

that imply that whenever
\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(h\omega_{2})\rvert\lesssim Q

\lvert h\omega_{1}-h\omega_{2}\lvert\lesssim\frac{Q}{\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{1})-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}.

(E.52)

and

\lvert h\omega_{1}-h\omega_{2}\rvert\lesssim\frac{Q}{\lvert h\omega_{1}-b\rvert}.

(E.53)

Equipped with this, we will show that regardless of the value of
\delta
, we will have

\Lambda(S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))=\Lambda(\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))+\Lambda(\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\frac{1}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}.

(E.54)

Case 1:
\delta<F_{t}(a)

if
\omega\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, since
h\omega>a
, so
\delta<F_{t}(a)<F_{t}(h\omega)
, and by (
E.50
)

\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(a)\lvert<\lvert F_{t}(\omega h)-\delta\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}.

Therefore, since
a\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, by (
E.51
) the above implies that

\lvert h\omega-a\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}.

If
\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
,
h\omega\geq h\omega_{\kappa}>a
and
\delta<F_{t}(a)<F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})<F_{t}(h\omega)
, so by (
E.50
),

\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})\lvert<\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-\delta\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}.

and since both
\omega,\omega_{\kappa}\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, and since
h\omega_{\kappa}-b=\kappa>0
, by (
E.53
) the above implies that

\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\kappa}\rvert\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}_{h}}{\lvert h\omega_{\kappa}-b\rvert}\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}.

Let’s compute the Lebesgue measure of the sets implied by the above conditions. If
\omega\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
,
|h\omega-a\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}
, so
\omega
lies on an interval centered at a moving
a/h
but with constant length. Therefore,

\Lambda(\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))\leq\Lambda\Big(\omega\in\Big[\frac{a}{h}-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert},\frac{a}{h}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}\Big]\Big)\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert},

If
\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
then by the same argument (the interval is centered now at
\omega_{\kappa}
) we have the same bound for the size of the set. Then,

\Lambda(S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))=\Lambda(\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))+\Lambda(\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}.

and so (
E.54
) holds.

Case 2:
F_{t}(a)\leq\delta<F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})

Suppose that
\omega\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
. Let
\omega_{\delta}
be the unique such that
F_{t}(h\omega_{\delta})=\delta
. We have
a\leq h\omega_{\delta}\leq h\omega_{\kappa}
, i.e.,
\omega_{\delta}\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
.
Then, by (
E.50
),(
E.51
) we have

\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\delta}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(h\omega_{\delta})\rvert=\frac{1}{L^{\prime}}\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-\delta\rvert\leq\widetilde{R}_{h}.

If
\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, then, since
\delta<F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})
, and by similar arguments as in Case 1, we pivot on
h\omega_{\kappa}
to obtain

\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})\lvert<\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-\delta\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h},

so that by (
E.51
),

\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\kappa}\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}.

In this case, the total measure is bounded by

\Lambda(S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))=\Lambda(\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))+\Lambda(\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b))\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|},

so (
E.54
) holds.

Case 3:
F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})\leq\delta<F_{t}(b)

If
\omega\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, we pivot on
\omega_{\kappa}
to use (
E.50
) and (
E.51
). Specifically, since
F_{t}(h\omega)<F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})<\delta
, in this case

\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(h\omega_{\kappa})\lvert<\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-\delta\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h},

which implies that
\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\kappa}\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}
.

Now, if
\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, define
\omega_{\delta}
as before, since
h\omega_{\kappa}<h\omega_{\delta}<b
, we have that
\omega,\omega_{\delta}\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
. Then,

\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-F_{t}(h\omega_{\delta})\rvert=\lvert F_{t}(h\omega)-\delta\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h},

Therefore, by (
E.50
) and (
E.52
)

\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\delta}\lvert\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}_{h}}{\lvert F_{t}(h\omega_{\delta})-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}=\frac{\widetilde{R}_{h}}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}=\frac{h\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)||\delta-F_{t}(b)|^{1/2}}.

Combining the bounds, we control the Lebesgue measure as before, and (
E.54
) holds.

Case 4:
\delta>F_{t}(b)

In this case, if
\omega\in\underline{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
it is implied that
\lvert h\omega-h\omega_{\kappa}\rvert\lesssim\widetilde{R}_{h}
, by the same argument as in Case 3. If
\omega\in\bar{S}_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
then, by (
E.50
) and since
b
is a local maximum

\frac{L}{2}\lvert h\omega-b\rvert^{2}\lesssim F_{t}(b)-F_{t}(h\omega)\lesssim F_{t}(b)-(\delta-\widetilde{R}_{h}).

In particular, for the set defined by the above inequality to be non-empty,
\delta-F_{t}(b)\leq\widetilde{R}_{h}
.
Up to constants, we conclude that
\omega
lies on an interval of length

l(h)=\frac{1}{h}\sqrt{\widetilde{R}_{h}-(\delta-F_{t}(b))}=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{R}}{h\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}-\frac{\delta-F_{t}(b)}{h^{2}}}

if
\delta-F_{t}(b)\leq\widetilde{R}_{h}
,
l(h)=0
otherwise. This length function admits a bound independent of
h
. Indeed, the function
g(x)=A/x-B/x^{2}
for
x>0
is increasing if
x\leq 2B/A
and decreasing if
2B/A
. Therefore,
x^{\ast}=2B/A
is the unique maximum, and
g(x^{\ast})=A^{2}/4B
. Taking
A=\widetilde{R}/|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|
and
B=\delta-F_{t}(b)
we obtain that

l(h)\lesssim\sqrt{\left(\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{4(\delta-F_{t}(b))}}\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{\lvert\delta-f^{\prime}(t)\rvert}\frac{1}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}.

We have concluded that in all cases (
E.54
) holds. In summary, by (
E.54
),(
E.49
) and (
E.48
) we have provided bounds for the Lebesgue measure for of the sets
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
and
S_{2}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
, and
D(\widetilde{R},a,b)
so that we can bound

\Lambda\left(S(\widetilde{R},a,b)\right)\leq\Lambda\left(S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)\right)+\Lambda(S_{2}\left(\Lambda(\widetilde{R},a,b\right))+\Lambda\left(D(\widetilde{R},a,b)\right)\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\frac{1}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}.

Now, it remains to analyze possible scenarios. Note first that we have assumed that
A>0
. But if
if, contrarily,
A<0
, then
A=-|A|
, and we can sketch the same argument switching the roles of
S_{1}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
and
S_{2}(\widetilde{R},a,b)
(for the analysis of
D(\widetilde{R},a,b)
we already considered these two scenarios). Also, so far we assumed that
F
is increasing and
b
is the only critical point of
F
. If
F
was decreasing, the argument is essentially the same, as we can do the same case analysis in inverted ordering. Additionally, if
a
is also a critical point (if
F
is increasing, it must be a local minimum), then, again, we can replicate this case analysis: we now divide the
[a,b]
interval into
[a,\omega_{\kappa}^{a}],[\omega_{\kappa}^{a},\omega_{\kappa}^{b}]
and
[\omega_{\kappa}^{b},\delta]
where
\omega_{\kappa}^{a}=a+\delta,\omega_{\kappa}^{b}=b-\delta
and bound the measures of each of these sets for different values of
\delta
. These can all be controlled by the same arguments as we did before, yielding the same final bound, but this time we must include a new term including the contribution of
a
. Therefore,

\Lambda\left(S(\widetilde{R},a,b)\right)\lesssim\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\frac{1}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(b)\rvert^{1/2}}+\frac{\widetilde{R}}{|\delta-f^{\prime}(t)|}\frac{1}{\lvert\delta-F_{t}(a)\rvert^{1/2}}.

∎

Lemma 26
.

Suppose that on the interval
0<b-x<\kappa
,
F(x)
is strictly increasing,
F^{\prime}(b)=0
, and that
F^{\prime\prime}(x)<-M
for some constant
M>0
(therefore,
b
is a local minimum). Then, for any
x_{1},x_{2}
such that
|x_{1}-b|<\delta,|x_{2}-b|\leq\delta
,

\frac{\sqrt{M}}{2}\lvert x_{2}-x_{1}\rvert\lvert F(b)-F(x_{1})\rvert^{1/2}\leq\frac{M}{2}\lvert x_{2}-x_{1}\rvert\lvert b-x_{1}\rvert\leq\lvert F(x_{2})-F(x_{1})\rvert.

The same conclusion applies if the function is striclty decreasing,
F^{\prime}(b)=0
and
F^{\prime\prime}(x)>M
for some
M>0
.

Proof.

Let’s assume first that
x_{1}<x_{2}
. By a second-order Taylor expansion on
F
around
x_{2}
and a first order expansion for
F
around
b
, we have that for some
\psi_{1}\in[x_{1},x_{2}]
and
\psi_{2}\in[x_{2},b]

\displaystyle F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}-x_{2})\left(F^{\prime}(x_{2})+\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{1})\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}-x_{2})\left(F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{2})(x_{2}-b)+\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{1})\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)\right)

Then, inverting signs in the above, and using that
-F(x)>M
on that interval and that
x_{1}<x_{2}<b
, the above implies

\displaystyle F(x_{2})-F(x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{2}-x_{1})\left(F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{2})(x_{2}-b)+\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{1})\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\left(\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{2})(x_{2}-b)+\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{1})\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\left(-\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{2})(b-x_{2})-\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{1})\left(x_{2}-x_{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\frac{M}{2}\left(b-x_{2}+x_{2}-x_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{M}{2}\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\lvert b-x_{1}\rvert.

We obtain the final bound using that, by second-order approximation for
F
around
b

|F(x_{1})-F(b)|=|F^{\prime\prime}(\psi)||x_{1}-b|^{2}>M|x_{1}-b|^{2}

If now
x_{1}>x_{2}
, we can exchange the roles to obtain

F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})\geq\frac{M}{2}\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\lvert b-x_{2}\rvert\geq\frac{M}{2}\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert\lvert b-x_{1}\rvert\geq\frac{\sqrt{M}}{2}\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert|F(x_{1})-F(b)|^{1/2},

where we used that
x_{1}>x_{2}
so that
b-x_{2}>b-x_{1}>0
.
The conclusion follows since
x_{1}<x_{2}
is equivalent to
F(x_{1})<F(x_{2})
. The proof for decreasing
F
follows by applying the same argument to
-F
.
∎

Lemma 27
.

Suppose that
F_{t}(x)
is a coordinate of a function satisfying the conditions of Lemma
23
. Then,
There is
\kappa>0
such that for each
t
and critical point
x(t)
,

0<\frac{L}{2}\leq\max_{|x-x(t)|<\delta}\Big\lvert F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(x)\Big\rvert.

Also, there is a constant
L^{\prime}>0
such that

\inf_{|x-x(t)|>\delta}\Big\lvert F^{\prime}_{t}(x)\Big\rvert>L^{\prime}.

Proof.

We first analyze the behavior around a critical point. Suppose that
F_{t}(x(t))>0
(the other case is analogous).
By the uniform equicontinuity of the second derivative, we have that for some
\delta>0
, whenever
|x-y|\leq\delta
, we have

\Big\lvert F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(x)-F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(y)\Big\rvert\leq\frac{L}{2}.

Then, choosing
y=x(t)
we obtain that if
|x-x(t)|\leq\delta
then

\frac{L}{2}\leq-\frac{L}{2}+F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(x(t))\leq F^{\prime\prime}_{t}(x).

Now, regarding the first derivative, for each
t
, define
L_{t}
as the infimum of
|F^{\prime}_{t}(x)|
in the region
|x-x(t)|\geq\delta.
We need to show that
\inf_{t\in[0,1]}L_{t}>0
. Then, for some sequence
t_{n}\rightarrow t^{\ast}
we have
L_{t_{n}}\to 0
. By continuity of
F_{t}
and compactness, the infimum is realized on a certain
x_{t_{n}}
satisfying
|x_{t_{n}}-x(t_{n})|>\delta
(
x_{t_{n}}
is away from any critical point of
F_{t_{n}}
). The sequence
x_{t_{n}}
must have an accumulation point
x^{\ast}=x_{t^{\ast}}
. By equicontinuity, this implies
L_{t_{n}}=F_{t_{n}}(x_{t_{n}})\to F(x_{t^{\ast}})
, so that
F(x_{t^{\ast}})=0
. Then,
x_{t^{\ast}}
must be a critical point of
F_{t^{\ast}}
, contradicting that
x_{t^{\ast}}
is away from the critical points of
F_{t^{\ast}}
.
∎

S.5.8
Proof of Corollary
1

Proof of Corollary
1
.

We first show that in this case,
R(x)
coincides with optimal transport. To see this, note first that by Lemma
34

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Phi(1,s,z_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\Phi(1,s,z_{s})v(s,z_{s})-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\Phi(1,s,z_{s})+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{d}{ds}z_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\Phi(1,s,z_{s}).

Therefore, we can write, for
0\leq s\leq 1

\Phi(1,s,z_{s})=\exp\left(\int_{s}^{1}\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z_{t})dt\right).

In particular, for
s=0
we obtain

R^{\prime}(x)=\Phi(1,0,x)>0
. Therefore,
R
is an increasing transport map, so it must be the optimal transport.

Now, let’s prove the remaining claims. Note that if we can establish the improved bias rate
h_{n}^{\beta+1}
then the CLT follows easily, as all the arguments for the variance analysis don’t depend on the dimension. We only need to establish this rate and show that the asymptotic variance doesn’t depend on the Kernel.

To establish the rate, recall first that, as in the proof of Proposition
8
, we need to bound the bias

\displaystyle b(h):=\mathbb{E}(\widehat{L}(x))=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}K\left(u\right)\left(v(s,z+hu)-v(s,z)\right)p_{s}(z+hu)duds.

We will use the following result, as stated in Lemma
35
(note that we are allowed to compute first derivatives since
\beta>2
).

u\frac{d}{ds}\left(\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)=-\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\frac{d}{dh}m(u,h,s),

(E.55)

where

m(u,h,s)=\left(v(s,z_{s}+hu)-v(s,z_{s})\right)p_{s}(z_{s}+hu).

This implies that the function

b_{0}(h,u)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi(1,s,z)}{p_{s}(z)}K(u)m(u,h,s)ds

is differentiable with respect to
h
at all
u
, with derivative

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dh}b_{0}(h,u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-uK(u)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\left(\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle uK(u)\left(\Phi(1,0,x)\frac{p_{0}(x+hu)}{p_{0}(x)}-\Phi(1,1,z_{1})\frac{p_{1}(R(x)+hu)}{p_{1}(R(x))}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle uK(u)\left(\Phi(1,0,x)\frac{p_{0}(x+hu)}{p_{0}(x)}-\frac{p_{1}(R(x)+hu)}{p_{1}(R(x))}\right)

At this point, the argument is the same as in usual kernel density estimation; by using the Hölder regularity of
p_{0},p_{1}
now we can demonstrate that
b_{0}(h)
has
\beta+1
Hölder regularity with respect to
h
and use it on the Taylor expansions as in the proof of Proposition
8
. Exchanging derivatives and integration with respect to
u
is justified by continuity of
\Phi,p_{0},p_{1}
and boundedness of the kernel.

It only remains to show (
33
). To do so, we consider the change of variables
\omega_{1}=u+\omega(\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t}))
in the integral with respect to
\omega
in (
32
), whenever
(\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t}))\neq 0
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\Sigma(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi(1,t,z_{t})}{p_{t}(z_{t})}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left(\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\right)^{2}}{\lvert\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\rvert}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K\left(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\right)K\left(u\right)dud\omega_{1}\Big|X_{t}=z_{t}\right]\Phi(1,t,z_{t})dt.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi^{2}(1,t,z_{t})}{p_{t}(z_{t})}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\rvert\Big|X_{t}=z_{t}\right]dt,

a quantity that doesn’t depend on
K
.
∎

S.5.9
Proof of Example
Example

Proof of Example
Example
.

We can easily extend the argument in the proof of Proposition
1
to show that if
X_{i}\sim N\left(m_{i},\Sigma_{i}\right)
are independent, then
z_{t}=z_{t}(x)=z(t,0,x)
satisfies

z_{t}(x)=m_{t}+\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}\left(\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\right)^{1/2}\Sigma_{0}^{-1/2}\left(x-m_{0}\right).

Also,

v(t,z_{t})=m_{1}-m_{0}+(t\Sigma_{1}-(1-t)\Sigma_{0})\left(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)^{-1}(z_{t}-m_{t}),

and since
X_{t}\sim N\left(m_{t},t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)
, in the one dimensional case we get

\displaystyle\Phi(1,t,z_{t})=\exp\left(\int_{t}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{t}^{1}(s\Sigma_{1}-(1-s)\Sigma_{0})\left(s^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-s)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)^{-1}ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\Sigma_{1}\right)\right)}{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\left(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}\right)^{-1/2}.

Additionally, it is easy to check that the distribution of
\Delta=X_{1}-X_{0}
conditional on
X_{t}=z_{t}
is Gaussian with mean
m
and variance
\Sigma
given by

m:=m_{0}-m_{1}+\frac{s\Sigma_{1}-(1-s)\Sigma_{0}}{s^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-s)^{2}\Sigma_{0}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\Sigma:=\frac{1}{t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}(\lvert\Delta-v(t,z_{t})\lvert\Big|X_{t}=z_{t})=\sqrt{\frac{2\Sigma}{\pi}}=\frac{1}{(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}},

and

\displaystyle\Sigma(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Phi^{2}(1,t,z_{t})}{p_{t}(z_{t})}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\Delta-v_{t}(z_{t})\rvert\Big|X_{t}=z_{t}\right]ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Sigma_{1}\sqrt{2\pi}(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{1/2}}{t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}}\exp\left(\frac{(z_{t}-m_{t})^{2}}{2(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})}\right)\frac{1}{(t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0})^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{t^{2}\Sigma_{1}+(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{0}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\Sigma_{0}}(x-m_{0})^{2}\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}}{\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}}\left(\arctan\left(\frac{\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}}{\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}}\right)+\arctan\left(\frac{\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}}{\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}}\right)\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\Sigma_{0}}(x-m_{0})^{2}\right)

∎

S.5.10
Additional results for Section
5

Lemma 28

(Lemma 2.2 in
48
)
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying the Poincaré inequality (
E.23
) with constant
\sigma^{2}>0
. If
h
is a locally Lipschitz function with
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}(h)=0
, then, for any
p\geq 2
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left(\lvert h(X)\rvert^{p}\right)\leq\left(\frac{\sigma p}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left(\lVert\nabla h(X)\rVert^{p}\right).

(E.56)

Lemma 29

(Lyapunov CLT)
.

Let
U_{n,i}
by a triangular array in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean zero and finite second moment. Define
V_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{Cov}(U_{n,i})
and let
v_{n}^{2}=\lambda_{min}(V_{m})
(the least eigenvalue of
V_{n}
) If for some
\delta>0
we have that

\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2+\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left(|U_{n,i}^{2+\delta}|\right)=o(1),

then,

V_{n}^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{n,i}\rightarrow N\left(0,I_{d}\right).

Lemma 30

(Gronwall’s inequality)
.

If
u
and
\beta
are differentiable and continuous real-valued functions defined on an interval
I
such that
u^{\prime}(t)\leq\beta(t)u(t)
in the interior of
I
, then

u(t)\leq u(t_{0})\exp\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\beta(s)ds\right).

Lemma 31
.

Let
Y,X
be random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consider the problem of estimating the conditional mean
\mu(x)=\mathbb{E}(Y|X=x)
from i.i.d pairs
(X_{0i},X_{1i})
. Suppose that
X
has a bounded density
p
which is of Hölder class
\beta
and that
K
is a kernel of order
l=\left\lfloor{\beta}\right\rfloor
. Assume that
h=o(1)
,
nh^{d}\rightarrow\infty
. Assume also that for some
\delta>0
and every
x

\mathbb{E}(|Y-\mu(X)|^{2+\delta}|X=x)<M,\quad\int|K(\psi)|^{2+\delta}d\psi<\infty.

Then, the classical Nadaraya-Watson kernel density estimator
\widehat{\mu}_{h}(x)
of
\mu(x)

\widehat{\mu}_{h}(x)\equiv\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{h}(X_{0i}-x)X_{1i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{h}(X_{0i}-x)},

satisfies a central limit theorem

\sqrt{nh^{d}}(\widehat{\mu}_{h}(x)-\mu_{h}(x))\rightsquigarrow N\left(0,\frac{\big\|K\big\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}{p(x)}\Sigma(x)\right),

where
\mu_{h}=\mu(x)+O(h^{\beta})
and
\Sigma(x)\equiv\mathrm{Var}(Y|X=x)=\mathbb{E}(Y^{2}|X=x)-\mathbb{E}(Y|X=x)^{2}
.
The proof is standard (e.g. see
68
;
96
) and we skip it.

Lemma 32
.

Let
(X_{0i},X_{1i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
be an i.i.d sequence of random variables. For fixed continuous functions
c
and
d
define

W(z)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(c(z)X_{1i}+d(z)\right)K_{h}(X_{0i}-z)-\mathbb{E}\left(\left(c(z)X_{1i}+d(z)\right)K_{h}(X_{0i}-z)\right).

Then, over any compact
B
, with probability one,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{\frac{2nh_{n}^{d}}{\log h_{n}^{-1}}}\sup_{z\in B}||W(z)||=\sup_{z\in B}\mathbb{E}\left(\left(c(z)Y+d(z)\right)^{2}|X=z\right)p(z)||K||_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(E.57)

Proof.

This is a simple multivariate extension of Theorem 1 in
37
. Extension of closely related results to the multivariate case has already been pursued in e.g
(
45
, Proposition 3.1)
, so we skip the details.
∎

Lemma 33
.

Let
\widehat{v}(t,z)
be the kernel-based estimator of the velocity. Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
7
we have that for
l=0,1,2
, and
R=O_{p}(1)
,

\sup_{t\in[0,1],\lVert z\rVert\leq R}\Big\lVert\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}v(t,z)\Big\rVert=O_{p}\left(h^{\beta-l}+\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nh_{n}^{d+2l}}}\right)

Proof.

The proof is standard so we only sketch the high-level idea. For
l=0
the results follow by a linearization of the kernel regression estimator in the same line as the proof of Theorem
7
to reduce the analysis of the ratio to a linear quantity as the one appearing in Lemma
32
. The deviations of this linearized term can be controlled using (
E.57
) giving the logarithmic term. The term
h^{\beta}
appears from the usual bias analysis.
The analysis of derivatives is also standard. The key observation is that (for
l=1
)

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{v}(t,z)-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(t,z)=\frac{1}{\widehat{p}(t,z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{f}(t,z)-\frac{1}{p(t,z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}f(t,z)-\left(\frac{\widehat{v}(t,z)}{\widehat{p}(t,z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\widehat{p}(t,z)-\frac{v(t,z)}{p(t,z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}p(t,z)\right).

The analysis of the differences above is similar to the one for the original kernel regression estimator, but this one contains derivatives of the kernel. Each kernel differentiation contributes a
h^{-1}
term to the variance and bias analysis (see e.g. the proof of Theorem 6 in
53
for details in the heuristics).
∎

Lemma 34
.

Let
\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})
be as in (
34
). It satisfies the matrix linear ODE (in
t
, with
t_{0},z_{0}
fixed)
(
15
)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0}),\quad\Phi(t_{0},t_{0},z_{0})=I_{d}.

(E.58)

If, additionally,
v(s,z)
is twice continuously differentiable, then the derivatives of
\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})
with respect to
t_{0}
and
z_{0}
satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial z^{2}}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})+\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\partial t_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0})v(t_{0},z_{0})-\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t_{0},z_{0}).

We note the abuse of notation in expressing the above matrix-product-like formula, as some quantities are tensor-like structures. For our purposes, this detail is immaterial: since we will ultimately use these expressions to demonstrate that some functions of the above derivatives are bounded, we will rely on coordinate-wise analysis.

Proof.

These results are classical (e.g.
15
) and we only provide a brief sketch. It is known that
(
15
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial z}{\partial t_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})v(t_{0},z_{0}).

where
\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})
satisfies (
E.58
). By differentiating this equation with respect to
z_{0}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial z^{2}}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})+\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t,z(t,t_{0},z_{0}))\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0}).

Likewise, differentiation with respect to
t_{0}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\partial t_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}z}{\partial z_{0}\partial t_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{0}}\left(\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})v(t_{0},z_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{0}}(t,t_{0},z_{0})v(t_{0},z_{0})-\Phi(t,t_{0},z_{0})\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}(t_{0},z_{0}).

∎

Lemma 35
.

Suppose that
p_{0},p_{1}
are differentiable. Under the assumptions of Theorem
6
, in the one-dimensional case,

u\frac{d}{ds}\left(\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)=-\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\frac{d}{dh}m(u,h,s),

where
m(u,h,s)=\left(v(s,z_{s}+hu)-v(s,z_{s})\right)p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)
.

Proof of Lemma
35
.

Recall first that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Phi(1,s,z_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\Phi(1,s,z_{s}).

also, by Lemma
36

\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s})=-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\right)p_{s}(z_{s}).

Therefore,

\frac{d}{ds}\left(\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)=\frac{\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})},

and consequently

\displaystyle u\frac{d}{ds}\left(\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-u\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle u\Phi(1,s,z_{s})\left(\frac{\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\frac{p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(1,s,z_{s})}{p_{s}(z_{s})}u\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu).

It only remains to identify the above derivative as a derivative of
m
with respect to
h
. This is true since

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d}{ds}\left(p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{d}{ds}z_{s}-\delta\right)p^{\prime}_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(v(s,z_{s})-\delta\right)p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p^{\prime}_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{d}{ds}z_{s}-\delta\right)p^{\prime}_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(v(s,z_{s})-\delta\right)p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p^{\prime}_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta.

and, on the other hand,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dh}m(u,h,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)\frac{d}{dh}p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)p^{\prime}_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle+u\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\delta-v(s,z_{s})\right)p_{0}(z_{s}+hu-s\delta)p^{\prime}_{1}(z_{s}+hu+(1-s)\delta)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-u\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s}+hu).

∎

Lemma 36
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6

\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s})=-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\right)p_{s}(z_{s})

(E.59)

Proof.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\frac{d}{ds}z_{s}-\delta\right)\cdot\left(\nabla p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)+p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)\nabla p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)\right)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(v_{s}-\delta\right)\cdot\left(\nabla p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)+p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)\nabla p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)\right)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(v_{s}-\delta\right)\cdot f(\delta,s,z_{s})d\delta

where we have defined the vector-valued function

f(\delta,s,z_{s})=\nabla p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)+p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)\nabla p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)

Note that the derivative of the velocity with respect to the
z
coordinate is the following matrix

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\int\delta p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}

(E.60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})\delta^{T}d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}-\frac{\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}\frac{\int\delta^{T}p_{0}(z_{s}-s\delta)p_{1}(z_{s}+(1-s)\delta)d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})\delta^{T}d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}-\frac{\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})d\delta}{p_{s}(z_{s})}v_{s}(z_{s})^{T}

Then,

\displaystyle p_{s}(z_{s})\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v(s,z_{s})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})\left(\delta-v_{s}(z_{s})\right)^{T}d\delta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\int f(\delta,s,z_{s})\cdot\left(\delta-v_{s}(z_{s})\right)d\delta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{ds}p_{s}(z_{s})

∎

S.6
Proofs of Results in Section
6

For any set-valued mapping
t\mapsto A(t)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define limsup and liminf in the Painlevé-Kuratowski sense as

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to t_{0}}A(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v:\,\limsup_{t\to t_{0}}\,\mbox{dist}(v,A(t))=0\right\},

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to t_{0}}A(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v:\,\liminf_{t\to t_{0}}\,\mbox{dist}(v,\,A(t))=0\right\}.

(See Definition 1.1.1 of
4
.) The (contingent) tangent cone of a closed set
\Omega
at a point
x\in\Omega
is defined as

T_{\Omega}(x):=\limsup_{h\to 0+}\,\frac{\Omega-x}{h}.

(See Definition 4.1.1 of
4
.)

Proposition 10

(Properties of
S_{t}(z)
)
.

Suppose assumption
(B1)
holds. Fix
z\in\Omega
. Define

\displaystyle L_{t}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,z-t\delta\in\Omega\},

\displaystyle U_{t}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,z+(1-t)\delta\in\Omega\},

and

S_{t}(z):=L_{t}(z)\cap U_{t}(z).

Then the following statements hold:

1.

for all
t\in(0,1)
,

L_{t}(z)~\subseteq~-T_{\Omega}(z),\quad\mbox{and}\quad U_{t}(z)~\subseteq~T_{\Omega}(z).

Moreover, for
t\leq\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)/\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
, then

L_{t}(z)\subseteq U_{t}(z)\quad\Rightarrow\quad S_{t}(z)=U_{t}(z).

In general,

S_{t}(z)\subseteq\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)-\mathrm{dist}(z,\,\partial\Omega)}{\max\{t,1-t\}}\right).

2.

The set-valued map
t\mapsto L_{t}(z):=\{\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,z-t\delta\in\Omega\}
is non-decreasing in
t\in[0,1]
(i.e.,
L_{t}(z)\subseteq L_{t^{\prime}}(z)
if
t\leq t^{\prime}
) but not continuous at
t=0
, unless
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
. (Here continuity is in the Painlevé-Kuratowski sense.) Similarly, the map
t\mapsto U_{t}(z)
is non-increasing in
t\in[0,1]
, but not continuous at
t=1
, unless
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
.

3.

For any non-negative function
(t,\delta)\to f(t,\delta)
continuous in
t\in[0,1]
satisfying

\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{-T_{\Omega}(z)}f(t,\delta)d\delta=\int_{-T_{\Omega}(z)}f(0,\delta)d\delta,

we have

\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{S_{t}(z)}f(t,\delta)d\delta=\int_{(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap(\Omega-z)}f(0,\delta)d\delta.

Similarly, if

\lim_{t\to 1}\int_{T_{\Omega}(z)}f(t,\delta)d\delta=\int_{T_{\Omega}(z)}f(1,\delta)d\delta,

then

\lim_{t\to 1}\int_{S_{t}(z)}f(t,\delta)d\delta=\int_{T_{\Omega}(z)\cap(z-\Omega)}f(1,\delta)d\delta.

4.

For any
t\in(0,1)
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
with

\|z-z^{\prime}\|~\leq~\gamma~\leq~\bar{t}\min\{\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial\Omega),\,\mathrm{dist}(z^{\prime},\partial\Omega)\},

(E.61)

we have

(S_{t}(z))^{-2\gamma/(t(1-t))}~\subseteq~S_{t}(z^{\prime})~\subseteq~(S_{t}(z))^{2\gamma/(t(1-t))}.

Additionally, for any
t\in(0,1),h>0
such that
t+h\in(0,1)
and
z\in\Omega
,

(S_{t}(z))^{-2\mathrm{diam}^{2}(\Omega)h/(\varepsilon\bar{t})}\subseteq S_{t+h}(z)\subseteq(S_{t}(z))^{2\mathrm{diam}^{2}(\Omega)h/(\varepsilon\bar{t})},\quad\mbox{for}\quad\varepsilon=\mathrm{dist}(z,\,\partial\Omega).

(E.62)

Here
\bar{t}=\min\{t,1-t\}
.

5.

If
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
and
\widetilde{z}\in\mathrm{Proj}_{\partial\Omega}(z)
, then

-\frac{\mathrm{dist}^{2}(z,\,\partial\Omega)}{1-t}~\leq~\inf_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)}\,\delta^{\top}(z-\widetilde{z})~<~\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)}\,\delta^{\top}(z-\widetilde{z})~\leq~\frac{\mathrm{dist}^{2}(z,\,\partial\Omega)}{t}.

6.

If
z\in\mathrm{SC}(\Omega)
, then
S_{t}(z)=\{0\}
for all
t\in(0,1)
.
In particular, if
\Omega
is a strictly convex set, then
S_{t}(z)=\{0\}
whenever
t\in(0,1)
and
z\in\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

By Proposition 4.2.1 of
4
, we have

\mbox{closure}(\mbox{cone}(\Omega-z))=T_{\Omega}(z).

In words, this says that the closure of the cone spanned by
\Omega-z
is the tangent cone.
This implies that

\frac{\Omega-z}{t}\subseteq T_{\Omega}(z)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\Omega-z}{1-t}\subseteq T_{\Omega}(z),\quad\mbox{for any}\quad t\in(0,1).

(E.63)

Moreover,

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\,\frac{\Omega-z}{t}~=~T_{\Omega}(z),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{t\uparrow 1}\,\frac{\Omega-z}{1-t}~=~T_{\Omega}(z).

(E.64)

1.

From (
E.63
), we get that
\delta\in L_{t}(z)
implies
\delta\in-T_{\Omega}(z)
and
\delta\in U_{t}(z)
implies
\delta\in T_{\Omega}(z)
, which proves the first statement. We now prove that for any
z\in\Omega
,

\sup_{y\in\Omega}\|z-y\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega).

(E.65)

Assuming (
E.65
), we get that

\displaystyle U_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega-z}{1-t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:max-distance-to-boundary}}{\subseteq}\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)}{1-t}\right)

\displaystyle{\subseteq}\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)}{t}\right),\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\leq\frac{\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)}{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\subseteq\frac{z-\Omega}{t}=L_{t}(z).

To prove (
E.65
), take any two points
z_{1},z_{2}\in\partial\Omega
such that
z=\lambda z_{1}+(1-\lambda)z_{2}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Then

\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|=\|z-z_{1}\|+\|z-z_{2}\|.

Take
z_{2}
so that
\|z-z_{2}\|=\sup_{y\in\Omega}\|z-y\|
; if there is no such point, consider a limiting sequence on the boundary. The corresponding
z_{1}
is a point on
\partial\Omega
and the definition of
\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)
implies
\|z-z_{1}\|\geq\mbox{dist}(z,\,\partial\Omega)
. Therefore, we get

\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|\geq\mbox{dist}(z,\,\partial\Omega)+\sup_{y\in\Omega}\|z-y\|.

The left hand side is bounded above by the diameter of
\Omega
by definition. Hence, we obtain (
E.65
). The second part of the result follows from the observation that

L_{t}(z)\subseteq\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)}{t}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad U_{t}(z)\subseteq\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)}{1-t}\right).

2.

It is easy to see that

L_{0}(z)=\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\mbox{and}\quad U_{1}(z)=\mathbb{R}^{d}.

As
t\downarrow 0
, note that
L_{t}(z)
increases to
-T_{\Omega}(z)
and
U_{t}(z)
decreases to
\Omega-z
. This is because of the convexity of
\Omega
:
z-t\delta\in\Omega
(along with
z\in\Omega
) implies that
z-s\delta\in\Omega
for all
s\in[0,t]
(or equivalently,
\delta\in(z-\Omega)/s
. (Similar reasoning also holds for
(\Omega-z)/(1-t)
). If
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
, then
-T_{\Omega}(z)=T_{\Omega}(z)=L_{0}(z)=U_{1}(z)=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

3.

Because
S_{t}(z)=L_{t}(z)\cap U_{t}(z)
, by non-negativity of
f
, we get

\displaystyle 0\leq f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in S_{t}(z)\}

\displaystyle=f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in L_{t}(z)\}\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in U_{t}(z)\}

\displaystyle\leq f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in L_{t}(z)\}

\displaystyle\leq f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in-T_{\Omega}(z)\}.

Hence,

0\leq f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in S_{t}(z)\}\leq f(t,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in-T_{\Omega}(z)\},

and each of these functions converges to
0
,
f(0,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap(\Omega-z)\}
, and
f(0,\delta)\mathbf{1}\{\delta\in-T_{\Omega}(z)\}
, as
t\to 0
. Therefore, by our assumption and Pratt’s
(
50
, Theorem 5.5)
lemma, we get the result.

The proof for convergence as
t\to 1
is identical.

4.

We prove the result for
t\leq 1/2
. Clearly,

\displaystyle S_{t}(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(S_{t}(z)\cap S_{t}(z^{\prime}))\cup(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z))

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{t}(z)\cup(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z)).

Hence, it suffices to prove that

S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z)~\subseteq~(S_{t}(z))^{2\gamma/(t(1-t))}.

(E.66)

Consider a vector
\delta^{\prime}\in S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z)
. To prove (
E.66
), it suffices to produce
\delta\in S_{t}(z)
such that
\|\delta^{\prime}-\delta\|\leq 2\gamma/(t(1-t))
. Because
\delta^{\prime}\in S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z)
, we must have either

\begin{split}&z^{\prime}-t\delta^{\prime}\in\Omega,\;z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta^{\prime}\in\Omega,\;z-t\delta^{\prime}\notin\Omega,\\
&z^{\prime}-t\delta^{\prime}\in\Omega,\;z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta^{\prime}\in\Omega,\;z+(1-t)\delta^{\prime}\notin\Omega.\end{split}

(E.67)

Let us consider the first case. Because
S_{t}(z)\neq\emptyset
(note that
0\in S_{t}(z)
for all
z\in\Omega
), the set

\Upsilon:=\Omega\cap\left(\frac{z-t\Omega}{1-t}\right),

is non-empty. In fact, for any
v\in S_{t}(z)
, we have
z-tv=\xi\in\Omega
and

z+(1-t)v=z+(1-t)(z-\xi)/t=(z-(1-t)\xi)/t\in\Omega\quad\Rightarrow\quad\xi\in\frac{z-t\Omega}{1-t}.

Therefore,
\xi\in\Upsilon
. Define

\vartheta=\mbox{Proj}_{\Upsilon}(z-t\delta^{\prime})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta=\frac{z-\vartheta}{t}\in S_{t}(z).

It suffices to prove that
\|\delta-\delta^{\prime}\|\leq 2\gamma/(t(1-t))
. Note that

\|\delta-\delta^{\prime}\|=\left\|\frac{z-t\delta}{t}-\frac{z-t\delta^{\prime}}{t}\right\|=\frac{1}{t}\left\|\vartheta-(z-t\delta^{\prime})\right\|=\frac{\mbox{dist}(z-t\delta^{\prime},\,\Upsilon)}{t}.

To control the right hand side, note that the distance to a set is a decreasing function of the set. Hence, if we can show

\Upsilon\supseteq\left(\Omega\cap\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)}\neq\emptyset,

(E.68)

then

\displaystyle\mbox{dist}(z-t\delta^{\prime},\,\Upsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{dist}\left(z-t\delta^{\prime},\,\left(\Omega\cap\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{dist}\left(z-t\delta^{\prime},\,\Omega\cap\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)+\frac{\|z-z^{\prime}\|}{(1-t)}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{dist}(z-t\delta^{\prime},z^{\prime}-t\delta^{\prime})+\frac{\|z-z^{\prime}\|}{1-t}=\frac{2-t}{1-t}\|z-z^{\prime}\|,

which implies the result. In this calculation, it is necessary to prove that the set in (
E.68
) is non-empty, because the distance of any point to an empty set is infinity.
To prove (
E.68
), note that

\mathcal{B}\left(z^{\prime},\,\frac{t\mbox{dist}(z^{\prime},\partial\Omega)}{1-t}\right)~\subseteq~\Omega\cap\left(\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right).

(By the definition of the distance, we get
\mathcal{B}(z^{\prime},\mbox{dist}(z^{\prime},\partial\Omega))\subseteq\Omega
and so, the first inclusion holds for
t\leq 1/2
. For the second one, observe that for any
\mathcal{B}(z^{\prime},\varepsilon)\subseteq\Omega
,
(z^{\prime}-t\Omega)/(1-t)\supseteq\mathcal{B}(z^{\prime},t\varepsilon/(1-t))
. Applying this with
\varepsilon=\mbox{dist}(z^{\prime},\partial\Omega)
yields this inclusion result.) This implies that

\left(\Omega\cap\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)}\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{whenever}\quad\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq t\mbox{dist}(z^{\prime},\partial\Omega).

Using the fact that

\frac{z-t\Omega}{1-t}\supseteq\left(\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)},

we conclude

\Upsilon\supseteq\Omega\cap\left(\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)}\supseteq\left(\Omega\cap\frac{z^{\prime}-t\Omega}{1-t}\right)^{-\|z-z^{\prime}\|/(1-t)}\neq\emptyset.

This proves (
E.68
). The proof when
z+(1-t)\delta^{\prime}\notin\Omega
is similar. To prove the lower inclusion in the result, swap the roles of
z,z^{\prime}
.

To prove (
E.62
), consider any
\delta\in S_{t+h}(z)\setminus S_{t}(z)
. By monotonicity of
t\mapsto L_{t}(z)
and
t\mapsto U_{t}(z)
, we get

\delta\in L_{t+h}(z)\cap L_{t}(z)\cap U_{t+h}(z)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\delta\notin U_{t}(z).

By Lemma 1 of
60
, we get

\displaystyle\mbox{dist}(\delta,\,L_{t}(z)\cap U_{t}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mbox{diam}(L_{t}(z))}{\sup_{y\in S_{t}(z)}\mbox{dist}(y,\partial L_{t}(z))}\mbox{dist}(\delta,\,U_{t}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{t\mbox{dist}(0,\partial L_{t}(z))}\left\|\delta-\left(\frac{1-t-h}{1-t}\right)\delta\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)}{(1-t)\varepsilon}h.

The second inequality follows from the fact that
\delta\in S_{t+h}(z)\subseteq U_{t+h}(z)
implies that
\delta\in(\Omega-z)/(1-t-h)
or equivalently,
(1-t-h)\delta/(1-t)\in(\Omega-z)/(1-t)=U_{t}(z)
.
This proves the second inclusion of (
E.62
).

For the first inclusion of (
E.62
), consider any
\delta\in S_{t}(z)
such that

\mathcal{B}\left(\delta,\,\frac{2\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)h}{\varepsilon\bar{t}}\right)\subseteq S_{t}(z).

To prove the first inclusion, it suffices to show that
\delta\in S_{t+h}(z)
. Because
\delta\in S_{t}(z)=L_{t}(z)\cap U_{t}(z)
, we get
\delta\in U_{t+h}(z)
. Consider the vector
(1+h/t)\delta
. Clearly,

\left\|\delta-(1+h/t)\delta\right\|\leq\frac{h}{t}\|\delta\|\leq\frac{h}{\bar{t}}(2\mbox{diam}(\Omega))\leq\frac{2h\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)h}{\varepsilon\bar{t}}.

This implies
(1+h/t)\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\,2\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)h/(\varepsilon\bar{t}))\subseteq S_{t}(z)
. Therefore,

(1+h/t)\delta\subseteq L_{t}(z)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta\in L_{t+h}(z).

Hence,
\delta\in S_{t+h}(z)
.

5.

For
z\in\Omega
,
\delta\in S_{t}(z)
implies that
z-t\delta,z+(1-t)\delta\in\Omega
. Hence, from Lemma
13
, we get

\displaystyle(z-\widetilde{z})^{\top}(z-t\delta-\widetilde{z})\geq 0,\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\|z-\widetilde{z}\|^{2}\geq t\delta^{\top}(z-\widetilde{z}),

\displaystyle(z-\widetilde{z})^{\top}(z+(1-t)\delta-\widetilde{z})\geq 0,\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\|z-\widetilde{z}\|^{2}\geq-(1-t)\delta^{\top}(z-\widetilde{z})^{2}.

This implies the result. The strict inequality between the infimum and the supremum follows from the fact that
\delta\in S_{t}(z)
implies
-t\delta/(1-t)\in S_{t}(z)
(for
t<1/2
), and hence, the infimum is negative and the supremum is positive.

6.

Because
z\in\partial\Omega
and
\Omega
is a closed convex set, there exists a supporting hyperplane, i.e., there exists a vector
n\neq 0
such that
(y-z)^{\top}n\leq 0
for all
y\in\Omega
. Strict convexity of
\Omega
at
z
implies that
(y-z)^{\top}n<0
for all
y\in\Omega\setminus\{z\}
. (A proof is as follows: suppose there exists
z^{\prime}\in\Omega,z^{\prime}\neq z
and
(z^{\prime}-z)^{\top}n=0
. Then by strict convexity at
z
,
(z^{\prime}+z)/2\in\Omega^{\circ}
and hence,
(z^{\prime}+z)/2+\varepsilon u\in\Omega
for all
u\in S^{d-1}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough. This implies that

((z^{\prime}+z)/2+\varepsilon u-z)^{\top}n=\varepsilon u^{\top}n\leq 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad u\in S^{d-1},

which is a contradiction.)

To prove that
S_{t}(z)=\{0\}
, we note that
S_{t}(z)\subseteq(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap U_{t}(z)
for all
t\in(0,1)
. It is clear that
\{0\}\subset(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap U_{t}(z)
. Suppose, if possible, there exists
v\neq 0
such that
-v\in T_{\Omega}(z)
and
v\in U_{t}(z)
. Formally, this means that there exists
\tau_{k}\downarrow 0
and
z_{k}\in\Omega
such that
z_{k}\to z
and
(z_{k}-z)/\tau_{k}\to-v
, and
z+(1-t)v\in\Omega
. From the supporting hyperplane and that
v\neq 0
, we get

(z_{k}-z)^{\top}n<0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad k\geq 1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left(\frac{z_{k}-z}{\tau_{k}}\right)^{\top}n<0\quad\Rightarrow\quad-v^{\top}n\leq 0.

On the other hand, we have

(z+(1-t)v-z)^{\top}n<0\quad\Rightarrow\quad v^{\top}n<0,

which is a contradiction. One can also prove this result using the modulus of strict convexity; see, for example, the proof of Proposition
6
.

∎

Proposition 11

(Examples of
\Omega
and contingent cones)
.

1.

If
\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{i}^{\top}x\leq b_{i}\mbox{ for }1\leq i\leq m\}
, then setting

I(z)=\{j\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}|a_{j}^{\top}z=b_{j}\}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\partial\Omega,

we have

\displaystyle T_{\Omega}(z)

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{j}^{\top}w\leq 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j\in I(z)\},

\displaystyle(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap(\Omega-z)

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{j}^{\top}w=0\;\mbox{for}\;j\in I(z),\;a_{j}^{\top}w\leq b_{j}-a_{j}^{\top}z\;\mbox{for}\;j\notin I(z)\}.

2.

Suppose
\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,c_{i}(x)=0\;\mbox{for}\;i\in\mathcal{E},c_{i}(x)\leq 0\;\mbox{for}\;i\in\mathcal{I}\}
4
4

4

For this part, we do not need
c_{i}
’s to be convex.
for some finite sets
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{I}
. Set

I(z)=\{i\in\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{I}:\,c_{i}(z)=0\}.

If
\{\nabla c_{i}(z):\,i\in I(z)\}
are linearly independent, then

\displaystyle T_{\Omega}(z)

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,w^{\top}\nabla c_{i}(z)=0\;\mbox{for}\;i\in\mathcal{E}\;\mbox{and}\;w^{\top}\nabla c_{i}(z)\leq 0\;\mbox{for}\;i\in\mathcal{I}\cap I(z)\},

\displaystyle(-T_{\Omega}(z))\cap(\Omega-z)

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,w^{\top}\nabla c_{i}(z)=0,\,c_{i}(z+w)=0,\;\mbox{for}\;i\in\mathcal{E}\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,c_{i}(z+w)\leq 0\mbox{ for }i\in\mathcal{I},\;w^{\top}\nabla c_{i}(z)\geq 0\mbox{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}\cap I(z)\}.

Proof.

1.

The proof is straightforward. Let
\omega\in T_{\Omega}(z)
. Then by definition, there exists
\tau_{k}\downarrow 0,z_{k}\in\Omega
such that
z_{k}\to z
and
(z_{k}-z)/\tau_{k}\to\omega
. Because
z_{k}\in\Omega
,

a_{i}^{\top}z_{k}\leq b_{i}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i\in I(z)\quad\Rightarrow\quad a_{i}^{\top}(z_{k}-z)/\tau_{k}\leq 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i\in I(z).

Taking the limit as
k\to\infty
, we get
T_{\Omega}(z)\in\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{j}^{\top}w\leq 0\;\mbox{for}\;j\in I(z)\}
. To prove the converse, set

\varepsilon=\inf_{j\notin I(z)}(b_{j}-a_{j}^{\top}z).

By definition,
\varepsilon>0
(because
m<\infty
). Therefore,
z_{k}=z+\tau_{k}w
for any
w
satisfying
a_{j}^{\top}w\leq 0
for
j\in I(z)
satisfies

a_{j}^{\top}z_{k}=a_{j}^{\top}z+\tau_{k}a_{j}^{\top}w\begin{cases}\leq b_{j}+0,&\mbox{if }j\in I(z),\\
\leq b_{j}+\varepsilon+\tau_{k}a_{j}^{\top}w,&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence, as
\tau_{k}\to 0
,
\varepsilon+\tau_{k}a_{j}^{\top}w>0
and hence,
z_{k}\in\Omega
for
k
large enough. Recalling that

\displaystyle\Omega-z

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{j}^{\top}w\leq b_{j}-a_{j}^{\top}z\mbox{ for }1\leq j\leq m\}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{j}^{\top}w\leq 0\mbox{ for }j\in I(z),\;a_{j}^{\top}w\leq b_{j}-a_{j}^{\top}z\mbox{ for }j\notin I(z)\},

we obtain the result.

2.

Follows from Lemma 12.2 of
83
. See Proposition 4.3.7 of
4
for an alternative set of assumptions.

∎

Lemma 37
.

Suppose assumptions
(B1)
,
(B2)
, and
(B3)
hold. Then the integral equation (
42
) has a unique solution
z^{*}:[0,1]\to\Omega
with
z^{*}(t)\in\Omega^{\circ}
for all
t\in[0,1)
, whenever
x\in\Omega^{\circ}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9
, every solution
y(\cdot)
belongs to the interior on
[0,1-\delta]
, for any
\delta>0
. Furthermore, Lemma
8
along with assumption
(B3)
implies that assumption
(W1)
holds true. Hence, from Theorem
4
, it follows that there exists a unique solution
z^{*}:[0,1-\delta]\to\Omega^{\circ}
for any
\delta>0
. To prove the existence of a unique solution in
[0,1]
, consider any sequence
\{\delta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset[0,1]
such that
\delta_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. By the uniqueness on
[0,1-\delta]
for any
\delta>0
, we get a unique
z^{*}(1-\delta_{k})\in\Omega
such that
z^{*}(1-\delta_{k})=x+\int_{0}^{1-\delta_{k}}v(s,z^{*}(s))ds
. The boundedness of
v(\cdot,\cdot)
implies
\{z^{*}(1-\delta_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence and hence, is convergent. Because
\Omega
is compact (from assumption
(B1)
), the limit
z^{*}(1)
of
z^{*}(1-\delta_{k})
belongs to
\Omega
and must satisfy
z^{*}(1)=x+\int_{0}^{1}v(s,z^{*}(s))ds
.
∎

S.6.1
Proof of Lemma
8

Recall that
v(t,z)=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}|tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}=z]
. Hence, by Jensen’s inequality,
\|v(t,z)\|\leq\mathbb{E}[\|X_{1}-X_{0}\||tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}=z]
. Because
X_{0},X_{1}\in\Omega
, we have
\|X_{1}-X_{0}\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
almost surely. Therefore,
\|v(t,z)\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
for all
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
.

Fix
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
with
\|z-z^{\prime}\|=\eta
. Observe that

\displaystyle v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{t}(z)}{p_{t}(z)}-\frac{f_{t}(z^{\prime})}{p_{t}(z^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{t}(z)-f_{t}(z^{\prime})}{p_{t}(z)}-v(t,z^{\prime})\frac{p_{t}(z)-p_{t}(z^{\prime})}{p_{t}(z)}.

Note that

p_{t}(z)\geq\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}.

For any function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, consider

T_{h}(z)=\int_{S_{t}(z)}h(\delta)p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta.

Then

\displaystyle|T_{h}(z)-T_{h}(z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{S_{t}(z)\cap S_{t}(z^{\prime})}h(\delta)\left(\frac{p_{0}(z^{\prime}-t\delta)p_{1}(z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta)}{p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)}-1\right)p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{S_{t}(z)\setminus S_{t}(z^{\prime})}h(\delta)p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z)}h(\delta)p_{0}(z^{\prime}-t\delta)p_{1}(z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta)d\delta\right|

\displaystyle\leq p_{t}(z)\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)\cap S_{t}(z^{\prime})}|h(\delta)|\left|\frac{p_{0}(z^{\prime}-t\delta)p_{1}(z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta)}{p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)}-1\right|

\displaystyle\quad+p_{t}(z)\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)}|h(\delta)|p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\setminus S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}

\displaystyle\quad+p_{t}(z)\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z^{\prime})}|h(\delta)|p_{0}(z^{\prime}-t\delta)p_{1}(z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta)\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\setminus S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}.

From assumption
(B2)
(and (
40
), (
41
)), we get

\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)\cap S_{t}(z^{\prime})}\left|\frac{p_{0}(z^{\prime}-t\delta)p_{1}(z^{\prime}+(1-t)\delta)}{p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)}-1\right|\leq\left(1+\omega(\eta)\right)^{2}-1\leq 2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta),

and

\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)}p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)\leq\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}.

Therefore,

\frac{|T_{h}(z)-T_{h}(z^{\prime})|}{p_{t}(z)}\leq\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)\cup S_{t}(z^{\prime})}|h(\delta)|\left[2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta)+\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\right],

(E.69)

where
A\Delta B
represents the symmetric difference of
A
and
B
.
Taking
h(\delta)=e_{j}^{\top}\delta
and
h(\delta)\equiv v(t,z^{\prime})
, we get

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq 3\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\left[2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta)+\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\right],

(E.70)

because

\sup_{\delta\in S_{t}(z)}\|\delta\|\leq\frac{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\varepsilon}{\max\{t,1-t\}}\leq 2(\mbox{diam}(\Omega)-\varepsilon),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sup_{z\in\Omega^{\circ}}\|v(t,z)\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega).

The term
2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta)
can be simplified as follows. If
\eta\leq\omega^{-1}(1)
, then

2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta)\leq 3\omega(\eta).

If
\eta>\omega^{-1}(1)
, then using (
43
)

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq 2\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\eta/\omega^{-1}(1).

Therefore, in (
E.70
), we can replace
2\omega(\eta)+\omega^{2}(\eta)
with
3\omega(\eta)+\eta/\omega^{-1}(1)
. This yields

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq 3\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\left[3\omega(\eta)+\frac{\eta}{\omega^{-1}(1)}+\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\right].

(E.71)

It suffices to now prove an upper bound on
\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))/\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))
.

We shall first prove that bound under the simpler setting of
\min\{t,1-t\}\leq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
.
For
t\leq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
, Proposition
10
(1) implies that

S_{t}(z)=\frac{\Omega-z}{1-t}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{t}(z^{\prime})=\frac{\Omega-z^{\prime}}{1-t}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mbox{Vol}((\Omega-z)\Delta(\Omega-z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega-z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mbox{Vol}((\Omega-z+z-z^{\prime})\Delta(\Omega-z))}{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega-z)}.

Then Theorem 3 of
89
yields

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\leq\eta\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial(\Omega-z))}{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega-z)}.

(Here on the right hand side, we could have written
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial\Omega)
and
\mbox{Vol}(\Omega)
, but notationally, it would be easier to apply Lemma 2.1 of
44
in the way presented.) Because
\Omega-z\supseteq\mathcal{B}(0,\varepsilon)
, Lemma 2.1 of
44
yields

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\leq\frac{d\eta}{\varepsilon}.

(E.72)

Similarly, if
t\geq 1-\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
, we get

S_{t}(z)=\frac{z-\Omega}{t}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{t}(z^{\prime})=\frac{z^{\prime}-\Omega}{t}.

Therefore,

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\Delta S_{t}(z^{\prime}))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\leq\frac{d\eta}{\varepsilon}.

(E.73)

Hence, for
\min\{t,1-t\}\leq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
, the result is proved because

\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{d\eta}{\varepsilon}~\leq~\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\frac{d\eta}{\varepsilon}\times\frac{5^{d+1}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\varepsilon\in[0,\mbox{diam}(\Omega)]\quad\mbox{and}\quad d\geq 1.

To prove the result when
\min\{t,1-t\}\geq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega)
, we can assume that
\eta\leq\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}/2
.
Otherwise, using the boundedness of
v(t,z)
(i.e., inequality (
43
)) and that
{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}/{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}\geq 1
, we conclude that

\begin{split}\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|&\leq 2\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\\
&\leq 2\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\frac{2\eta}{\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}}\\
&=\left(\frac{4\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}}\right)\eta\\
&\leq\frac{4\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\eta\\
&\leq 3\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)\frac{\eta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}}d5^{d+1},\end{split}

(E.74)

which proves the result. Hence, it suffices to prove the validity of the result under

\eta\leq\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}/2,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\min\{t,1-t\}\geq\varepsilon/\mbox{diam}(\Omega).

(E.75)

From Proposition
10
(4), it follows that, under (
E.75
),

S_{t}(z)\cup S_{t}(z^{\prime})\subseteq(S_{t}(z))^{2\eta/(t(1-t))},\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{t}(z^{\prime})\supseteq(S_{t}(z))^{-2\eta/(t(1-t))}.

These relations imply that

\displaystyle\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\setminus S_{t}(z))

\displaystyle=\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\cup S_{t}(z))-\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{Vol}((S_{t}(z))^{2\eta/(t(1-t))})-\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)),

\displaystyle\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\setminus S_{t}(z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))-\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z)\cap S_{t}(z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))-\mbox{Vol}((S_{t}(z))^{-2\eta/(t(1-t))}).

Therefore,

\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\Delta S_{t}(z))\leq\mbox{Vol}(A^{\gamma}\setminus A)\quad\mbox{where}\quad A=(S_{t}(z))^{-2\eta/(t(1-t))}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\gamma=\frac{4\eta}{t(1-t)}.

(This follows from Eq. (3.15) of
88
.)
Because
z\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
, we have (under (
E.75
))

\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\varepsilon}{\max\{t,1-t\}}\right)\subseteq S_{t}(z)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{B}\left(0,\,\frac{\varepsilon}{2\max\{t,1-t\}}\right)\subseteq A.

This implies that
A
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
14
(with
r=\varepsilon/(2\max\{t,1-t\})
) and hence,

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\Delta S_{t}(z))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\leq\frac{\mbox{Vol}(A)}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}\frac{8d\eta\max\{t,1-t\}}{t(1-t)\varepsilon}\left(1+\frac{8\eta\max\{t,1-t\}}{t(1-t)\varepsilon}\right)^{d-1}.

Because
A\subseteq S_{t}(z)
, we conclude (under (
E.75
)) that

\begin{split}\frac{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z^{\prime})\Delta S_{t}(z))}{\mbox{Vol}(S_{t}(z))}&\leq\eta\times\frac{8d}{\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}}\left(1+\frac{4\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}\max\{t,1-t\}}{t(1-t)\varepsilon}\right)^{d-1}\\
&\leq\eta\times\frac{d5^{d+1}}{\varepsilon\min\{t,1-t\}}\leq\eta\times\frac{d5^{d+1}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.\end{split}

(E.76)

Substituting in (
E.71
), we obtain

\|v(t,z)-v(t,z^{\prime})\|\leq 3\mbox{diam}(\Omega)\left[{3\omega(\eta)}+\frac{\eta}{\omega^{-1}(1)}+\eta\times\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)d5^{d+1}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\right].

(E.77)

Combining inequalities (
E.70
), (
E.77
), and (
E.72
), (
E.73
), we obtain the result.

S.6.2
Proof of Lemma
9

The existence of a solution follows from Theorem
3
(1) with
\mathcal{S}=\Omega
and the boundedness of
v(\cdot,\cdot)
as proved in (
43
).

To prove a differential inequality for the distance to the boundary of
\Omega
. Set

\mathfrak{D}(t)=\mbox{dist}(y(t),\partial\Omega).

Suppose, if possible, that
T<1
.
By definition of
T
,
y(t)\in\Omega^{\circ}
for
t\in[0,T)
and hence,
\mathfrak{D}(t)>0
for all
t\in[0,T)
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{D}(T)=0
(i.e.,
y(T)\in\partial\Omega
), because otherwise an application of Theorem
3
(1) for the ODE starting at time
T
and the initial value
y(T)
would have a solution that lies in
\Omega^{\circ}
on
[T,T+\eta]
(for some small
\eta>0
). This contradicts the definition of
T
.

We shall now prove that
\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
is almost everywhere differentiable on
[0,T]
and that

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(t)\geq-\frac{1}{1-t}\mathfrak{D}(t)\quad\mbox{almost everywhere}\quad t\in[0,T).

(E.78)

Assuming (
E.78
) holds, we get that

\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(t)}{\mathfrak{D}(t)}dt\geq-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{(1-t)}dt\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\log\left(\frac{\mathfrak{D}(T)}{\mathfrak{D}(0)}\right)\geq\log(1-T)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{D}(T)\geq(1-T)\mathfrak{D}(0).

This implies that
\mathfrak{D}(T)>0
if
T<1
, contradicting the definition of
T
. Hence,
T=1
. Inequality (
48
) follows from this calculation.

To prove (
E.78
), note that for
t,s\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{D}(t)-\mathfrak{D}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mbox{dist}(y(t),\partial\Omega)-\mbox{dist}(y(s),\partial\Omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\|y(t)-y(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mbox{diam}(\Omega)|t-s|,

where the last inequality follows from (
43
). This implies that
t\mapsto\mathfrak{D}(t)
is absolutely continuous on
[0,T]
, and hence, almost everywhere differentiable. Since
y(\cdot)
is a solution to the integral equation (
47
), it is also absolutely continuous on
[0,T]
. Hence, there exists a zero measure set
\mathcal{N}\subset[0,1]
such that
\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
and
y(\cdot)
are both differentiable for all
s\in[0,1]\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

We shall prove the inequality (
E.78
) for any
t\in[0,T)\setminus\mathcal{N}
.
For any
s\in[0,1]
, let
\widetilde{y}(s)
be any projection of
y(s)
on to
\partial\Omega
:

\widetilde{y}(s)\in\mbox{Proj}_{\partial\Omega}(y(s)).

(Pick any element in the projection. Our bound below does not depend on this choice.)
By Lipschitz continuity of
\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
,
\mathfrak{D}(t)>0
for
t<T
implies the existence of
\bar{\eta}>0
such that
\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)>0
for all
\eta\in[0,\bar{\eta}]
.
Fix any
\eta\in[0,\bar{\eta}]
.
It is clear that

\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)=\|y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)\|,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathfrak{D}(t)\leq\|y(t)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)\|.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)-\mathfrak{D}(t)}{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left(\frac{\|y(t)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)\|^{2}}{\|y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)\|^{2}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=1-\left(1+\frac{\|y(t)-y(t+\eta)\|^{2}+2(y(t)-y(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))}{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}\right)^{1/2}.

Using the inequality
\sqrt{1+x}\leq 1+x/2
for all
x\geq-1
, we get

\frac{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)-\mathfrak{D}(t)}{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}\geq-\frac{\|y(t)-y(t+\eta)\|^{2}}{2\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}-\frac{(y(t)-y(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))}{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}.

(E.79)

Because
y(\cdot)
solves the integral equation (
42
), inequality (
43
) implies

\|y(t)-y(t+\eta)\|^{2}\leq 4\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)\eta^{2},

(E.80)

Furthermore, because
t\in[0,1]\setminus\mathcal{N}
, we get

\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\,\frac{\|y(t+\eta)-y(t)-\eta v(t,y(t))\|}{\eta}\to 0.

(E.81)

Hence, inequality (
E.79
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)-\mathfrak{D}(t)}{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{4\eta^{2}\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)}{2\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(y(t+\eta)-y(t)-\eta v(t,y(t)))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))}{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\eta\frac{v(t,y(t))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))}{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}.

To control the third term, note that

v(t,y(t))=\int_{S_{t}(y(t))}\delta q(\delta;t,y(t))dt,\quad\mbox{where}\quad q(\delta;t,z):=\frac{p_{0}(z-t\delta)p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta)}{\int_{S_{t}(z)}p_{0}(z-t\delta^{\prime})p_{1}(z+(1-t)\delta^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}}.

Because
y(t)\in\Omega^{\circ},

q(\cdot;t,y(t))
is a valid probability density and, hence,

v(t,y(t))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))~\geq~\inf_{\delta\in S_{t}(y(t))}\,\delta^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)).

Because
\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)\in\mbox{Proj}_{\partial\Omega}(y(t+\eta))
, Lemma
13
implies

(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))^{\top}(z-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))\geq 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\Omega.

Because
\delta\in S_{t}(y(t))
implies
y(t)+(1-t)\delta\in\Omega
, we conclude

(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t)+(1-t)\delta-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))\geq 0.

Equivalently, for any
\delta\in S_{t}(y(t))
,

\displaystyle\delta^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{1-t}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1-t}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{1-t}(y(t+\eta)-\widetilde{y}(t+\eta))^{\top}(y(t)-y(t+\eta))

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}{1-t}-\frac{1}{1-t}\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)\|y(t)-y(t+\eta)\|

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}{1-t}-\frac{2\eta}{1-t}\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)\mbox{diam}(\Omega),

where the last inequality follows from (
E.80
).
Combining all the inequalities, we get that for all
\eta\in[0,\bar{\eta}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)-\mathfrak{D}(\eta)}{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{4\eta^{2}\mbox{diam}^{2}(\Omega)}{\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}-\frac{\|y(t+\eta)-y(t)-\eta v(t,y(t))\|}{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\eta\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}{(1-t)\mathfrak{D}^{2}(t+\eta)}-\frac{2\eta^{2}\mbox{diam}(\Omega)}{(1-t)\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}.

Note that this inequality does not depend on the choice of projection
\widetilde{y}(t+\eta)
. Now, dividing both sides by
\eta
and letting
\eta\downarrow 0
implies (using (
E.81
))

\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\,\frac{\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)-\mathfrak{D}(\eta)}{\eta\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)}\geq-\frac{1}{1-t}.

Furthermore, the continuity of
\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
implies
\mathfrak{D}(t+\eta)/\mathfrak{D}(t)\to 1
as
\eta\to 0
and therefore we get (
E.78
).

S.6.3
Proof of Lemma
10

Because
\mu_{0}
has a Lebesgue density and
\Omega
is a convex body implies
\mu_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{1})=1
. From Theorem
1
, we get
\mathfrak{R}(1,X)\sim\mu_{1}
whenever
X\sim\mu_{0}
and because
\mu_{1}
also has a Lebesgue density, we conclude
\mu_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{2})=1
. Finally, if
\omega(\eta)\leq C\eta
, then Lemma
8
implies that for any
t\in[0,1]
,
z\mapsto v(t,z)
is differentiable almost everywhere
z\in\Omega
. (This follows because for any
z\in\Omega^{\circ}
,
\mbox{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)>0
and for all
h
with small enough Euclidean norm,
z+h\in\Omega^{\circ}
. This yields
z,z+h\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
and hence,
z\mapsto v(t,z)
is locally Lipschitz on
\Omega^{\circ}
. Rademacher theorem, now, implies almost everywhere differentiability.) By Theorem
1
,
\mathfrak{R}(s,X)\overset{d}{=}(1-s)X_{0}+sX_{1}
and hence, is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Therefore,
\mu_{0}(B(t))=0
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Observe now that, by Fubini’s theorem,

(\mbox{Leb}\times\mu_{0})(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c})=\int_{0}^{1}\mu_{0}(B(t))dt=0.

On the other hand, note that

0=(\mbox{Leb}\times\mu_{0})(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c})=\int_{\Omega}\mbox{Leb}(\{t\in[0,1]:\,\mathfrak{R}(t,z)\in B(t)\})\mu_{0}(dz).

Because the integrand is non-negative, it must be zero almost everywhere, which implies that
\mu_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{4})=0
.

To prove the bounds on distance to boundary, note from (
49
) (and (
43
)) that

\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t^{\prime},x)\|\leq\mbox{diam}(\Omega)|t-t^{\prime}|\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1].

Hence, Lipschitzness of the distance to a compact set implies that

\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(t,x),\partial\Omega)\geq\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\partial\Omega)-\mbox{diam}(\Omega)(1-t).

Additionally, from Lemma
9
, we know

\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(t,x),\partial\Omega)\geq(1-t)\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(0,x),\,\partial\Omega).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,1]}\,\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(t,x),\partial\Omega)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\inf_{t\in[0,1]}\max\left\{\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\partial\Omega)-\mbox{diam}(\Omega)(1-t),\,(1-t)\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(0,x),\,\partial\Omega)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,x),\,\partial\Omega)\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(0,x),\partial\Omega)}{\mbox{diam}(\Omega)+\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(0,x),\,\partial\Omega)}.

This proves (
53
).

From Theorem
8
, we know that (
42
) has a unique solution, which by Theorem
1
implies that
\mathfrak{R}(1,X)\sim\mu_{1}
whenever
X\sim\mu_{0}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X)\in\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X)\in\Omega\setminus\Omega^{-\gamma})}{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X)\in\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}}\frac{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega\setminus\Omega^{-\gamma})}{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega)}.

Because
\Omega\supseteq\mathcal{B}(z^{*},r_{\mathrm{in}})
, we get
\Omega^{-\gamma}\supseteq\mathcal{B}(z^{*},r_{\mathrm{in}}-\gamma)
and hence, Lemma
14
yields

\frac{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega\setminus\Omega^{-\gamma})}{\mbox{Vol}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{d\gamma}{(r_{\mathrm{in}}-\gamma)_{+}}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{(r_{\mathrm{in}}-\gamma)_{+}}\right)^{d-1}.

If
\gamma\leq r_{\mathrm{in}}/2
, then
\max\{\gamma,r_{\mathrm{in}}/2\}\leq r_{\mathrm{in}}-\gamma
and hence,

\mathbb{P}(\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma)\leq\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}}\frac{2^{d}d\gamma}{r_{\mathrm{in}}}.

On the other hand, if
\gamma>r_{\mathrm{in}}/2
, then

\mathbb{P}(\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma)\leq 1\leq\frac{2\gamma}{r_{\mathrm{in}}}.

Hence, for all
\gamma>0
,

\mathbb{P}(\mbox{dist}(\mathfrak{R}(1,X),\,\partial\Omega)\leq\gamma)\leq\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}}\frac{2^{d}d\gamma}{r_{\mathrm{in}}}.

For all
x\in\mathcal{A}_{\gamma}^{c}\cap\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
, inequality (
53
) implies the result.

S.6.4
Proof of Theorem
9

Fix any
\varepsilon>0
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega^{-\varepsilon}
with
\|x-x^{\prime}\|=\eta
. From Theorem
8
, we know that
\mathfrak{R}(t,x)
and
\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})
are uniquely defined. From (
42
), we know that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(t,x)

\displaystyle=x+\int_{0}^{t}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))ds,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime}))ds.

This implies that

\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|\leq\|x-x^{\prime}\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))-v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime}))\|ds.

(E.82)

From Lemma
9
, we know that

\mathfrak{R}(s,x),\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime})\in\Omega^{-(1-s)\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad s\in[0,1].

This implies (using (
44
)) that

\|v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x))-v(s,\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime}))\|\leq\mathfrak{L}_{1}\omega(\|\mathfrak{R}(s,x)-\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime})\|)+\mathfrak{L}_{2}((1-s)\varepsilon;s)\|\mathfrak{R}(s,x)-\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime})\|.

Therefore, substituting in (
E.82
), we conclude

\begin{split}\|\mathfrak{R}(t,x)-\mathfrak{R}(t,x^{\prime})\|&\leq\|x-x^{\prime}\|+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\omega(\|\mathfrak{R}(s,x)-\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime})\|)ds\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{L}_{2}((1-s)\varepsilon;s)\|\mathfrak{R}(s,x)-\mathfrak{R}(s,x^{\prime})\|ds.\end{split}

(E.83)

Set

\displaystyle V(t)
